THE FIRST CITY — C. R. ASHEN
ElevenLabs performance script (eleven_v3 audio tags)

CHAPTER 1 — THE SEALED TOMB

Narrator: CHAPTER 1
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Sealed Tomb
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The desert spat secrets when it felt like it. Today it gave up a ridge of brick half-buried in sand. A windstorm had stripped the dunes down to hard earth and left the upper edge exposed. Zeek crouched at the dig and brushed grit from the mortar with his fingers. The bricks were ordinary baked clay, worn down by time. The mortar wasn't. In places it had turned glassy and black, the kind of change that only came from heat no ordinary fire could make. The joints sat too clean. Too exact. His father's voice returned with the clarity of something said the day before.
Bel-iddin: [warmly] Read the mortar, not the bricks.
Narrator: He was twelve again, kneeling beside a collapsed wall while the old man scraped dust from a joint with the tip of a bronze knife.
Bel-iddin: [dryly] Bricks lie. Men shape them to look holy or expensive. Nobody bothers faking the mortar.
Narrator: Zeek never forgot that.
Balag: [dryly] Looks fresh,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Narrator: The big man stood with his spear against one shoulder, wide enough to block a good portion of the desert behind him. Sand had worked into his beard and the folds of his clothes, but he carried himself like the desert was the one that had been inconvenienced. He had the particular stillness of a man who had already decided the ground was wrong and was simply waiting for the rest of them to notice.
Zik: [dryly] Fresh enough to have been built before your grandfather drew breath,
Narrator: Zeek answered.
Balag: [deadpan] That isn't what fresh means.
Zik: [dryly] It is when you're staring at a ruin.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's mouth tightened, the expression of someone who had considered several replies and discarded them as not worth the air. A few paces off, En-KHEH-doo turned a broken seal over in her hands, knife tucked under one arm. She worked the edges with her thumb the way other people worried a coin, testing for value even in the broken pieces.
Enhedu: [teasing] You two bicker like a married pair.
Zik: [dryly] You're welcome to take him,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Enhedu: [amused] Not a chance.
Balag: [mutters] See?
Narrator: BAH-lahg muttered.
Balag: [mutters] She has sense.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's smile thinned.
Enhedu: [playfully] I said no to him. I didn't say no to you.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh laughed from the back, too loud and too fast. Three weeks with the group. Long enough to learn the habits. Not nearly long enough to understand what any of it cost. He stared at the exposed brickwork with the kind of hunger Zeek recognized. He had worn that same look once. KOO-rahsh crouched beside the exposed bricks and brushed away sand with exaggerated care, as though copying the motions he'd watched Zeek make all morning.
Kurash: [eager] So what do you see?
Narrator: he asked.
Narrator: Zeek didn't look up.
Zik: [deadpan] I see you smearing dirt back into the joint I just cleaned.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh frowned, rubbed harder, and managed only to spread the grit farther.
Kurash: [defensive] I meant the wall.
Zik: [dryly] I know what you meant.
Narrator: BAH-lahg folded his arms.
Balag: [amused] This should be good.
Narrator: Zeek hooked the tip of his knife into the mortar and lifted out a flake no larger than his thumbnail.
Zik: The bricks are ordinary.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh blinked.
Kurash: [disappointed] That's it?
Zik: No.
Narrator: Zeek turned the fragment over between his fingers. Dust rasped between the fragment and the pad of his thumb.
Zik: [knowingly] They're ordinary bricks laid by extraordinary masons.
Narrator: He handed the piece over.
Zik: Feel it.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh pinched it between his fingers. KOO-rahsh tested the edge harder, then frowned when nothing broke away.
Kurash: [puzzled] It feels like…rock.
Zik: It should crumble.
Kurash: [surprised] It doesn't.
Zik: [satisfied] Exactly.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo wandered over, abandoning the broken seal she'd been studying.
Enhedu: [curious] The mortar's been fired.
Zik: Not fired.
Narrator: Zeek shook his head.
Zik: Changed.
Narrator: He pointed to the black sheen running through the joint.
Zik: [intrigued] Clay melts if you get it hot enough. Not campfire hot. Kiln hot. Hot enough that whoever built this either knew something we've forgotten…
Narrator: His finger traced the dark glass.
Zik: [darkly] …or they were trying to seal something badly enough that they invented a way.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh looked from the wall to the ridge disappearing beneath the sand.
Kurash: [curious] So this isn't a temple.
Zik: No.
Kurash: [eagerly] A palace?
Zik: No.
Kurash: [impatient] Then what is it?
Narrator: Zeek smiled faintly.
Zik: [amused] If I already knew, we'd be digging somewhere else.
Narrator: BAH-lahg grunted.
Balag: [grunts] That's the first honest thing you've said today.
Zik: [dryly] I've been honest all day.
Balag: [deadpan] You've been specific. That's different.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo laughed.
Enhedu: [laughs] I'll remember that.
Narrator: She leaned close to the exposed masonry, eyes narrowing.
Enhedu: [curious] These upper bricks don't match.
Zik: They're replacements,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Enhedu: [skeptical] You can tell that from this?
Zik: I can tell from that.
Narrator: He pointed farther down the wall where the courses disappeared beneath packed sand.
Zik: The lower bricks settled decades before the upper ones were laid.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh stared.
Kurash: They all look the same.
Zik: They're supposed to.
Narrator: Zeek brushed away another thin layer of dust.
Zik: The mason who repaired it copied the original dimensions.
Kurash: [impressed] So he fooled everyone.
Zik: [dryly] He fooled almost everyone.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked sideways at him. The sideways look carried more amusement than surprise.
Enhedu: [amused] And you noticed because…
Zik: The mortar shrank differently.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh groaned.
Kurash: [groans] That's impossible.
Zik: It isn't impossible.
Kurash: [whining] It's unfair.
Narrator: BAH-lahg barked a laugh. The laugh struck the stone and came back larger.
Balag: [laughs] I've been saying that for years.
Narrator: Zeek tucked the knife back into his belt.
Zik: [thoughtful] My father used to say every wall lies.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh frowned.
Kurash: [skeptical] Walls lie?
Zik: [quietly] People build walls to be believed.
Narrator: He rested a hand on the ancient brickwork. Heat stored in the brick pressed steadily into his palm.
Zik: They repair them. Reface them. Reuse them. Tear them down and build them again.
Narrator: He tapped the black mortar.
Zik: But nobody thinks about the mud between the bricks.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo nodded slowly.
Enhedu: So you read the mud.
Zik: I read the people who mixed it.
Narrator: For a moment no one spoke. The desert wind hissed across the ridge above them, carrying another veil of sand into the excavation. It settled over their boots and against the exposed face of the ruin as though trying to bury it again. KOO-rahsh looked back toward the open desert.
Kurash: [eager] My uncle always said the old kings hid their treasure because they feared thieves.
Narrator: BAH-lahg snorted.
Balag: [snorts] The old kings were the thieves.
Kurash: [sheepish] I suppose that's true.
Kurash: [curious] So why bury it at all?
Narrator: Zeek's eyes stayed on the wall. The pattern of replacements held his attention even while he answered.
Zik: [thoughtful] Maybe it wasn't treasure they wanted hidden.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo followed his gaze.
Enhedu: [quietly] What, then?
Zik: [quietly] I don't know.
Narrator: He brushed one last ribbon of sand from the black mortar.
Zik: [ominous] But whatever this place was built to keep…
Narrator: His fingers paused against the glassy seam.
Zik: [ominous] …someone came back long after it was finished and decided it still wasn't safe enough.
Narrator: No one joked after that.
Zik: [firmly] Clear it,
Narrator: Zeek said.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They dug until the sun started its slow drop toward the west. Sand slid back into the hole as fast as they pulled it out. The deeper they went, the colder the air turned. A draft rose out of the dark and touched Zeek's face. It smelled of dry earth. Under that was something that had never met the sun.
Narrator: The stairway went farther down than the structure above had any right to allow. Walls narrowed until they walked single file. BAH-lahg took the lead because he was largest and apparently considered it his job to find whatever killed them first. En-KHEH-doo came next. Zeek followed. KOO-rahsh brought up the rear and complained under his breath every time the steps grew steeper. Zeek paid him no mind. He listened.
Narrator: The deeper they went, the quieter the surface became. Wind and shifting sand faded first. Then the voices of the men working above. Then the faint creak of the rope they had used to secure the entrance. The stairway did not carry sound. It took it. BAH-lahg stopped without warning. Zeek nearly walked into him.
Enhedu: [whispers] What?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo kept her voice low.
Narrator: BAH-lahg raised one hand. For several long breaths nothing moved in the dark past the reach of the torch. Then, far below, a sound reached them—not a knock, not stone scraping stone, but a low vibration, as if something massive had shifted its weight under a great thickness of rock. BAH-lahg touched two fingers to his chest in the old ward his grandfather had taught him. Zeek had seen the gesture before, always when BAH-lahg thought no one watched.
Enhedu: [whispers] [teasing] You believe in that now?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Balag: [seriously] I believe in not insulting whatever sits under this much earth.
Enhedu: [dryly] Specific religion.
Balag: [dryly] Keep talking. I'll convert you.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They went on. At the bottom stood a stone door set so clean into the wall that the seam nearly vanished. Heavy bronze brackets held it shut from the outside. Age had darkened the metal but left it free of corrosion. Each bracket carried the same mark: a jagged spiral closed around a single drop. Zeek had only seen that symbol on private royal seals, the kind belonging to kings whose reigns ended briefly, violently, and with their names scrubbed from most records. He crouched beside the nearest bracket.
Balag: [quietly] How old?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Zik: Older than the door.
Balag: [dryly] Not helpful.
Zik: [dryly] Wasn't trying to be.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo stepped up beside him. Her eyes moved across the bronze, the stone, the symbol.
Enhedu: [uneasy] Whatever's inside wasn't trying to leave.
Narrator: Zeek considered the brackets.
Zik: [quietly] Someone worked hard to keep it there.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's smile returned, slow and sharp.
Enhedu: [confidently] Then they chose the wrong door.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They spent the better part of an hour on the brackets. The bronze had been driven deep and sealed with something harder than ordinary mortar. BAH-lahg worked the iron tools. En-KHEH-doo searched the masonry for weak points. Zeek checked every inch of the entrance for traps while KOO-rahsh stood off to the side, shifting his weight and asking twice whether they were certain anything waited on the other side. Zeek told him yes. The door opened with a long scream of metal that traveled down the passage and came back altered. Torchlight spilled into the chamber beyond.
Narrator: Zeek held everyone at the threshold while he read the room from the doorway. The jamb carried no soot above shoulder height, which meant no ordinary lamp had burned here after the chamber was sealed. A faint gray bloom clung to the lower stones instead. He scraped it with the tip of the awl and rubbed the powder between finger and thumb. Salt, but not the loose desert salt that crept into abandoned masonry. This had risen through the wall from below, drawn upward by water that should not have existed at this depth.
Narrator: The floor had settled less than a finger's breadth toward the throne. Every line of gold followed that fall. Plates overlapped cups at measured intervals; chains bridged the narrow gaps; coins filled the spaces between them. It was a complete surface disguised as treasure. Zeek pointed out the darker squares where the weight changed and made KOO-rahsh repeat the warning back to him. KOO-rahsh did, impatiently, his eyes never leaving the nearest bowl. BAH-lahg tested the lintel with the iron bar. En-KHEH-doo studied the seams rather than the wealth.
Enhedu: [suspicious] No dust on the gold,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: There was dust everywhere else. It lay thick along the walls, packed into the throne's carvings, soft enough on the untouched border of the floor to preserve the smallest disturbance. The treasure itself remained clean.
Kurash: [eager] Air movement,
Narrator: KOO-rahsh said.
Zik: [sharply] There is no air movement,
Narrator: Zeek replied. The torch flames stood straight. He could hear each man's breathing and, beneath it, a faint mineral tick from somewhere under the black paving.
Zik: [firmly] Nothing crosses that pattern until I know what it does.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh nodded. The answer came easily. Believing it was another matter. Gold covered the floor in careful geometric lines across black stone—plates, cups, coins, small statues, chains, jewelry—the wealth of a kingdom arranged with such deliberate care that the hoard looked less like an offering and more like a mechanism left waiting. No one moved at first. BAH-lahg stayed where he was. En-KHEH-doo's eyes narrowed. KOO-rahsh made a sound that almost became a laugh.
Zik: [sharply] Don't,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh looked at him.
Kurash: [innocently] Don't what?
Zik: [firmly] Touch anything.
Narrator: The boy's gaze slid back to the gold.
Kurash: [curious] Why?
Zik: [tense] Because no one buries a room like this and walks away without leaving something behind to guard it.
Kurash: [dismissively] Maybe they died.
Zik: [flatly] Then the trap is still here.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh's expression shifted. Zeek watched the calculation happen behind his eyes. The boy no longer saw a warning. He saw a house. A horse. A woman. A life that no longer required following another man's orders. Zeek knew that look. He had believed the same things once.
Balag: [whispers] [warning] KOO-rahsh,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said, voice low.
Narrator: The young man moved anyway. He crossed the threshold and closed his hand around a heavy golden bowl. The pattern on the floor broke. A single tile sank under his foot. For one heartbeat nothing happened. Then the ceiling opened. The stone slab dropped with perfect, silent speed. It caught KOO-rahsh below the ribs and drove him into the floor. His scream lasted less than a second. The slab pinned him across the gold. Blood spread in a dark sheet between the coins and over the curve of the bowl still trapped in his hand.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one spoke. BAH-lahg took one step forward, hand reaching toward the body as if he could pull the moment back. En-KHEH-doo stood motionless, knife still under her arm, face unreadable. The blood reached the edge of a coin and kept going. Zeek's share of the room had just grown by one. The first thought that arrived was practical: one less share. The second was that he hated himself for thinking it. The second thought lasted longer.
[pause 1.5s]
Zik: [shaken] Don't touch the gold,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: BAH-lahg turned on him.
Balag: [bitterly] You think?
Narrator: Zeek stepped around the body and entered the chamber. The treasure had been arranged around a central path. The pattern itself made the trap obvious once it had sprung. Whoever built the room had not wanted to keep people out. They had wanted to control the way people came in.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: At the far end a stone throne rose from the floor. A skeleton sat upon it, wrapped in the last scraps of royal robes so old they seemed to cling to the bones only because they had forgotten how to fall away. A crown of blackened silver rested on the skull. At its center sat the same spiral and drop. Zeek had seen versions of that mark before. It appeared on private seals belonging to kings whose reigns ended short and incomplete. Some vanished. Others were erased from monuments so thoroughly that later scholars argued over whether they had ever existed. He had always called it politics. Now he was less certain.
Narrator: A leather-bound journal rested in the skeleton's lap. Beside it lay a small brass compass. The torchlight caught the compass and threw a dull gleam across its face. He knew it. He had watched his father carry that same compass through most of his childhood—the worn leather case, the dent along the rim, the tiny scratch across the glass. His father had been carrying it the day he disappeared. Something in Zeek's chest pulled tight, sharp and unexpected. For a moment the chamber seemed too small. He forced the breath out slowly and crossed the room.
Balag: [warning] Zeek,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said behind him.
Narrator: He ignored the warning. The compass lay clean amid the dust, as if the years had simply refused to touch it. When he picked it up the metal was cold enough to hurt. The needle spun once, then twice, then settled. It pointed straight down. He stood there longer than he meant to, turning the compass once in his hand, before he told himself the instrument was broken and opened the journal. The leather was cracked and dark with age, but the binding still held. The last page was already waiting. Two lines stood in his father's hand:
Bel-iddin: [solemn] IF YOU FIND THIS, DO NOT LOOK FOR ME.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] FIND THE CITY.
Narrator: A third line had been added beneath in different ink. The handwriting was uneven, the letters pressed so hard the pen had nearly torn the page.
Bel-iddin: [urgently] ZIKIR—WHATEVER YOU DO, DO NOT OPEN THE SECOND DOOR.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The words sat there. Zeek read them twice. The chamber stayed quiet around him. Then the torch flame bent sideways. The shadows on the walls were moving, and not with the fire. The skeleton's jaw shifted. BAH-lahg swore under his breath. En-KHEH-doo's knife was in her hand before the sound finished leaving his mouth. The empty sockets of the skull turned toward Zeek.
Dead King: [whispers] Zee-keer AHSH-shoor,
[pause 2s]
Narrator: the dead mouth rasped.
Narrator: Then the skeleton collapsed. Bones struck stone. Dust rose from the throne and rolled across the floor. Zeek stumbled back, journal clutched to his chest, as the chamber trembled under their feet. Something far below the tomb shifted—slow, massive, almost gentle, like a sleeper turning over beneath the earth. Zeek closed the journal. The sound echoed through the chamber.
Zik: [shakily] Well,
Narrator: he said, voice steadier than he felt,
Zik: [deadpan] that's new.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 2 — THE WEIGHT OF THE SHARE

Narrator: CHAPTER 2
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Weight of the Share
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They left the tomb at dusk.
Narrator: The desert wind had already begun erasing their tracks. Sand slid into the open stairway behind them in soft continuous sheets, as if the ground itself preferred the hole filled. BAH-lahg walked ahead with the two remaining donkeys, spear across his shoulders. En-KHEH-doo kept to the side, knife loose in her hand, eyes on the darkening ridges. Zeek brought up the rear, the journal and the compass both heavy against his ribs.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one spoke for the first hour.
Narrator: The donkeys noticed before any of them admitted it.
Narrator: The lead animal slowed every few hundred paces, ears twitching as though waiting for the familiar whistle that always came from somewhere behind the line. KOO-rahsh had been the one to soothe them when they grew stubborn. He talked to them more than he talked to people, inventing long stories they could not possibly understand.
Narrator: Now the lead donkey stopped altogether.
Narrator: BAH-lahg sighed through his nose and walked back.
Balag: [sighs] Come on.
Narrator: The animal planted its feet.
Narrator: BAH-lahg tugged the lead rope.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Nothing.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo watched for a moment.
Enhedu: [uneasy] It keeps looking behind us.
Balag: [gruffly] It smells him.
Enhedu: [quietly] No.
Narrator: She shook her head.
Enhedu: [softly] It smells that he's gone.
Narrator: BAH-lahg gave the donkey another patient pull.
Balag: [grumbling] I liked you better when you only corrected my archaeology.
Enhedu: [dryly] I've never corrected your archaeology.
Balag: [dryly] You've corrected everything else.
Narrator: That earned the faintest curl at the corner of her mouth.
Narrator: The expression vanished almost immediately.
Narrator: BAH-lahg rested his forehead briefly against the donkey's neck before trying again.
Balag: [gently] Stubborn beast.
Zik: [softly] It learned from KOO-rahsh,
Narrator: Zeek said quietly.
Narrator: The words surprised even him.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked over his shoulder.
Balag: [softly] So it did.
Narrator: This time the donkey started walking without another protest.
Narrator: For several minutes only the sound of hooves disturbed the desert.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Finally En-KHEH-doo spoke.
Enhedu: [quietly] My brother used to whistle.
Narrator: Neither man answered.
Enhedu: [wistful] He couldn't carry a tune to save his life.
Narrator: Her eyes remained on the horizon.
Enhedu: [wistful] My mother always knew he was home because every dog in the neighborhood started barking.
Narrator: Another stretch of silence.
Enhedu: [quietly] He died before he turned twenty.
Narrator: BAH-lahg glanced sideways.
Balag: [gently] You've never mentioned him.
Enhedu: [flatly] There hasn't been a reason.
Balag: [gently] And now?
Narrator: She shrugged.
Enhedu: [casually] There still isn't.
Balag: [softly] But KOO-rahsh reminded you.
Narrator: She considered lying.
Narrator: Instead she nodded once.
Enhedu: [quietly] He reminded me that people disappear faster than habits do.
Narrator: No one had an answer for that.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The wind shifted.
Narrator: Behind them, another ribbon of sand spilled over the ridge where the tomb lay buried once more.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh's absence sat between them like a fourth set of footprints that refused to fade. Every gust left fewer places where KOO-rahsh had ever walked.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: When they finally stopped, BAH-lahg dropped his gear and began checking the animals' hooves with the same methodical care he gave every practical task. En-KHEH-doo crouched a short distance away and cleaned dried blood from under her nails with the tip of her knife. The motion was precise, almost delicate.
Narrator: Zeek sat with his back against a low outcrop and stared at the linen wrapping on his left hand. The mark beneath it was quiet for the first time since the tomb. He did not trust the quiet.
Narrator: BAH-lahg finished with the donkeys and came to sit across from him. The big man did not look at the journal.
Balag: [gruffly] You counting the share already?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek glanced up.
Zik: What?
Balag: [knowingly] KOO-rahsh's share. You're sitting there doing the arithmetic in your head. I've seen that face before.
Narrator: Zeek almost denied it. Then he shrugged.
Zik: [coldly] If I don't, somebody else will.
Narrator: The words came out harder than he meant. He heard how cold they sounded and hated himself for a second.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg just grunted.
Balag: [gruffly] Someone always does. Doesn't mean I have to like watching it.
Narrator: He was quiet for a few breaths, then added, almost to the sand,
Balag: [quietly] First crew I worked on, we lost a boy before we even reached the quarry. Fifteen years old. Same hungry look. Same fucking math.
Narrator: He shook his head once.
Balag: [quietly] Still don't like the sound of it.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's knife paused.
Enhedu: [coldly] He would have done the same to any of us if the numbers came out that way.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Zik: [bitterly] That supposed to make it easier?
Enhedu: [flatly] No. It's supposed to make it true.
Narrator: They sat with that for a time. The desert cooled around them. Somewhere far off a night bird called once and did not call again.
Narrator: BAH-lahg eventually stood and checked the straps on the packs a second time, even though nothing had shifted. Zeek recognized the habit. The big man trusted his hands more than he trusted silence.
Balag: We'll reach the city in two days if the weather holds,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Balag: [grimly] Collector's going to notice the missing man.
Zik: [dismissively] Let him notice,
Narrator: Zeek answered.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo wiped the last of the blood from her blade and slid it home.
Enhedu: [dryly] He'll notice the extra silver more.
Narrator: Zeek almost smiled. Almost.
Narrator: He opened the journal instead.
Narrator: The last page was still waiting. The three lines in his father's hand had not changed. The ink no longer looked wet, but the paper itself felt slightly warmer than the desert night should have allowed.
Narrator: He closed it again before anything new could appear.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They slept in shifts. Zeek took the middle watch. He spent it staring at the dark and listening for the low vibration they had heard beneath the tomb. It did not come. The mark on his palm stayed quiet. The compass, when he checked it, still pointed straight down.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Toward morning En-KHEH-doo relieved him. She sat without speaking for a long time, turning a small copper figurine between her fingers the way other people turned prayer tokens. Time had polished its smiling face nearly smooth.
Enhedu: [quietly] You going to tell the Collector everything?
Narrator: she asked eventually.
Zik: [firmly] No.
Enhedu: [approvingly] Good.
Narrator: She tossed the figurine once and caught it.
Enhedu: [knowingly] Everything has a price. Some prices are better left unpaid.
Narrator: Zeek did not answer. He lay down, pulled his cloak over his face, and tried not to dream of water that wrote.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They broke camp at first light and continued toward OO-rook.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Behind them, the last trace of KOO-rahsh's footprints disappeared under the morning wind.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 3 — THE CRACKED STRAP

Narrator: CHAPTER 3
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Cracked Strap
[pause 2s]
Narrator: By mid-morning the heat had climbed into that dry weight that made thinking feel expensive. One of the donkeys started favoring its left hind leg just after they crossed a stretch of hardpan. BAH-lahg noticed first. He always did.
Narrator: They stopped in the thin shade of a dead acacia that looked like it had argued with the wind for a century and finally lost. BAH-lahg crouched beside the animal, hands moving down the leg with the careful attention of someone who trusted what his fingers told him more than anything a person might say.
Balag: [mutters] Not the leg,
Narrator: he muttered.
Balag: [mutters] Strap's gone soft. Leather's cracking through.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo squinted at the sun.
Enhedu: Spare?
Balag: One. Older than this one.
Narrator: Zeek dropped his pack and knelt without being asked. He pulled the damaged strap free and turned it over. The leather had dried and split along the stress line where the buckle bit. Not finished yet, but another day of this heat and it would go completely.
Narrator: He dug out a short length of braided cord and the small bronze awl he kept for quiet failures like this. The others watched while he worked. He scored the cracked section, cut away the worst of the dry edge, drove new holes, then rebound the strap in a tight overlapping pattern that would take the load better than the original leather ever had.
Narrator: It wasn't pretty. It didn't need to be.
Narrator: BAH-lahg rolled the repaired strap between his hands, pressing a thumb against every new stitch.
Balag: [teasing] You always sew like you're angry.
Zik: [dryly] I'm not sewing.
Balag: [amused] No?
Zik: [dryly] I'm arguing with bad leather.
Narrator: BAH-lahg snorted.
Balag: [snorts] The leather's losing.
Zik: [dryly] It usually does.
Narrator: He looped the strap back through the buckle and cinched it tight around the pack frame. The leather creaked in protest but held.
Balag: [satisfied] Good enough,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Zik: [dryly] It'll outlive the donkey.
Balag: [mock-offended] The donkey heard that.
Narrator: As if to object, the animal swung its head toward Zeek and gave a long, offended bray.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked up.
Enhedu: [amused] I think it agrees with BAH-lahg.
Zik: [dryly] It always agrees with whoever isn't asking it to work.
Narrator: BAH-lahg scratched the animal behind one ear.
Balag: [fondly] You'd complain too if someone expected you to carry half a mountain through the desert.
Enhedu: [indignant] I don't complain.
Balag: [teasing] You complain constantly.
Enhedu: [primly] I make observations.
Balag: [teasing] Very loudly.
Enhedu: They're important observations.
Balag: [grumbling] They're mostly about me.
Enhedu: [smugly] They're the easiest ones to verify.
Narrator: For the first time since leaving the tomb, BAH-lahg laughed without forcing it.
Narrator: The sound caught all three of them by surprise.
Narrator: It died almost as quickly as it had come.
Narrator: Zeek busied himself trimming one loose strand from the braided cord.
Zik: [softly] I'd forgotten what that sounded like.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked away.
Balag: [quietly] So had I.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The silence returned, but it no longer felt like something pressing on their shoulders. It settled beside them instead.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo picked up the strip of ruined leather and turned it over in her hands.
Narrator: The crack ran almost perfectly through its center.
Narrator: She rubbed her thumb along the brittle edge.
Enhedu: [thoughtful] You know why leather fails here?
Narrator: BAH-lahg shrugged.
Balag: [casually] Because it gets old.
Enhedu: No.
Narrator: She handed it to Zeek.
Enhedu: [knowingly] He'll see it.
Narrator: Zeek studied the split.
Zik: [thoughtful] It wasn't the weight.
Narrator: BAH-lahg folded his arms.
Balag: Go on.
Zik: It wasn't the heat either.
Narrator: He bent the leather gently.
Zik: See these lighter lines?
Narrator: Neither of them answered.
Zik: It was drying from the inside.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned.
Balag: [skeptical] Leather can do that?
Zik: It loses the oils first.
Balag: The outside still looks fine.
Zik: Exactly.
Narrator: He handed the scrap back.
Zik: [meaningfully] It fails long before it looks broken.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo slipped it into her pouch.
Enhedu: [thoughtful] Useful thing to remember.
Narrator: BAH-lahg raised an eyebrow.
Balag: [skeptical] For leather?
Enhedu: [quietly] For people.
Narrator: Neither man argued.
Narrator: BAH-lahg tested the repair with two hard tugs and grunted.
Balag: [grunts] It'll hold.
Zik: [dryly] Until it doesn't,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Zik: [darkly] Same as everything else.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo reached down, picked up the scrap of ruined leather Zeek had cut away, and slipped it into a pouch at her belt without comment. BAH-lahg noticed.
Balag: [puzzled] What the hell do you want with that?
Enhedu: [flatly] Waste bothers me.
Narrator: That was all she offered. She turned the scrap once between her fingers, then left it alone.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They rested longer than the schedule allowed. The animals needed it more than anyone wanted to admit. Zeek sat with his back against the dead tree and took the journal out only long enough to confirm the last page hadn't changed. Three lines. No new ink. He closed it and, for the first time since the tomb, deliberately left it in the pack instead of keeping it against his ribs.
Narrator: The decision felt small. It also felt like the first thing he had chosen rather than reacted to since the skeleton spoke his name.
Narrator: BAH-lahg noticed him staring at nothing in particular.
Balag: You thinking about the Collector?
Zik: [tired] Thinking about how much silver it takes to keep a mother in medicine and a crew in rope.
Narrator: BAH-lahg spat into the dust.
Balag: [bitterly] Same fucking math again.
Zik: [wearily] Always is.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They moved on once the shadow of the acacia had shrunk to nothing useful. The repaired strap held. The donkeys settled into their usual sullen rhythm. The journal stayed in the pack, quiet for once.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Late afternoon the wind shifted and brought the faintest smell of smoke and river mud. OO-rook was still a day out, but the desert had started remembering there were cities in the world.
Narrator: Zeek flexed his left hand inside the linen. The mark was warm again. Not painful. Just present, like a second pulse that hadn't been invited.
Narrator: He did not check the compass.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: He already knew which way it pointed.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 4 — THE EDGE OF SMOKE

Narrator: CHAPTER 4
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Edge of Smoke
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The desert didn't end so much as it thinned.
Narrator: By late afternoon the dunes had flattened into hard, broken ground scattered with the first signs of people who lived too close to nothing: a collapsed sheepfold, a half-buried boundary stone, a scatter of broken pottery no one had bothered to gather. The air carried the faint greasy smell of cooking fires and the sharper edge of river mud. OO-rook was still hours away, but the land had started remembering it.
Narrator: They walked in a loose line. BAH-lahg kept the donkeys moving with short clicks of his tongue. En-KHEH-doo had fallen a few paces back. Zeek stayed near the middle, the journal still in the pack where he'd left it the day before.
Narrator: A low mud-brick wall appeared on the right, half-swallowed by sand. Someone had tried to keep a garden once. A few dried stalks still clung to the soil like they hadn't gotten the message. BAH-lahg stopped the animals and walked the length of the wall, testing the corners with the heel of his boot.
Balag: [approvingly] Someone put real work into this,
Narrator: he said.
Balag: Foundations are deeper than they needed to be.
Narrator: Zeek came up beside him. He ran a hand along the top course. The mortar was ordinary, but the joints had been laid with care. Whoever built it had known what they were doing and still lost the argument with the desert.
Narrator: One section near the middle had slumped. Two courses of brick had shifted outward, the mortar cracked into a long jagged seam. It wasn't dangerous. It also wasn't right.
Narrator: Zeek set his pack down, pulled the bronze awl and a short length of cord from the side pocket, and began working without asking anyone's opinion. He scraped the loose mortar free, tapped the shifted bricks back into true, then bound the seam with the cord driven through fresh holes so the load would sit properly again. It wouldn't last another season. It would last longer than it would have if he'd walked past it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg watched for a while, then said,
Balag: [curious] Why bother?
Narrator: Zeek didn't look up.
Zik: [quietly] Because it was built right.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stood beside him a while longer.
Balag: That's not an answer.
Zik: It is.
Balag: It isn't.
Narrator: Zeek packed fresh mortar into the seam with his thumb.
Zik: [quietly] The people who built this wall are gone.
Balag: [dryly] I noticed.
Zik: They spent days making sure it stood.
Balag: So?
Zik: [firmly] So somebody should respect the effort.
Narrator: BAH-lahg scratched his beard.
Balag: [skeptical] You think the wall cares?
Zik: No.
Balag: Then who?
Narrator: Zeek looked along the crumbling length of brick disappearing into the sand.
Zik: [softly] The next person who leans against it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg was quiet.
Balag: [thoughtful] You fix things you'll never see again.
Zik: [quietly] I excavate things no one remembers.
Balag: That's different.
Zik: [quietly] I don't think it is.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo brushed dust from her knees and joined them.
Enhedu: [wistful] My grandfather repaired irrigation ditches.
Narrator: Neither man interrupted.
Enhedu: [fondly] He'd spend weeks fixing channels on land that wasn't his.
Narrator: BAH-lahg smiled.
Balag: [teasing] I've met farmers.
Enhedu: He wasn't a farmer.
Balag: No?
Enhedu: [proudly] He copied contracts.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned.
Balag: [skeptical] Then why repair ditches?
Enhedu: [fondly] He said the water couldn't read ownership marks.
Narrator: Zeek laughed softly.
Narrator: The sound surprised all three of them.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo tilted her head.
Enhedu: [amused] First laugh in three days.
Zik: [dryly] I wasn't keeping count.
Enhedu: [softly] I was.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked from one to the other.
Balag: [grumbling] I preferred it when neither of you talked.
Enhedu: [teasing] You've said that before.
Balag: [dryly] I'll say it again.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo rested a hand on the repaired section of wall.
Enhedu: [quietly] It'll fail.
Zik: Eventually.
Enhedu: You know that.
Zik: Everything fails.
Enhedu: [curious] So why fix it?
Narrator: Zeek tightened the final knot.
Zik: [firmly] Because failure doesn't mean today.
Narrator: The three of them stood looking at the wall for another moment.
Narrator: The repair wasn't invisible.
Narrator: It wasn't meant to be.
Narrator: The new cord crossed the old brick in pale lines that anyone could see.
Narrator: Someone else would come along one day and know another pair of hands had cared enough to keep it standing a little longer.
Narrator: That was all. He finished the last knot, tested the repaired section with his weight, and stood. The wall still leaned a little. It no longer looked like it was giving up.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo had wandered over during the work. She crouched, picked up one of the loose bricks Zeek had set aside, weighed it in her hand, then set it neatly back into the gap he hadn't needed. No comment. Just the brick returned to where it belonged.
Narrator: They didn't rest long after that. The light was already sliding toward the kind of copper dusk that made distances hard to trust. Zeek adjusted a slipped pack strap on the nearer donkey while the others drank. The motion was automatic. His hands knew the work even when his mind was somewhere else.
Narrator: BAH-lahg watched him a moment longer than necessary.
Balag: [knowingly] You still thinking about the Collector?
Zik: [quietly] I'm thinking about what I'm not going to tell him.
Narrator: BAH-lahg gave a short nod, as if that answer was the only one he'd expected.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They started walking again. The broken ground gradually gave way to the first real tracks—cart ruts, donkey dung, the scuffed places where people had stood arguing about tolls or water or nothing important at all. The smell of the city grew stronger. Smoke. Onions. The particular sourness of too many bodies living too close together.
Narrator: Zeek felt the mark under the linen give a single slow pulse. Not a demand. Just a reminder that it was still there.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He kept walking.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Ahead, the first low roofs of the outer districts hunched against the darkening sky.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 5 — THE PRICE OF DUST

Narrator: CHAPTER 5
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Price of Dust
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The streets of OO-rook-Under-the-Sun stank of sweat, spiced lamb, hot dust, and the particular desperation that came with the end of the month. Zeek preferred it that way. In a city this loud, a man could think without being overheard.
Narrator: Merchants shouted over one another beneath patched awnings. Donkeys pushed through the crowd with the confidence of creatures that had never once considered whether anyone else had somewhere to be. Children slipped between adult legs. Smoke from cooking fires drifted low, carrying the smell of roasted meat and burning oil. Somewhere nearby a woman was screaming at a man who had either stolen from her or married her. Zeek did not stop to find out which.
Narrator: He moved through the market with his father's journal tucked against his ribs and the brass compass heavy in his pouch. The mark on his palm itched beneath the linen wrapping, a hot, persistent irritation that had followed him out of the tomb and refused every ordinary explanation. The third time he checked it, the shape matched the symbol on the dead king's crown exactly. He decided not to look again.
Narrator: That was how he handled most problems. If something refused to make sense, he gave it time to become someone else's problem.
Narrator: BAH-lahg and En-KHEH-doo kept a respectful distance behind him. Neither had asked what he found in the tomb. Neither had asked why he went pale when he opened the journal. They had both noticed, of course. BAH-lahg noticed everything that might kill him. En-KHEH-doo noticed everything that might make her rich.
Narrator: The Collector waited in the back room of a wine shop that pretended to be respectable. The front room was crowded with merchants drinking watered wine beneath a faded painted sign of a smiling woman holding a cup. The woman had lost most of her face to age and smoke, but the smile remained. Zeek had always thought that was a fair summary of the city.
Narrator: He pushed through the curtain at the back.
Narrator: The Collector looked up from his ledger. He was a narrow man with a narrow face, a narrow beard, and the sort of eyes that made people check whether their purses were still attached. His name had been forgotten by nearly everyone who dealt with him. That was deliberate. A man who bought stolen antiquities for a living had no use for a name people could remember.
The Collector: [dryly] You're late,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: Zeek dropped a small leather sack onto the table. It landed with a dull clatter.
Zik: [dryly] You're alive.
Narrator: The Collector glanced at the sack, then back at him.
The Collector: [smoothly] I was beginning to worry.
Zik: [skeptical] That's unlike you.
The Collector: [smoothly] It isn't.
Narrator: He opened the sack and began removing the contents one piece at a time: beads, a cylinder seal, two unbroken oil lamps, and a handful of minor bronze pieces that would fetch more than they deserved from the right buyer.
The Collector: [pointedly] You usually arrive with four men.
Narrator: Zeek said nothing.
Narrator: The Collector's fingers paused over the cylinder seal.
The Collector: [coldly] You're missing one.
Zik: [flatly] KOO-rahsh got greedy.
Narrator: The Collector looked up.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek shrugged.
Zik: [casually] The desert is full of traps. You know how it is.
The Collector: Yes.
Narrator: The Collector turned the seal over in his fingers.
The Collector: [sarcastic] The desert is famously responsible for the poor decisions of men.
Zik: [flatly] That one was.
Narrator: The Collector's eyes moved toward Zeek's left hand.
The Collector: [insinuating] One less share means more for you.
Narrator: Zeek smiled the way a man smiled when he was holding a knife behind his back.
Zik: [coldly] That's how the world works.
The Collector: [skeptical] Is it?
Zik: [flatly] Everything has a price.
Narrator: The Collector's smile was thin.
The Collector: [smoothly] You sound like your father.
Narrator: The room seemed to become a little smaller.
Narrator: Zeek kept his face still.
Zik: [coldly] Then you should know better than to insult me.
Narrator: The Collector chuckled and returned his attention to the finds.
Narrator: The Collector held up one of the bronze pieces between two fingers.
The Collector: [dismissively] Corrosion.
Zik: [annoyed] It's three thousand years old.
The Collector: [smoothly] So it is.
Zik: [sarcastic] You expected polish?
The Collector: [dryly] I expected better preservation.
Zik: It survived three thousand years.
The Collector: [dryly] It barely survived you.
Narrator: Zeek leaned one hand against the table.
Zik: [knowingly] You've already decided who you're selling it to.
The Collector: [evasive] I've considered possibilities.
Zik: Temple collector.
The Collector: No.
Zik: Foreign merchant.
The Collector: No.
Zik: Noble trying to impress a second wife.
Narrator: The Collector smiled.
The Collector: [amused] Closer.
Narrator: Zeek nodded toward the bronze.
Zik: [impatient] Then stop pretending it's worthless.
The Collector: [smoothly] It is worthless.
Zik: [sharply] You just called it desirable.
The Collector: [smoothly] I called the buyer foolish.
Zik: [dryly] That's a valuable distinction.
Narrator: The Collector reached for the cylinder seal instead.
The Collector: [pleased] This one I like.
Zik: I know.
The Collector: [dryly] Unfortunately.
Zik: So you'll pay for it.
The Collector: I'll pay enough.
Zik: You'll pay honestly.
The Collector: [amused] I've never done that.
Zik: [dryly] I know.
Narrator: They haggled for nearly twenty minutes, which meant the Collector offered half of what the objects were worth and Zeek threatened to take them elsewhere without ever naming another buyer. Neither of them believed the other. That was the entire point.
Narrator: In the end Zeek walked away with enough silver to keep his mother in medicine for another month, enough coin to replace the supplies lost in the desert, and enough lies to keep the Collector from asking the questions that mattered.
Narrator: He was almost through the front room when the Collector spoke again.
The Collector: [softly] Zee-keer.
Narrator: Zeek stopped. The Collector had not used his full name in years.
Narrator: He turned slowly. The Collector was still sitting behind the table, one hand resting on the open ledger. His gaze had returned to Zeek's palm.
The Collector: [curious] Where did you get that?
Narrator: Zeek looked down at his hand. The linen concealed the mark completely.
Zik: [guarded] What?
Narrator: The Collector's eyes narrowed.
The Collector: [smoothly] Nothing.
Narrator: Zeek held his stare for another second, then turned and walked out. He did not hurry. Hurrying suggested guilt.
Narrator: He waited until he was three streets away before checking the wrapping. The linen was still tied exactly as he had left it. Beneath it, the mark was warm.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo caught up with him near the Street of Scribes. She moved through the crowd like a knife through cloth, slipping between merchants and customers without apologizing to either. She was smaller than BAH-lahg and far less imposing, but people moved out of her way more quickly. Zeek had never decided whether that was because of the knife at her hip or because of the expression she wore when someone did not.
Enhedu: [teasing] You kept the good stuff,
Narrator: she said.
Zik: [dryly] Always do.
Enhedu: That wasn't an accusation.
Zik: [dryly] It sounded like one.
Enhedu: [amused] Then you should have heard the version I didn't say.
Narrator: Zeek kept walking. The Street of Scribes opened ahead of them, narrower and quieter than the main market. Clay tablets sat in neat rows beneath awnings, covered in the wedge marks of contracts, inventories, prayers, and curses—the kind of writing that turned ownership into something people could pretend was natural.
Narrator: A line of apprentices sat cross-legged outside one of the scriptoria, each with a damp clay tablet balanced on a board across their knees. A gray-bearded instructor drifted slowly behind them with a reed cane tucked beneath one arm. He didn't strike anyone with it. He simply reached over shoulders, erased wedges with a wet thumb, and made the students begin again.
Narrator: One boy looked up as Zeek passed.
Apprentice Boy: [shyly] Master Zee-keer.
Narrator: Zeek stopped.
Narrator: The boy couldn't have been older than twelve. His fingertips were stained gray with clay, and one side of his face carried the unmistakable imprint of having fallen asleep on a tablet.
Zik: [surprised] I know you?
Apprentice Boy: [shyly] No, Master.
Narrator: The title still sounded strange.
Apprentice Boy: [shyly] But my instructor does.
Narrator: The old scribe finally looked over, squinting against the afternoon light.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Recognition came slowly.
Old Scribe: [slowly] …BAYL-ID-din's son.
Narrator: Zeek inclined his head.
Zik: I am.
Narrator: The old man studied him for a long moment.
Old Scribe: [skeptical] I thought you'd left archaeology.
Zik: I've thought the same thing.
Narrator: That earned the faintest twitch at the corner of the scribe's mouth.
Old Scribe: [wistful] He used to stand exactly there.
Narrator: The old man pointed to a worn patch of paving stones outside the school.
Old Scribe: [wistful] Every market day.
Zik: My father?
Old Scribe: [fondly] He'd argue with anyone willing to waste an afternoon.
Zik: What about?
Narrator: The old man snorted.
Old Scribe: [amused] What didn't he argue about?
Narrator: He leaned more heavily on his cane.
Old Scribe: [fondly] Mostly history.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo folded her arms.
Enhedu: [curious] History?
Old Scribe: [amused] He claimed scribes lied.
Bel-iddin: [gruffly] I never said that.
Narrator: The old man's voice shifted effortlessly into a rough imitation.
Bel-iddin: Scribes don't lie. They simply decide which truths survive.
Narrator: The imitation was so precise that Zeek almost turned, expecting to find his father standing beside him.
Narrator: Instead there was only the old man.
Zik: [curious] He said that often?
Old Scribe: [dryly] Often enough that I stopped inviting him inside.
Zik: [quietly] What happened?
Old Scribe: [flatly] He disappeared.
Narrator: The old scribe shrugged.
Old Scribe: [sadly] Before that, he stopped smiling.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence settled over the narrow street.
Narrator: One of the apprentices finally dared to ask,
Apprentice Boy: [shyly] Master…
Narrator: The old scribe waved him quiet without looking away from Zeek.
Old Scribe: [wistful] I always wondered whether he'd found what he was looking for.
Narrator: Zeek rested a hand against the journal hidden beneath his tunic.
Zik: [quietly] So do I.
Narrator: The old man nodded once.
Old Scribe: [gravely] If you discover the answer…
Narrator: His eyes drifted toward the crowded street beyond.
Old Scribe: [gravely] …write it down before someone important changes it.
Narrator: Without another word he turned back to his students.
Old Scribe: [firmly] Again.
Narrator: A chorus of groans rose from the apprentices as fresh tablets were passed out.
Narrator: BAH-lahg waited until they had walked half the length of the street before speaking.
Balag: [fondly] I liked him.
Zik: [dryly] You don't know him.
Balag: [amused] I know anyone who annoys scribes that much can't be all bad.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo glanced sideways at Zeek.
Enhedu: [gently] He sounded like your father.
Zik: [quietly] He did.
Enhedu: [teasing] You smiled.
Zik: [surprised] I did?
Enhedu: [teasing] For half a heartbeat.
Zik: [dryly] I'll try not to make a habit of it.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo walked beside him now.
Enhedu: [uneasy] That room didn't want us there.
Narrator: Zeek's expression did not change.
Enhedu: [quietly] And it especially didn't want you.
Zik: [dismissively] Desert tricks.
Enhedu: [skeptical] That's what you said before.
Zik: [dismissively] Sand, heat, imagination.
Enhedu: [quietly] You forgot the dead king.
Narrator: Zeek stopped walking.
Narrator: For a moment the noise of the market seemed to press in around them.
Zik: [sharply] Who told you?
Enhedu: [dryly] Nobody.
Zik: [suspicious] Then how do you know?
Enhedu: [knowingly] Because you came out of that tomb looking like a man who couldn't stab what he had seen.
Narrator: Zeek stared at her.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo shrugged.
Enhedu: [casually] It's a distinctive expression.
Narrator: He started walking again. She followed. Neither of them mentioned the dead king after that.
Narrator: Zeek's mother's house was a single cramped room above a potter's kiln. The heat never left. Even after sunset the walls radiated the day's warmth into the room, and the air smelled faintly of smoke, clay, and the bitter medicinal herbs his mother had begun boiling more often than she admitted.
Narrator: She was thinner than she had been the week before. Zeek noticed it immediately. He always noticed.
Narrator: She sat on her pallet beside the small window, wrapped in a faded shawl despite the heat. Her skin had taken on the dry, papery quality of old parchment, and the hollows beneath her eyes had deepened.
Narrator: Zeek set the medicine pouch beside her.
Zik: [gently] This should last you another month.
Narrator: His mother looked at the pouch, then at him.
Zik's Mother: [knowingly] You found something.
Narrator: Zeek removed his cloak.
Zik: [evasive] I found enough.
Zik's Mother: [sharply] That's not what I said.
Zik: [dryly] It is what I answered.
Narrator: She reached for his hand.
Narrator: Zeek moved before he thought about it.
Narrator: Her fingers closed around his wrist instead. For one terrible second neither of them moved. Her hand was cold. His skin beneath the linen was burning.
Narrator: His mother looked down at the wrapping, then up at him.
Zik's Mother: [sadly] You look like your father when he came back from that last trip.
Narrator: Zeek gently pulled his arm free.
Zik: [dryly] That's unfortunate.
Zik's Mother: [quietly] Same eyes.
Zik: [dryly] People have eyes.
Zik's Mother: [softly] Same lie.
Narrator: Zeek turned toward the window. Outside, the city was settling into evening. Lamps appeared one by one along the streets. Voices drifted upward from below, blurred by the heat.
Zik: [quietly] I'm not him.
Narrator: His mother was quiet for a while.
Zik's Mother: [softly] No,
Narrator: she said.
Zik's Mother: [softly] You never were.
Narrator: Zeek looked back at her.
Narrator: She had turned toward the wall.
Zik's Mother: [wistful] He believed some things should stay buried.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The words sat between them.
Narrator: Zeek touched the journal beneath his tunic.
Zik: [quietly] You think he was wrong?
Narrator: His mother did not answer immediately. When she did, her voice was soft.
Zik's Mother: I think your father believed that knowing gave him the right to touch it.
Narrator: Zeek said nothing.
Narrator: She coughed into her sleeve. The sound was small, but it seemed to fill the entire room.
Zik's Mother: [coughs] [softly] That's not the same as being brave,
Narrator: she said.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek looked away.
Narrator: He left without answering her. He never did.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: That night he sat on the roof above the kiln with the journal open in his lap. OO-rook stretched beneath him in layers of firelight and shadow. The city was beautiful from above, if a person was willing to ignore the smoke, the sewage, the screaming, and the fact that most of its wealth had been built by people who had never been allowed to keep any of it.
Narrator: Zeek pulled out his own compass and set it beside his father's. His pointed north. His father's pointed straight down.
Narrator: The needle trembled inside its brass housing as if it were trying to drill through the roof.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek frowned.
Narrator: Broken things usually behaved more honestly than this.
Narrator: He set both compasses on the roof tiles side by side.
Narrator: His own settled almost immediately.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: North.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Steady.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Predictable.
Narrator: His father's wandered in a slow uncertain circle before dropping once more toward the earth beneath the city.
Narrator: He rotated them together.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: North remained north.
Narrator: The other ignored the movement completely.
Narrator: He turned them again.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Nothing.
Narrator: The second compass refused every rule that made a compass worth carrying.
Narrator: Zeek picked it up and listened.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: No loose weight.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: No broken pivot.
Narrator: The needle moved too smoothly for damage.
Narrator: He held it beside the brass buckle on his pack.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: No change.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Beside his knife.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Nothing.
Narrator: He fished one of the bronze beads from the leather pouch he'd kept hidden from the Collector.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Still nothing.
Narrator: He tried the cylinder seal.
Narrator: The needle quivered for barely an instant before sinking downward again.
Zik: [thoughtful] Hm.
Narrator: That was interesting.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not useful.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Interesting.
Narrator: He opened the journal and laid it beside the compass.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Nothing happened.
Narrator: He slid the book farther away.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Still nothing.
Narrator: His fingers drummed absently against the linen wrapped around his left hand.
Narrator: The mark beneath it burned faintly.
Narrator: Without thinking, he rested that hand beside the compass.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The needle twitched.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek froze.
Narrator: Very slowly, he lowered his palm.
Narrator: The brass needle climbed.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not toward north.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Toward him.
Narrator: It stopped directly beneath the center of his wrapped hand.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: His pulse quickened.
Narrator: He lifted his hand.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The needle dropped.
Narrator: He lowered it again.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The same result.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Again.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The same.
Narrator: He unwrapped one corner of the linen just enough to expose the edge of the black spiral burned into his skin.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The mark pulsed.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The compass answered.
Narrator: The needle snapped upward so violently it struck the inside of the glass with a tiny metallic click.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek stared.
Narrator: He wrapped the linen back over the mark immediately.
Narrator: The needle drifted downward once more, settling toward the stone beneath the house as though nothing unusual had happened.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He sat motionless.
Narrator: Whatever his father had been searching for…
Narrator: the compass no longer believed it was merely buried.
Narrator: It believed part of it was sitting on the roof.
Narrator: He told himself it was broken. He almost believed it.
Narrator: The journal lay open beside him. He had not read the new lines again. He had read them enough.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] IF YOU FIND THIS, DO NOT LOOK FOR ME.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] FIND THE CITY.
Narrator: He had spent twelve years looking for his father. Now, apparently, his father had spent at least some portion of those twelve years trying to make sure he was not found.
Narrator: Zeek closed the journal.
Narrator: The mark beneath the linen pulsed once. He froze.
Narrator: The compass needle moved.
Narrator: Not toward north. Not toward the ground. For a single instant it pointed toward the journal. Then it spun and settled downward again.
Narrator: Zeek stared at it.
Narrator: Below him, somewhere deep beneath the city, there was a sound. It was not loud. It was not even clearly a sound. More like a pressure moving through stone.
Narrator: The roof beneath Zeek's hand gave a faint vibration. He remained perfectly still.
Narrator: The city continued around him. A donkey brayed. Someone shouted in the street. A child began crying.
Narrator: Then the vibration stopped.
Narrator: He did not sleep well.
Narrator: When sleep finally took him, he dreamed of black water moving around his ankles in a chamber he had never seen. Somewhere in the dark, something was writing—slow strokes, patient, one after another. A voice behind him said a name he had never heard.
Narrator: He woke with the taste of that water still in his mouth. The mark under the linen was warm.
Narrator: Below the floor, a single knock.
Narrator: Zeek sat up. The sound did not come again.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: He reached for his father's journal.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 6 — THE FOURTH SHARE

Narrator: CHAPTER 6
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Fourth Share
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They found her in the lower archives district two days after the Collector paid them.
Narrator: The building was half-sunk into the slope above the old flood channel, its upper rooms rented to scribes who couldn't afford better light and its lower rooms left to the kind of people who preferred not to be found. Zeek had come looking for a particular copy of a boundary tablet the Collector had mentioned in passing. BAH-lahg waited outside with the packs. En-KHEH-doo had already decided the place smelled like unpaid debts and was keeping one eye on the street.
Narrator: NEEN-soon was in the back room, seated at a low table covered in clay tablets and scrap parchment. Ink stained both wrists. A small oil lamp burned with the kind of careful thrift that said the oil was more expensive than the work. She looked up when Zeek's shadow crossed the tablets, and for a moment neither of them spoke.
Ninsun: [knowingly] You're the one who came out of the sealed tomb,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: It wasn't a question.
Narrator: Zeek kept his expression flat.
Zik: [flatly] People talk too much.
Ninsun: [dryly] People talk exactly enough.
Narrator: She set the stylus down.
Ninsun: [intense] The mark on your hand matches a symbol that appears on seven private royal seals and nowhere else in the public record. The same symbol that shows up in the oldest underworld fragments the temple pretends not to keep. I've been looking for someone who actually saw it in the ground instead of on a tablet.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's bulk filled the doorway behind Zeek. He didn't speak, but the way he stood said he had already decided this woman was either useful or a problem.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo drifted in last, eyes moving over the tablets, the lamp, the ink-stained hands.
Enhedu: [suspicious] You're not temple.
Ninsun: No.
Enhedu: [curious] University?
Ninsun: [flatly] Not anymore.
Enhedu: [skeptical] Then what are you?
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked at each of them in turn.
Ninsun: [intense] Someone who spent three years watching a district rearrange itself around my sister until the city records forgot she had ever lived there. Someone who is finished being told the old texts are only metaphor.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The room went quiet except for the small hiss of the lamp.
Narrator: Zeek let the silence stretch.
Narrator: People often rushed to fill silence.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: She didn't.
Narrator: That interested him more than anything she'd said so far.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Finally he spoke.
Zik: [guarded] You've seen the symbol.
Ninsun: Yes.
Zik: [sharply] Where?
Ninsun: [evasive] A dozen places.
Zik: [impatient] I asked where.
Narrator: NEEN-soon reached beneath one of the stacked tablets and produced a narrow wooden board wrapped in faded linen.
Narrator: She hesitated only long enough to brush dust from it before laying it carefully on the table.
Narrator: The linen came away.
Narrator: Inside rested seven clay impressions, each no larger than a man's thumb.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Cylinder seals.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Old ones.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Very old.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg stepped closer.
Balag: [surprised] You keep those here?
Ninsun: I copied them.
Balag: [alarmed] You copied royal seals?
Ninsun: [dryly] I copied the impressions.
Balag: [skeptical] That's still illegal.
Ninsun: [dryly] Only if someone important notices.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo leaned over the table.
Enhedu: [curious] They're different.
Ninsun: They are.
Narrator: NEEN-soon pointed to the first impression.
Ninsun: This belongs to King LOO-gahl AH-neh.
Narrator: Her finger moved to the next.
Ninsun: This one was catalogued as belonging to his grandson.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Then another.
Ninsun: Different century.
Narrator: Another.
Ninsun: Different dynasty.
Narrator: She looked at Zeek.
Ninsun: [testing] What do you see?
Narrator: He studied them.
Narrator: At first glance the spirals appeared identical.
Narrator: Then he noticed the tiny variations.
Zik: [slowly] No.
Narrator: He frowned.
Zik: [intrigued] They're changing.
Narrator: A flicker crossed her face.
Narrator: The closest thing to approval.
Ninsun: [testing] How?
Zik: The outer ring.
Narrator: He leaned lower.
Zik: [intrigued] It grows.
Narrator: She nodded.
Ninsun: [approvingly] Again.
Narrator: He looked farther along the line.
Zik: The center loses a turn.
Ninsun: Again.
Zik: [slowly] The spacing…
Narrator: He stopped.
Zik: [realizing] …they aren't copies.
Zik: [quietly] They're revisions.
Narrator: NEEN-soon folded her arms.
Ninsun: [satisfied] Exactly.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked from one to the other.
Balag: [grumbling] I feel like I'm listening to two people discuss sheep.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo ignored him.
Enhedu: [curious] Why revise a royal seal?
Ninsun: You don't.
Narrator: NEEN-soon answered immediately.
Ninsun: [firmly] You revise a story.
Narrator: She tapped the oldest impression.
Ninsun: This symbol appears nowhere before this reign.
Narrator: Another tap.
Ninsun: Then it appears everywhere.
Narrator: Another.
Ninsun: Then less often.
Narrator: Another.
Ninsun: Then only in private records.
Narrator: Finally she touched the last impression.
Ninsun: And then…
Narrator: She looked up.
Ninsun: [quietly] …it disappears.
Narrator: Zeek's eyes remained fixed on the clay.
Zik: [skeptical] But the kings are still there.
Ninsun: The kings survive.
Zik: [thoughtful] The symbol doesn't.
Ninsun: [pointedly] The official symbol doesn't.
Narrator: He looked at her.
Zik: [quietly] You think someone erased it.
Ninsun: [firmly] I know someone erased it.
Zik: [skeptical] How?
Ninsun: [intense] I've spent four years copying tablets that no longer agree with tablets copied twenty years earlier.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned.
Balag: [skeptical] People make mistakes.
Ninsun: [dryly] Scribes do.
Narrator: NEEN-soon nodded.
Ninsun: [firmly] Kings don't allow the same mistake on three hundred different tablets.
Narrator: She reached beneath the table again.
Narrator: This time she produced a broken fragment no larger than her palm.
Ninsun: [dryly] The temple archives catalogued this as an inventory.
Zik: [puzzled] It isn't?
Ninsun: It is.
Narrator: She turned it over.
Narrator: The reverse side carried faint scratches.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not cuneiform.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Something older.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The same spiral.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek stared.
Zik: [stunned] I've seen that carving.
Ninsun: [calmly] I know.
Zik: [quietly] In the tomb.
Ninsun: [calmly] I know.
Narrator: He looked up sharply.
Zik: [sharply] No.
Zik: [sharply] You don't know.
Ninsun: [calmly] I do.
Zik: [defensive] You couldn't.
Ninsun: [slowly] I couldn't…
Narrator: She smiled for the first time.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: It wasn't pleasant.
Ninsun: [coldly] …unless I've been following exactly the same trail as your father.
Narrator: The room became very quiet.
Narrator: Even the lamp seemed smaller.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at Zeek.
Balag: [suspicious] You never mentioned your father.
Zik: [guarded] I didn't.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's eyes stayed on him.
Ninsun: [knowingly] I didn't need you to.
Narrator: Zeek felt the mark under the linen give a single, slow pulse. He ignored it.
Zik: [flatly] We're not hiring,
Narrator: he said.
Ninsun: [firmly] I'm not asking to be hired.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stood. She was shorter than En-KHEH-doo and carried herself like someone who had learned to take up less space than she needed.
Ninsun: [intense] I'm telling you I already know more about the symbol on your skin than the Collector does, and I am coming with you whether you like the arrangement or not.
Narrator: BAH-lahg made a low sound that might have been a laugh.
Balag: [laughs] Confident.
Ninsun: [quietly] Desperate,
Narrator: NEEN-soon corrected.
Ninsun: [firmly] There's a difference.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo tilted her head, studying her the way she studied anything that might turn out to have unexpected value.
Enhedu: [shrewdly] What's your cut?
Ninsun: [flatly] I don't care about the silver.
Enhedu: [skeptical] Everyone cares about the silver.
Ninsun: [intense] I care about the tablets that keep going blank. I care about the streets that only exist when the fog is thick enough. I care about the fact that my sister's name no longer appears in any record the city is willing to admit exists.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice stayed level, but something underneath it had gone hard.
Ninsun: [intense] You can keep the silver. I want the truth the city is trying to edit out of itself.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her for a long moment.
Narrator: He thought of the wet ink in his father's journal. The compass that pointed straight down. The way the Collector had already known more than he should. He thought of the empty space where KOO-rahsh had walked and the way the desert had already started forgetting the boy's footprints.
Narrator: Four shares instead of three.
Narrator: The arithmetic was simple enough.
Zik: [coldly] You slow us down, we leave you,
Narrator: he said.
Ninsun: [calmly] Understood.
Zik: [coldly] You get us killed chasing blank tablets, I'll make sure the city remembers your name long enough to regret it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon almost smiled. It didn't reach her eyes.
Ninsun: [dryly] Also understood.
Narrator: BAH-lahg shifted his weight.
Balag: [impatient] We taking her or not?
Narrator: Zeek looked at En-KHEH-doo. She gave the smallest shrug, the kind that said she had already calculated the risk and decided it was acceptable for now.
Narrator: He turned back to NEEN-soon.
Zik: [firmly] Pack light. We leave at first light. And if the Collector asks, you were never here.
Narrator: NEEN-soon blew out the lamp.
Ninsun: [softly] I was never here,
Narrator: she agreed.
Narrator: Outside, the lower district continued its ordinary noise—scribes arguing, a donkey complaining, someone shouting about a missing delivery. The city had not yet noticed the fourth share being added.
Narrator: Zeek flexed his left hand inside the linen and started walking.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Behind him, NEEN-soon followed.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 7 — THE JOURNAL THAT REMEMBERS

Narrator: CHAPTER 7
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Journal That Remembers
[pause 2s]
Narrator: Zeek woke with the taste of black water still in his mouth.
Narrator: It was brackish and heavy. He sat upright on the narrow pallet, breathing through his nose while the taste clung to the back of his throat. The room was small, hot, and familiar. A cracked clay basin sat beside the wall. His boots were where he had left them. The thin curtain over the window stirred once and fell still.
Narrator: The dream had been short. Darkness with weight. Pressure against his chest. A voice calling him by a name he had never heard.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not Zeek.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The other name.
Narrator: He swung his feet to the floor and stood. Through the window the city lay under morning fog, its black spires jutting upward like jagged teeth. Ordinary sounds reached him—a cart on uneven stone, a woman shouting, a dog that barked once and went silent.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: They helped.
Narrator: Zeek turned to the bedside table. His father's journal lay there, bound in worn leather. He had placed it there the night before and left it untouched.
Narrator: He picked it up.
Narrator: The cover was cool against his palm. Almost damp. He opened it to the last marked page—an inventory of supply costs from a failed expedition years ago. Rope. Water. Lamp oil. Grain. Replacement tools. The dull accounting his father had always preferred to any heroic story.
Narrator: Zeek almost closed the book again.
Narrator: Then he saw the writing beneath the numbers.
Narrator: Dark ink shimmered faintly in the dim morning light. Not faded. Not cracked with age. Wet enough to catch the light.
Narrator: He closed the journal.
Narrator: Maybe he had missed it. The thought arrived quickly, almost desperately. Perhaps the writing had been there all along. Perhaps the light had been wrong. Perhaps he was exhausted.
Narrator: He waited, then opened it again.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The writing remained.
Bel-iddin: [gravely] Zee-keer, if you are reading this, the seal is failing.
Narrator: Zeek stopped breathing.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The next lines were written in a hand he knew better than his own.
Bel-iddin: [gravely] The First City is not a tomb.
Bel-iddin: [ominous] It is a lid.
Narrator: His fingers tightened on the edge of the page.
Bel-iddin: [warning] Do not trust the symbol on your skin.
Bel-iddin: [sorrowful] It was never meant for you.
Narrator: The last line sat alone.
Bel-iddin: [sorrowful] You were never supposed to find me.
Narrator: Zeek let the journal fall onto the bed.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: It landed open.
Narrator: The wet lines shone against the page.
Narrator: He stood over it, waiting for the ink to soak into the fibers or smear or do anything ordinary. It remained on the surface, black and glossy, as though the words had been written seconds ago by a hand that had only just withdrawn.
Narrator: Zeek reached toward the page.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Stopped.
Narrator: Then went to the basin instead.
Narrator: A little water remained in the bottom from the night before. He dipped one finger into it and touched the edge of the final line.
Narrator: The ink did not run.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The water did.
Narrator: It gathered into a bead against the page and rolled away without darkening the parchment.
Narrator: Zeek wiped his finger on his tunic.
Zik: [flatly] Fine.
Narrator: The journal offered no opinion.
Narrator: He took the bronze awl from his pack and pressed its tip lightly into the margin beside the writing. The point left a pale indentation. The paper behaved like old paper.
Narrator: Only the ink refused.
Narrator: He turned back one page.
Narrator: Supply figures. A note about a cracked axle. His father's complaint that a guide had watered the beer and lied badly about it.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The next page held a rough map of a canal settlement abandoned before Zeek was born.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Nothing unusual.
Narrator: He returned to the final page.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The message waited.
Bel-iddin: [quietly] Zee-keer, if you are reading this, the seal is failing.
Narrator: He read the words again, slower this time.
Narrator: His father rarely used his full name. Only when angry. Or frightened. Or when he needed Zeek to understand that the next thing mattered more than whatever came before it.
Narrator: Zeek pulled a stick of charcoal from the writing case and copied the message onto a scrap of parchment.
Bel-iddin: [quietly] The First City is not a tomb.
Bel-iddin: [quietly] It is a lid.
Narrator: His hand moved steadily until he reached the next line.
Bel-iddin: [quietly] Do not trust the symbol on your skin.
Bel-iddin: [quietly] It was never meant for you.
Narrator: The charcoal snapped between his fingers.
Narrator: He stared at the broken piece.
Zik: [bitterly] Convenient.
Narrator: The journal remained silent.
Zik: [bitterly] You disappear for twelve years, and now you develop an interest in instructions.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Nothing.
Narrator: He brushed the charcoal dust from the parchment and compared the copy to the page. The handwriting was unmistakable. The narrow wedge of the first stroke. The way his father crowded letters together when his thoughts moved faster than his hand.
Narrator: Zeek had copied that hand hundreds of times as a boy.
Narrator: He had forged it twice.
Narrator: Once to avoid a lesson. Once to gain access to an excavation inventory he had been forbidden to read.
Narrator: His father had caught both attempts.
Narrator: No one else had.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek bent closer.
Narrator: There, beneath the word seal, a tiny hook curled backward from the final stroke. His father had broken the bone beneath his smallest finger years before Zeek was born. The injury had never healed straight. Every time he wrote too quickly, the hand dragged at the end of certain letters.
Narrator: A forger could imitate the shape.
Narrator: They could not know why it existed.
Narrator: Zeek sat on the edge of the pallet.
Narrator: The mark beneath the linen warmed.
Narrator: He ignored it and turned to the inside cover, where his father had written his name on the day he purchased the journal.
Bel-iddin: [dryly] BAYL-ID-din, son of NAH-rahm, surveyor by training, liar by necessity.
Narrator: Zeek had always assumed the last part was a joke.
Narrator: This morning it looked more like a warning.
Narrator: He returned to the message and pressed his thumb against the dry margin beside it.
Zik: [whispers] What are you?
Narrator: The ink trembled.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not much.
Narrator: A faint tightening of the surface, like black water disturbed by distant rain.
Narrator: Zeek jerked his hand away.
Narrator: The words remained unchanged.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He waited.
Narrator: A new dot appeared beneath the final line.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Then another.
Narrator: Ink gathered slowly between them, joining into the beginning of a stroke.
Narrator: Zeek did not breathe.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The line lengthened.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Stopped.
Narrator: The wet ink drew itself upward into a single crooked mark.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not a letter.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: A direction.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Down.
Narrator: The mark on his palm answered with one hard pulse.
Narrator: Zeek slammed the journal shut.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: For several seconds he sat with both hands pressed against the cover, as if whatever had written inside might force the book open again.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Nothing moved.
Narrator: Outside, a cart wheel struck a broken stone. Someone cursed. A vendor began calling out the price of onions.
Narrator: The ordinary city went on pretending nothing beneath it had spoken.
Narrator: Zeek pulled back his sleeve. The spiral-drop mark was warm beneath his touch—not feverish, not inflamed, warm in a way that felt deliberate.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: It pulsed once.
Narrator: He shut the journal inside his satchel and began packing.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: By mid-morning he had gathered the others in the shaded courtyard of a cheap inn that asked no questions. The air smelled of damp stone, roasting spices, and old beer. A fountain in the center had long since stopped working, though someone had left a small clay cup beside it as if expecting the water to return.
Narrator: BAH-lahg sat on the edge of the fountain with his spear across his knees, wearing the look of a man who had slept badly and intended to blame everyone else. En-KHEH-doo leaned against a pillar, sharpening her knife with slow strokes. She had not asked why he summoned them, which meant she already knew something was wrong.
Narrator: NEEN-soon sat on a low stone wall with a bundle of scrolls tied beside her. Ink and stone dust stained her fingers.
Narrator: Zeek studied their faces and felt the first real unease about memory itself. These were people he had chosen. He knew their voices, their habits. Yet the City had already shown him that perception could be bent.
Narrator: He was no longer certain which parts of them he actually knew.
Ninsun: [uneasy] The City is quiet today,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Narrator: She kept her eyes on the shifting skyline.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] Usually the morning fog brings a change. A new passage. A building that wasn't there the night before. Today everything feels static.
Zik: [dryly] Static is good,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Zik: It means we know where we are.
