CHAPTER 14 — THE MAN WHO ALREADY KNEW

Narrator: CHAPTER 14
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Man Who Already Knew
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The Collector found them before they finished packing for the east-side survey.
Narrator: He was waiting in the narrow lane behind the cheap room, leaning against the wall like a man who had all morning and no intention of wasting it on politeness. Two temple guards stood a short distance away, looking bored in the specific way of men paid to look dangerous. The Collector himself looked almost regretful. That bothered Zeek more than anger would have.
The Collector: [smoothly] You've been busy,
Narrator: the Collector said.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's hand drifted toward the haft of his axe and then stopped. En-KHEH-doo's expression went carefully blank. NEEN-soon simply watched, stylus still in her fingers from the map she had been updating.
Narrator: Zeek set the pack down.
Zik: [guarded] We're taking a paying job. East side. Walls that need measuring. Nothing that should interest you.
The Collector: [smoothly] Everything you do interests me lately.
Narrator: The Collector's eyes moved across the four of them, lingering a fraction too long on the linen around Zeek's left hand and the satchel where NEEN-soon kept her scraps.
The Collector: [smoothly] Notches in walls that officially don't exist. Questions about blank tablets. A certain district that keeps appearing on no one's maps but yours. You've drawn attention.
Balag: [gruffly] From who?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Narrator: The Collector almost smiled.
The Collector: [amused] From people who prefer their attention not to be noticed in return.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo tilted her head.
Enhedu: [shrewdly] You're not here to buy.
The Collector: [smoothly] No.
Narrator: He looked at Zeek.
The Collector: [gravely] I'm here because your father made the same mistake you're making now. He thought the City was a puzzle that wanted to be solved. It isn't. It's a lid. And lids exist to keep something from rising. The more you mark it, the more you tell it exactly where the curious ones are standing.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice was quiet.
Ninsun: [quietly] You know about the marks.
The Collector: [gravely] I know about the ones who made them before you. Most of them stopped. A few didn't. The ones who didn't are why certain drawers in the lower archives stay locked.
Narrator: He glanced at the two guards, then back.
The Collector: [smoothly] I'm offering you the same chance I offered your father. Walk away from the shifted streets. Take the east-side work. Take the silver. Let the lid stay closed.
Narrator: Zeek held his stare.
Zik: [defiantly] And if we don't?
Narrator: The Collector's expression didn't change.
The Collector: [coldly] Then you'll learn, the same way he did, that some prices are paid in advance. And some debts collect themselves.
Narrator: He pushed off the wall, adjusted the sleeve of his robe, and walked away. The guards fell in behind him without being told. At the end of the lane he paused, just long enough to look back once.
The Collector: [softly] Your father finally understood he had been asking the wrong question,
Narrator: he said.
The Collector: [dryly] I hope you're smarter than he was. Or luckier. I'm not certain which one matters more.
Narrator: Then he was gone.
Narrator: The lane felt smaller after he left.
Narrator: BAH-lahg exhaled through his nose.
Balag: [annoyed] I fucking hate that man.
Enhedu: [thoughtful] He's not wrong about the attention,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said. She was already recalculating something behind her eyes.
Enhedu: [shrewdly] We've been loud in places that prefer quiet.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked down at the map still half-open in her hands. The boxes labeled CLAIMED, COST, and LIMIT sat in a tight cluster near the tanners' row.
Ninsun: [intense] He knew about the marks. He knew about the ones who came before us. That means the locked drawers have names in them. Or the absence of names.
Narrator: Zeek picked up the pack. The journal inside it had not been opened in nine days. The decision still felt like the only clean thing he owned.
Zik: [firmly] We take the east-side job,
Narrator: he said.
Zik: [firmly] We get paid. We watch who watches us. And we don't cut another notch until we know whether the Collector is warning us or herding us.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one argued.
Narrator: They walked out of the lane in the order that had become habit. Behind them the cheap room and the map and the accumulating scraps of evidence waited like a second set of lungs the city had not yet decided to collapse.
Narrator: Ahead, the ordinary noise of OO-rook continued—merchants, animals, arguments that had nothing to do with lids or locked drawers or the kind of debts that collected themselves.
Narrator: Zeek flexed his left hand inside the linen. The mark was warm.
Narrator: He did not look back.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The east-side walls were exactly as boring as the contract promised.
Narrator: Three hundred cubits of mud-brick curtain wall, two collapsed towers, and a stretch of foundation that had been patched so many times the original line was anybody's guess. The temple clerk who hired them wanted measurements, load assessments, and a written recommendation on whether the whole section needed tearing down before the next flood season. Nothing shifted. Nothing breathed cooler air. Nothing carried notches.
Narrator: It was the most restful work they had done in weeks.
Narrator: BAH-lahg walked the foundation with a measuring rod and a piece of chalk, marking every place the brick had settled more than a finger's width. En-KHEH-doo counted and sorted the reusable stone the way other people counted coin. NEEN-soon copied the older survey marks still visible on the inner face—temple codes, old ownership seals, the usual bureaucratic scars. Zeek checked the places where later repairs had been cut into earlier ones, reading the sequence the way his father had taught him to read mortar.
Narrator: He paused halfway along the inner wall.
Narrator: One repair interrupted another.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Then another.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Three different masons.
Narrator: Three different clay mixes.
Narrator: The oldest repair had been careful. Tight joints. Fired brick cut to match the original courses.
Narrator: The second had simply filled cracks.
Narrator: The newest had hidden both.
Narrator: BAH-lahg noticed him stop.
Balag: [curious] What?
Zik: [thoughtful] Someone kept fixing the same place.
Balag: [curious] Bad foundation?
Zik: No.
Narrator: Zeek brushed away another layer of dust.
Zik: [thoughtful] If it were structural, they'd replace the whole course.
Balag: [skeptical] So?
Zik: [intrigued] They only repaired this section.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo joined them.
Enhedu: [curious] Meaning?
Zik: [quietly] They weren't trying to save the wall.
Narrator: He traced the line of repairs with one finger.
Zik: [intrigued] They were trying to hide something inside it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at the perfectly ordinary brickwork.
Balag: [skeptical] You can tell that from mud?
Zik: [dryly] I can tell someone paid for three repairs where one would've done.
Narrator: He smiled faintly.
Zik: [amused] People rarely waste labor without a reason.
Narrator: For most of the morning no one spoke about lids or locked drawers or the Collector's quiet threat. The ordinary work was enough.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Near midday NEEN-soon stopped in front of a section of inner wall that looked no different from the rest. She ran her fingers along a line of faded wedge marks, then crouched and brushed dust from a deeper cut near the base.
Narrator: NEEN-soon crouched beside the repaired section.
Ninsun: [urgently] Zeek.
Narrator: He came over. The others followed without being called.
Ninsun: [whispers] [excited] There's writing here.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked.
Balag: [skeptical] I don't see anything.
Ninsun: [quietly] You aren't meant to.
Narrator: She dampened one corner of her sleeve and wiped gently across the brick.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The dust disappeared.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Nothing else happened.
Narrator: Then sunlight shifted behind a passing cloud.
Narrator: The shallow cuts suddenly caught the light.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo sucked in a breath.
Enhedu: [breathless] I see it.
Narrator: Barely.
Narrator: The survey marks had been cut so shallow they disappeared unless viewed from exactly the right angle.
Narrator: Zeek knelt beside them.
Zik: [intently] They expected later repairs.
Balag: [puzzled] What?
Zik: [intently] They carved them after the wall was finished.
Narrator: He pointed.
Zik: [intently] If these were construction marks, they'd be beneath this plaster.
Narrator: Instead they sat on top of it.
Zik: [quietly] They wanted someone to erase them.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned.
Balag: [skeptical] That's a strange thing to want.
Ninsun: [quietly] No.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice was almost a whisper.
Ninsun: [whispers] It's a very ordinary thing.
Narrator: They all looked at her.
Ninsun: [quietly] History is easier to hide when you know exactly where someone will try.
Narrator: She continued cleaning.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Slowly.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: One wedge.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Another.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Then the spiral.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not scratched.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not painted.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Deliberately carved.
Narrator: Then attacked with a chisel.
Narrator: The damage had broken the symbol but not destroyed it.
Narrator: Whoever erased it had worked angrily.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Or hurriedly.
Narrator: Zeek touched the deepest cut.
Zik: [quietly] They came back.
Balag: [curious] How do you know?
Zik: [intently] The scoring crosses weathering.
Narrator: He looked up.
Zik: [quietly] The wall aged.
Ninsun: [uneasy] Then somebody returned years later just to erase this.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked around the empty survey site.
Balag: [grumbling] I dislike the amount of effort strangers keep spending on old bricks.
Narrator: The marks were old temple survey code, the kind used for private boundary work rather than public walls. Most of it was routine. One cluster near the bottom was not. The same spiral-drop that lived under the linen on Zeek's hand had been cut into the brick and then deliberately defaced—scored through so hard the chisel had split the face of the course.
Narrator: Beside it, in a tighter hand, three signs remained legible.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice had gone flat.
Ninsun: [flatly] That's the same seal language as the broken piece we pulled from the shifted chamber. And the name under it—
Narrator: She stopped, then finished quietly.
Ninsun: [softly] It's the uncrossed name. The one from the basin. The ordinary one. The potter or the dock worker or whoever the city decided wasn't worth erasing completely.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stared at the defaced symbol.
Balag: [uneasy] So the same mark shows up on a royal tomb, a shifted street, and a temple curtain wall that officially has nothing to do with either.
Enhedu: [grimly] And on an ordinary person's name the city only half-deleted,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said. She was already looking at the surrounding courses for anything else worth taking.
Enhedu: [grimly] That's not coincidence. That's inventory.
Narrator: Zeek ran his thumb across the scored spiral. The edges were old. Whoever had tried to erase it had done the work years ago, maybe decades. The wall had simply kept the evidence the way walls kept everything else—without comment, without loyalty, without any interest in whether the people who came later were ready to see it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon was already copying the cluster onto a fresh scrap, her handwriting smaller and tighter than usual.
Ninsun: [intense] The Collector said the locked drawers hold the ones who didn't stop. What if they also hold the names the city tried to cross out and couldn't quite finish?
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one answered.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They finished the survey because the contract required it and because leaving early would have told anyone watching that they had found something worth finding. BAH-lahg completed his measurements. En-KHEH-doo stacked the reusable stone. Zeek wrote the load assessment in the dry, practical language the temple clerks preferred. NEEN-soon kept the new copy of the defaced seal and the half-erased name folded inside her map, separate from the rest.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: By late afternoon the work was done. The clerk paid them in full and did not ask why they had spent so long on one short section of wall. They walked back toward the lower districts in the order that had become habit, four people carrying an ordinary day's pay and one more piece of proof that the city's erasures were never as complete as it wanted them to be.
Narrator: Zeek flexed his left hand beneath the linen.
Narrator: The mark remained quiet.
Narrator: Not because nothing had happened.
Narrator: Because whatever lay beneath the city no longer needed to call him every day.
Narrator: It already knew where he was going.
Narrator: He thought about the Collector.
Narrator: About the locked drawers.
Narrator: About the wall someone had repaired three separate times simply to hide one damaged symbol and one ordinary name.
Narrator: The Collector had said his father eventually learned he had been asking the wrong question.
Narrator: Walking home through the evening crowds, Zeek realized he had never asked what that question had been.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: For the first time since opening the tomb, that frightened him more than the answer.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 15 — THE LEDGER’S MARGIN

Narrator: CHAPTER 15
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Ledger's Margin
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The walk back from the east gate took longer than the morning's survey. The rain that had threatened the lower districts since noon finally broke, not in a clean downpour, but in a thin, greasy mist that turned the dust in the unpaved alleys into gray paste.
Narrator: BAH-lahg pulled his wool cloak tighter over his shoulders, his boots making a wet, sucking sound in the lane.
Balag: [grumbling] If it keeps up like this, the foundations on the south quarter are going to float before we finish the month.
Enhedu: [dryly] They aren't going to float,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said, stepping carefully around a broken drainage tile.
Enhedu: [shrewdly] They're going to settle. Which means the temple will have to pay twice as much for shoring next season. The water's just doing the math for them.
Narrator: NEEN-soon walked between them, her head down, holding her satchel tight against her chest to keep the damp off the paper. Her boots were caked in the same commercial mortar dust Zeek had noticed earlier, the gray residue that didn't match the temple mix.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They reached the room without running into the Collector or anyone else who looked like they had business with them.
Narrator: BAH-lahg dropped the survey rods by the door, unlatched the shutter to let the stale air out, and dropped onto his bench without lighting the lamp. The room went gray as the daylight faded through the unglazed window frame.
Narrator: NEEN-soon didn't sit. She laid her map out on the pine table, smoothing the curled edges of the parchment with the flat of her hand. The four boxes—CLAIMED, COST, LIMIT, and the empty space she'd left after the east wall—were barely visible in the dim light.
Narrator: She picked up her stylus, hovered the metal point over the blank space beside the east-side survey mark, and then stopped.
Narrator: Zeek set his pack down by the threshold. He didn't take anything out.
Zik: [quietly] You're not marking it,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon kept the stylus steady, though her hand cast a long, shaky shadow across the parchment under the gray light from the window.
Ninsun: [quietly] I don't have a name for this box yet.
Enhedu: [dryly] Call it what it is,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said, leaning against the doorframe while she wrung the mist from the fringe of her shawl.
Enhedu: [flatly] Inventory.
Narrator: BAH-lahg let out a short, rough breath that sounded almost like a laugh.
Balag: [laughs] The city's keeping score. We're just writing down the numbers.
Narrator: Zeek walked over to the table and looked down at the map. The defaced spiral-drop NEEN-soon had drawn in the corner sat right on the boundary line between the east wall and the lower tanneries, as if the same crack ran underneath both of them without caring about the distance.
Narrator: He reached out, took the small ceramic ink-pot by its neck, and moved it two inches to the left, covering the empty space entirely.
Zik: [firmly] Leave it blank,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked up at him, her eyes dark in the failing light. For a second she looked like she wanted to argue, to reach for the stylus and scratch the word down anyway before the dark took the room. Then she slowly lowered her hand, the metal point resting against the grain of the wood beside the paper.
Ninsun: [sharply] We can't leave it blank,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said, her voice dropping into a sharper, harder register.
Ninsun: [intense] Not after what we saw on the curtain wall. The temple survey codes don't match the commercial stock. That means the records were altered twice—once when the wall went up, and again when they tried to scrape the name out.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo pushed off the doorframe, her interest suddenly sharp.
Enhedu: [intrigued] You're saying the east-side survey isn't the whole record.
Ninsun: [intense] I'm saying the public registry only shows what the temple wanted kept after the second consolidation,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said, tapping the edge of her uninked stylus against the table.
Ninsun: [intense] If we want to know whose name was under that defaced seal, we aren't going to find it on the outside of the wall.
Balag: [curious] Where, then?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Ninsun: [firmly] The lower archive branch behind the third district weigh-station,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Ninsun: [firmly] The old tax ledgers from before the plague years. The ones the Collector said stay locked.
Narrator: Zeek looked at the ink-pot covering the empty box on the map. He didn't reach for his pack. He didn't look for an excuse to wait.
Zik: [decisively] Grab the lantern,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Zik: [firmly] If we get to the weigh-station before the evening shift changes, the night clerk won't check the seals on the side bins.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stood up, his boots hitting the floorboards with a dull, heavy jar, and reached for his spear. En-KHEH-doo was already sliding her ledger into her pack.
Narrator: NEEN-soon picked up her stylus, tucked it behind her ear, and rolled the map tight.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They left the room without looking back.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The rain had thickened by the time they reached the third district weigh-station.
Narrator: It stood between a grain warehouse and a public cistern, a low administrative building with a roof pitched just enough to send runoff into the alley. Water streamed from the carved mouths of two clay lions above the entrance. Beneath them, porters waited with wet sacks over their shoulders while a clerk shouted weights through an open window.
Narrator: The front doors were still busy.
Narrator: The side entrance was not.
Narrator: Zeek led them around the rear wall, where broken amphorae and discarded tally sticks had collected beneath the eaves. A narrow wooden door sat between two storage bins. Its temple seal had been pressed into a lump of clay at shoulder height.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at the seal.
Balag: [whispers] [skeptical] You said the night clerk wouldn't check it.
Zik: [whispers] He won't.
Balag: [whispers] [skeptical] It's still sealed.
Zik: [whispers] Yes.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stared at him.
Balag: [whispers] [dryly] I'm beginning to suspect your plans rely heavily on other people being worse at their jobs than you are.
Zik: [whispers] [dryly] They usually are.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo leaned close to the seal.
Enhedu: [whispers] Fresh clay.
Ninsun: [whispers] Replaced this morning,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Enhedu: [whispers] How do you know?
Ninsun: [whispers] The impression is sharp, but the edges have started taking damp. Four hours. Six at most.
Narrator: BAH-lahg lowered his voice.
Balag: [whispers] [impatient] That helps us how?
Narrator: NEEN-soon removed a small wrapped packet from her satchel.
Narrator: Inside lay three clay seal impressions.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked at her.
Enhedu: [whispers] [surprised] You brought copies.
Ninsun: [whispers] [dryly] I brought possibilities.
Enhedu: [whispers] [disapproving] You copied temple seals.
Ninsun: [whispers] [primly] I copied errors made by temple seals.
Enhedu: [whispers] [dryly] That answer would not comfort a magistrate.
Ninsun: [whispers] [dryly] It wasn't prepared for one.
Narrator: She selected the closest impression and handed it to Zeek.
Narrator: The copied seal matched the clay on the door except for a tiny break in the outer ring.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek examined both.
Zik: [whispers] This one is older.
Ninsun: [whispers] [dryly] By three administrators.
Zik: [whispers] And they still use it?
Ninsun: [whispers] [dryly] The third district is not where competent officials are promoted.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked toward the front of the building, where another weight was shouted through the rain.
Balag: [whispers] [dryly] At last. A weakness in government we can depend on.
Narrator: Zeek drew his knife.
Narrator: He did not cut through the clay. That would leave an obvious break. Instead, he slid the blade beneath the lump and worked it slowly away from the wood until the entire seal came loose in one piece.
Narrator: The door opened inward.
Narrator: The room beyond smelled of dust, wet rope, and old grain.
Narrator: They entered single file.
Narrator: Zeek pressed the original seal back into place from the inside. It would not survive close inspection, but it might survive indifference.
Narrator: BAH-lahg watched him finish.
Balag: [whispers] [curious] You've done that before.
Zik: [whispers] [evasive] My father disliked locked rooms.
Balag: [whispers] [dryly] That is not the same answer.
Zik: [whispers] [dryly] It's the one you're getting.
Narrator: The storage room held cracked measuring jars, sacks of outdated weights, and stacks of waxed tally boards tied with cord. Rain drummed against the roof. Voices from the public chamber reached them through the inner wall, flattened into meaningless sound.
Narrator: NEEN-soon moved to the far shelves.
Ninsun: [whispers] The lower ledgers won't be stored with current accounts.
Enhedu: [whispers] Why?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Ninsun: [whispers] Current records are useful. Old ones are dangerous.
Narrator: BAH-lahg lifted a sack and looked behind it.
Balag: [whispers] [dryly] Useful distinction.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They found the archive door at floor level behind a stack of broken scales.
Narrator: It was smaller than Zeek expected. A clerk would have to stoop to enter. The bronze lock had been polished recently, though dust covered the surrounding bricks.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo crouched beside it.
Enhedu: [whispers] This gets opened.
Narrator: NEEN-soon nodded.
Ninsun: [whispers] The Collector said the drawers stay locked. He didn't say abandoned.
Narrator: Zeek looked at the lock.
Zik: [whispers] No scratches around the keyway.
Balag: [whispers] So whoever opens it has the key,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Zik: [whispers] Or doesn't use the keyway.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo pressed her thumb against the lower hinge.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The pin shifted.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: She smiled slightly.
Enhedu: [whispers] [amused] The hinge was installed backward.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at Zeek.
Balag: [whispers] [dryly] Other people being worse at their jobs again?
Zik: [whispers] [dryly] This time we owe them thanks.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo worked the pin loose with the tip of her knife. BAH-lahg caught the door before it dropped.
Narrator: The space behind it descended three narrow steps into darkness.
Narrator: Cool air breathed upward.
Narrator: Zeek felt the mark beneath the linen warm.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not pulse.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Recognize.
Narrator: NEEN-soon saw him flex his hand.
Ninsun: [whispers] [knowingly] You felt something.
Zik: [whispers] No.
Ninsun: [whispers] [dryly] You're a poor liar.
Zik: [whispers] [dryly] I'm an economical one.
Narrator: They went down.
Narrator: The archive chamber was long and low, its ceiling supported by cedar beams blackened with age. Clay tablets filled wooden pigeonholes from floor to ceiling. Some were stacked openly. Others sat inside sealed bins marked with year signs and district codes.
Narrator: At the far end stood seven narrow drawers fitted into the wall.
Narrator: Each had a bronze faceplate.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Each was locked.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon stopped.
Ninsun: [whispers] [uneasy] The Collector was being literal.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked back toward the steps.
Balag: [whispers] [impatient] Can we be literal quickly?
Narrator: NEEN-soon moved among the shelves, reading labels under her breath.
Ninsun: [whispers] Pre-plague property levy. Grain exemptions. Inheritance disputes. Boundary tax…
Narrator: She stopped at a row of tablets bundled in rotted linen.
Ninsun: [whispers] [excited] Here.
Narrator: The tag read:
Narrator: [slowly] EAST CURTAIN DISTRICT—PRIVATE HOLDINGS BEFORE CONSOLIDATION
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo took the lamp while NEEN-soon untied the cord.
Narrator: The first tablet listed three warehouse owners.
Narrator: The second recorded a dyer's yard absorbed into temple property.
Narrator: The third had been scraped smooth and reused for grain tallies.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon's jaw tightened.
Ninsun: [whispers] [tense] They overwrote it.
Zik: [whispers] Can you recover anything?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
Ninsun: [whispers] Not without daylight and hours.
Zik: [whispers] We have neither.
Narrator: She moved to the next.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Then the next.
Narrator: The records changed halfway through the bundle.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Names vanished.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Plots became numbers.
Narrator: Private holdings became temple allocations without purchase sums or witness seals.

Ninsun: [whispers] [dryly] They regularized it,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Enhedu: [whispers] [bitterly] That is the same thing with a cleaner robe.

Balag: [whispers] [urgently] Find the name.

Narrator: Her fingers moved faster.

Narrator: Then stopped.

Narrator: she said.

[pause 0.5s]

Narrator: he said.

Narrator: NEEN-soon added.

Balag: [whispers] What happened to him?

Narrator: The reverse had been scraped.

Narrator: Not cleanly.

CHAPTER 16 — THE SEVENTH DRAWER

Narrator: CHAPTER 16
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Seventh Drawer
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The lock turned.
Narrator: BAH-lahg shifted in front of NEEN-soon, placing his body between her and the archive door. In the dark, the movement was nearly silent. Only the leather wrapping on his spear gave a faint creak beneath his grip.
Narrator: NEEN-soon held Ahm-MAH-roo's ledger tablet against her chest.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo stood beside the lowest shelf with her knife angled down along her forearm, hidden from anyone entering above. Zeek remained near the foot of the steps, close enough to see the thin line of light beneath the door.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The key withdrew.
Narrator: The door did not open.
Narrator: Rain drummed against the roof of the weigh-station. The sound came through the ceiling beams and packed earth as a low, continuous tapping, like fingers spread across the entire building.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: For several breaths, nothing happened.
Narrator: Then a man spoke from the other side.
Mardu: [calmly] You have taken the wrong tablet.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked toward Zeek.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek said nothing.
Narrator: The voice was old. Not weak. Worn smooth by repetition.
Mardu: [dryly] The grain exemptions are two shelves to your right,
Narrator: the man continued.

Narrator: NEEN-soon's fingers tightened around the clay.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo raised her voice just enough to carry through the door.

Narrator: The bolt slid back.
Narrator: The door opened inward.
Narrator: Lamplight spilled down the three narrow steps and spread across the archive floor in a weak amber wedge.
Narrator: A single man stood above them.
Narrator: He wore the undyed robe of a lower archive custodian. The cloth hung loosely from narrow shoulders bent forward by years spent over tables. Ink stained the first two fingers of his right hand all the way to the nails. His hair had gone almost entirely white, though his eyebrows remained dark and heavy over deep-set eyes.
Narrator: No guard stood behind him.
Narrator: He looked first at BAH-lahg's spear.

Mardu: [quietly] Ahm-MAH-roo,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon did not lower it.
Ninsun: [tense] You know the name.

Narrator: he said.

Narrator: Zeek asked.
Narrator: The custodian's mouth tightened.

Ninsun: [intense] This says he kept the lower cistern stair.

Narrator: She glanced down.
Narrator: The writing along the margin had softened.
Narrator: At first Zeek thought rain had found its way into the chamber, but the tablet was dry. The old clay had begun to darken around the smallest wedges. Their edges rounded before his eyes.

Narrator: NEEN-soon inhaled sharply.
Ninsun: [horrified] No.

Narrator: The first line contracted into itself.
Narrator: [slowly] AMMARU, SON OF DAGAN-ILI.

Narrator: NEEN-soon scraped at one sign with her nail.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The clay yielded.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Fresh.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Soft.

Mardu: [calmly] Nothing.

Narrator: [slowly] POTTER BY TRADE. KEEPER OF THE LOWER CISTERN STAIR BY HEREDITARY OBLIGATION.

Narrator: She pulled her copied scrap from inside her robe and unfolded it with one hand.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Her ink remained.
Mardu: [quietly] Your copy will last longer,
Narrator: the custodian said.
Ninsun: [urgently] How much longer?
Narrator: she asked.
Mardu: [quietly] I don't know.

Narrator: [slowly] PROPERTY EXEMPT FROM LEVY WHILE SEVENTH ACCESS REMAINS SEALED.

Narrator: The wedges shuddered.
Narrator: Zeek saw it clearly.
Narrator: Not the whole tablet.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Only the writing.
Narrator: The signs trembled in place like insects pinned to the clay.

Narrator: The tablet became blank except for the ordinary tax figures on its face.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stared at it.
Narrator: The custodian watched her copied scrap.

Narrator: he said.

Narrator: NEEN-soon asked.
Mardu: [quietly] No.
Narrator: His eyes moved toward the seven narrow drawers fitted into the far wall.

Narrator: A heavy tread crossed the storage room above them.
Narrator: The custodian's head turned slightly.
Narrator: Someone dragged one of the broken scales aside.
[pause 0.5s]

Narrator: Another voice called,
Archive Guard: [shouts] MAR-doo?

CHAPTER 17 — THE MAP THAT SHOULD NOT EXIST

Narrator: CHAPTER 17
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Map That Should Not Exist
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They met at dusk in the shaded courtyard of the inn, long after the heat had bled from the stone.
Narrator: Zeek set the satchel on the table.
Narrator: For ten days he had not opened the journal.
Narrator: Nine days had become ten that morning. The refusal had felt precise. Deliberate. A clean edge drawn between himself and whatever had begun writing in his father's hand.
Narrator: Now Ahm-MAH-roo's words were disappearing from clay.
Narrator: His father's name had waited in a drawer older than his birth.
Narrator: And the journal had followed them through a drain where water climbed uphill.
Narrator: Zeek unfastened the satchel.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon leaned closer.
Ninsun: [sharply] Do not touch anything until we understand what changed.
Zik: [dryly] You were the one asking me to open it.
Ninsun: [primly] That is not permission to become careless.
Zik: [dryly] You have an interesting relationship with consistency.
Ninsun: [primly] I am consistent about other people being cautious.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo rested one ankle on her opposite knee.
Enhedu: [impatient] Open the book before it finds another way to complain.
Narrator: Zeek reached inside.
Narrator: The journal was warm.
Narrator: Not warmed by his body.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Warmer.
Narrator: The cover flexed beneath his fingers.
Narrator: He pulled it out and placed it on the table.
Narrator: No one reached for it.
Narrator: Rain whispered against the courtyard canopy. Beyond the wall, someone laughed, the sound muffled by wet brick. The journal sat in the middle of the table as though it belonged there.
Narrator: BAH-lahg folded his arms.
Balag: [impatient] Well?
Narrator: Zeek looked at the cover.
Zik: [quietly] Nothing happened before.
Enhedu: [sarcastic] That's comforting,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Enhedu: [dryly] Most terrible ideas begin with those words.
Narrator: NEEN-soon leaned closer without touching it.
Ninsun: [intently] The leather changed.
Zik: [skeptical] It was always dark.
Ninsun: [firmly] No.
Narrator: She pointed toward the spine.
Ninsun: [intently] The surface has stretched.
Narrator: Zeek frowned.
Zik: [skeptical] I don't see—
Ninsun: [intently] The stitching.
Narrator: He looked again.
Narrator: The thread along the spine no longer sat perfectly straight. It rose and settled almost imperceptibly, as though something beneath the cover had shifted.
Narrator: BAH-lahg noticed it too.
Balag: [uneasy] I don't like that.
Enhedu: [dryly] You never like anything,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Balag: [grumbling] I like walls.
Enhedu: [dryly] This one may become one again.
Narrator: Zeek rested two fingers against the cover.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Warm.
Narrator: Not the warmth of a book carried beneath a cloak.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Something deeper.
Narrator: He slipped a thumb beneath the edge of the leather.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The cover resisted.
Narrator: Then, before he could lift it—
Narrator: The journal opened by itself.
Narrator: Its front cover lifted.
Narrator: Pages turned in a rapid dry flutter, though there was no wind beneath the canopy. Blank leaves flashed past. Old calculations. BAYL-ID-din's survey notes. A fragment about the western tomb. Lists of names Zeek had not yet studied.
Narrator: The pages stopped near the middle.
Narrator: Across two facing sheets, wet ink spread from the binding.
Narrator: No pen touched it.
Narrator: Lines extended outward in thin black threads.
Narrator: NEEN-soon bent over the table.
Narrator: Her fingers hovered inches above the parchment.
Ninsun: [whispers] [awed] This is not possible.
Zik: [dryly] Welcome to my life,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: The map continued drawing itself.
Narrator: At first, the strokes formed the broad shape of OO-rook.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The river's bend.
Narrator: The east curtain wall.
Narrator: The temple district raised above the lower streets.
Narrator: Old roads disappeared beneath newer ones. Abandoned settlements clustered beyond the walls like broken teeth in the earth.
Narrator: Then the lines descended.
Narrator: Ink bled downward from the familiar geography, creating shafts, chambers, stairs, and inverted towers beneath the city. Some structures hung below others like roots from an overturned tree. Others occupied spaces that could not exist together, overlapping without crossing.
Narrator: NEEN-soon moved around the table.
Ninsun: [intently] It is not a surface plan.
Narrator: Zeek leaned closer.
Zik: [dismissively] Old surveyors liked details.
Narrator: She frowned.
Ninsun: [skeptical] No.
Narrator: Zeek looked up.
Zik: [surprised] No?
Ninsun: [thoughtful] I answered too quickly.
Narrator: She pulled one of her own tablets closer and began sketching the outline beside the journal.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] If this were a city plan…
Narrator: She drew a rectangle.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] …the temple should sit here.
Narrator: A second mark.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] The river…
Narrator: Another line.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] The gate…
Narrator: She stopped.
Narrator: Her stylus hovered above the clay.
Ninsun: [frustrated] It doesn't work.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo leaned over.
Enhedu: [dryly] Perhaps whoever drew it was a poor surveyor.
Ninsun: [firmly] No.
Narrator: NEEN-soon tapped the journal.
Ninsun: [intently] The measurements are too consistent.
Enhedu: [impatient] So what's wrong?
Ninsun: [quietly] We are.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence settled over the table.
Narrator: NEEN-soon turned the journal ninety degrees.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Then another ninety.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Nothing changed.
Narrator: She looked at the drawing one last time.
Narrator: Her eyes widened slightly.
Ninsun: [realizing] It isn't meant to be viewed from above.
Narrator: She turned to Zeek.
Ninsun: [excited] It's a section.
Narrator: The ink river turned downward in a wide spiral. At three points, channels split from it and entered separate levels beneath the city.
Ninsun: [intently] This shows depth.
Zik: [curious] How much?
Ninsun: [quietly] I don't know.
Zik: [skeptical] You just called it a section.
Ninsun: [intently] A section requires a scale. This has none.
Narrator: She traced the outline of the east-side wall.
Narrator: A line descended from the defaced spiral-drop they had copied that morning. It passed through two buried chambers before joining a circular structure beneath the tanners' row.
Narrator: The boxes NEEN-soon had drawn on her map—CLAIMED, COST, LIMIT—corresponded to small black marks along the descending line.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo leaned forward.
Enhedu: [uneasy] It has our passages.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's face hardened.
Ninsun: [tense] It has more than our passages.
Narrator: Below each known shifted street, the map showed additional routes.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Closed corridors.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Collapsed stairs.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Rooms beneath rooms.
Narrator: Some paths ended in black circles. Others joined a large spiral that continued below the bottom edge of the page.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked over from the courtyard entrance.
Balag: [uneasy] Does it show a way out?
Enhedu: [ominous] It shows too many ways in,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon leaned nearer to the page.
Ninsun: [intently] Hold the lamp behind it.
Narrator: Zeek lifted the courtyard lamp.
Narrator: The light did not reflect from the parchment.
Narrator: It sank into it.
Narrator: The sheet became faintly translucent. Dark fibers appeared inside the material, branching beneath the surface in irregular patterns. They were thicker near the binding and finer toward the edges.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon stopped moving.
Zik: [curious] What?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
Narrator: She pointed to a pale ridge crossing the lower corner of the right-hand page.
Ninsun: [intently] Turn it slightly.
Narrator: He tilted the lamp.
Narrator: The ridge became clearer.
Narrator: It forked once before ending beneath a cluster of newly drawn stairs.
Enhedu: [curious] Damage?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Ninsun: [quietly] No.
Narrator: NEEN-soon lowered herself until her eyes were level with the page.
Ninsun: [intently] The tissue thickened along both sides.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned.
Balag: [skeptical] You can tell that?
Ninsun: [intently] Parchment damage splits fibers. This closed before the material was cured.
Narrator: She looked at Zeek.
Ninsun: [quietly] [shaken] It healed.
Narrator: The musician in the alley played the same three notes again.
Narrator: The last one failed entirely.
Narrator: NEEN-soon examined the dark points scattered beneath the ink.
Ninsun: [quietly] Follicles,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: Zeek looked at the page.
Zik: [quietly] What kind?
Ninsun: [uneasy] Too sparse for goat. Too irregular for sheep.
Narrator: She moved to the opposite sheet.
Narrator: Another pale mark lay near the outer edge. Not a cut this time.
Narrator: A small round depression surrounded by puckered tissue.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's face lost color.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo saw it.
Enhedu: [uneasy] What is that?
Ninsun: [quietly] A burn scar.
Enhedu: [uneasy] Before or after?
Ninsun: [quietly] Before.
Narrator: NEEN-soon straightened slowly.
Ninsun: [horrified] The scar belonged to the body before the page belonged to the book.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one spoke.
Narrator: Zeek looked at his father's old handwriting between the spreading lines.
Zik: [quietly] What body?
Narrator: NEEN-soon stared at the parchment.
Ninsun: [shaken] A human one.
Narrator: The carters near the well stopped arguing.
Narrator: One lifted his head.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo smiled toward them again.
Enhedu: [dryly] Funerary accounts.
Narrator: The carter grimaced and moved his game pieces farther away.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stepped from the entrance and approached the table.
Balag: [gruffly] How many people?
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked at the seam where the pages met the binding.
Ninsun: [shaken] I cannot tell.
Balag: [grimly] That means more than one is possible.
Ninsun: [quietly] Yes.
Balag: [uneasy] Does the book know?
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one answered him.
Narrator: Zeek touched the edge of the cover, careful not to contact the page.
Narrator: The mark beneath his linen throbbed.
Narrator: A slow pressure spread through his palm.
Narrator: The largest spiral-drop symbol appeared at the lowest visible point of the map. The ink gathered gradually, filling the jagged outline until it became darker than anything else on the pages.
Narrator: The symbol was not inside a chamber.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: It surrounded one.
Narrator: Seven narrow routes descended toward a central blank space. Six ended before reaching it.
Narrator: The seventh continued beyond the bottom edge of the parchment.
Narrator: NEEN-soon bent over it.
Ninsun: [whispers] The seventh access.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo glanced toward the copied ledger hidden in NEEN-soon's robe.
Enhedu: [quietly] Ahm-MAH-roo kept it sealed.
Ninsun: [ominous] Until someone used his name as a key,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Narrator: A golden spark moved through the black symbol.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's hand went to his axe.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's fingers closed around her knife hilt.
Narrator: The spark traveled upward from the spiral, following one of the routes through the underground city.
Narrator: The lantern flame bent toward the page.
Narrator: Zeek's palm pulsed in the same rhythm.
Enhedu: [tense] Tell me that was reflected light,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Ninsun: [quietly] No,
Narrator: NEEN-soon answered.
Narrator: The golden line reached the surface map and crossed west.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Beyond the city.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Into the desert.
Narrator: Zeek leaned over the table.
Narrator: The route passed abandoned roads and dry channels. It ended at a rectangular chamber beneath a cluster of burial mounds.
Narrator: He knew the shape.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The outer shaft.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The sloping corridor.
Narrator: The chamber where KOO-rahsh had died.
Zik: [stunned] The tomb,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon rotated the journal toward herself.
Narrator: The map did not end at the burial chamber.
Narrator: A line continued beneath it.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Straight down.
Narrator: It passed through a narrow black band and entered another structure below the known tomb—larger, older, and drawn with lines that appeared less like masonry than bone.
Narrator: Zeek stared at it.
Zik: [quietly] We thought the chamber was the bottom.
Ninsun: [quietly] It wasn't,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Narrator: The golden spark reached the lower structure.
Narrator: A memory struck Zeek with such force that his hand slipped from the table.
Narrator: Black stone beneath desert dust.
Narrator: A descending passage where the air had pressed against his ears.
Narrator: KOO-rahsh's voice behind him, joking because silence had become worse.
Narrator: A surface at the far end that reflected no lamplight.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not a wall.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Something smoother.
Narrator: Something that had seemed to move when no one looked directly at it.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon watched him.
Ninsun: [gently] What did you remember?
Narrator: Zeek looked at the map.
Narrator: The ink around the buried structure thickened.
Narrator: New handwriting appeared in the margin.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Cramped.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Jagged.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: His father's hand.
Narrator: The ink appeared one drop at a time.
Narrator: Not falling from above.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Rising.
Narrator: Darkness gathered beneath the surface of the page until a single line pushed upward.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Nobody moved.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo spoke first.
Enhedu: [whispers] Is it writing?
Narrator: Another stroke appeared.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Then another.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg stood.
Balag: [uneasy] I preferred it when books stayed books.
Narrator: The letters continued slowly.
Narrator: Each one seemed to press through the page rather than settle upon it.
Narrator: Zeek found himself holding his breath.
Narrator: The sentence completed itself one word at a time.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] [sorrowful] I WAS WRONG ABOUT THE DEPTH.
Narrator: Zeek shut the journal.
Narrator: The sound cracked through the courtyard.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one spoke for several breaths.
Narrator: Rain dripped steadily from the reed canopy into the basin beside the well.
Narrator: BAH-lahg finally broke the silence.
Balag: [grimly] Back to the place that killed KOO-rahsh?
Narrator: Zeek rested both hands on the closed journal.
Zik: [quietly] We never reached the place that killed him.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked down at the leather cover.
Ninsun: [quietly] We stopped one level too soon.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's voice was barely above a whisper.
Enhedu: [whispers] Then whatever your father found…
Narrator: Zeek finished the thought.
Zik: [quietly] …is still waiting below.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 18 — THE PRICE OF KNOWING

Narrator: CHAPTER 18
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Price of Knowing
[pause 2s]
Narrator: No one spoke after BAYL-ID-din's final words.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] …is still waiting below.
Narrator: The journal lay closed between them.
Narrator: Rain dripped from the reed canopy into the basin beside the well. Somewhere beyond the courtyard wall a donkey brayed, followed by an argument over whether it belonged to the temple or to the man trying to drag it away.
Narrator: BAH-lahg broke the silence first.
Balag: [firmly] If we're doing this again, we leave prepared.
Narrator: Zeek nodded.
Zik: [practical] Rope. Lamps. Water. Oil.
Narrator: NEEN-soon gathered her copied notes.
Ninsun: [briskly] I'll inventory what we already have.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo stood.
Enhedu: [shrewdly] I'll see what can be bought without attracting attention.
Narrator: Zeek tightened the satchel across his shoulder.
Zik: [quietly] My equipment is still at my mother's house.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at him.
Balag: [gruffly] Then don't wander.
Narrator: Zeek almost laughed.
Zik: [amused] You know me better than that.
Narrator: He stepped into the evening streets.
Narrator: The streets were still crowded with evening traffic. Pilgrims moved toward the temples in loose, chanting groups. Merchants dragged carts through the torchlit haze. Prostitutes leaned in the shadows beneath painted doorways, calling to men who pretended not to hear them. Priests moved through the crowd with the self-important expressions of men who believed the gods had personally appointed them to supervise everyone else's mistakes.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Normal. Manageable.
Narrator: Zeek preferred the city when it was loud. Noise gave a man cover. In a crowd, people saw what they expected to see: a trader, a scavenger, another hungry bastard trying to make enough silver to survive the month.
Narrator: Everything had a price.
Narrator: His own tools remained at his mother's house.
Narrator: He headed toward his mother's, keeping to the shadows out of habit. Halfway there he noticed the first wrong thing.
Narrator: A temple scribe was watching him from across the street.
Narrator: The man was young, well-dressed, and clean enough to look suspicious on principle. His linen robe was too fine for the neighborhood, and the wax tablet tucked beneath his arm was decorated with a small silver seal. He was the kind of man who usually never looked twice at men like Zeek.
Narrator: But he was looking now.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek kept walking.
Narrator: The scribe kept watching.
Narrator: Zeek slowed near a stall selling bronze chisels. The scribe stopped.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek turned left.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The scribe followed.
Narrator: Zeek did not look back again. He changed direction twice, moving through the evening crowd with the casual rhythm of a man who had nowhere particular to be. He passed a spice seller, crossed behind a row of pottery stalls, and slipped into a narrow alley between a wine merchant's warehouse and a shuttered bakery.
Narrator: The alley smelled of old water and rotting grain. Zeek waited.
Narrator: The scribe entered thirty seconds later.
Narrator: Zeek stepped out behind him and pressed a knife lightly against his side.
Zik: [coldly] Temple business or personal?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The scribe stiffened.
False Scribe: [nervous] Neither.
Zik: [coldly] That wasn't one of the options.
Narrator: The young man swallowed. Zeek pressed the blade a little harder. Not enough to cut. Enough to remind him that the distinction was temporary.
False Scribe: [nervous] The Collector sends his regards,
Narrator: the scribe said.
Zik: [dryly] Then send mine back.
False Scribe: [nervous] He would like to speak with you again.
Zik: [dismissively] Tell him I'm busy.
False Scribe: [nervous] Tonight.
Narrator: Zeek smiled faintly.
Zik: [dryly] That sounded less like a request.
Narrator: The scribe glanced toward the mouth of the alley. No one was there. The crowd moved past without looking in.
False Scribe: [nervous] The Collector is prepared to offer ten times what you received today.
Zik: [dryly] For the beads?
Narrator: The scribe hesitated.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek's smile disappeared.
False Scribe: [nervous] For the journal,
Narrator: the scribe said.
Narrator: The knife stopped moving.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He continued quickly.
False Scribe: [nervous] Or anything else you removed from the chamber.
Narrator: For a moment the alley seemed to contract around them. Zeek had never mentioned the journal. He had never told the Collector about the chamber beneath the desert. He had never told him about the skeleton. He had never told him about the symbol appearing beneath his skin.
Narrator: The scribe knew about all of it. Or enough of it.
Zik: [sharply] How does he know?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
Narrator: The scribe's mouth opened. Nothing came out.
Narrator: Zeek turned the knife slightly.
Zik: [coldly] Try again.
False Scribe: [nervous] The Collector knows many things.
Zik: [coldly] That's not an answer.
False Scribe: [nervous] He says the mark has a way of announcing itself.
Narrator: Zeek looked down at his palm. The symbol was barely visible beneath the skin—a faint discoloration, an old scar if a man didn't know what he was looking at.
Zik: [intense] Announcing itself to whom?
Narrator: The scribe looked away.
False Scribe: [nervous] Especially to those who have seen it before.
Narrator: The words settled between them. Zeek slowly removed the knife.
Narrator: The scribe turned to face him.
False Scribe: [nervous] Is that all?
Zik: [coldly] No.
Narrator: Zeek waited.
Narrator: The scribe's face had gone pale.
False Scribe: [nervous] The Collector says you should not go back.
Narrator: Zeek stared at him.
Zik: [sharply] To the site?
Narrator: The scribe said nothing. That was answer enough.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek stepped closer.
Zik: [intense] Did he tell you why?
Narrator: The scribe's eyes flicked toward the knife.
False Scribe: [nervous] No.
Zik: [coldly] Did he tell you what happens if I do?
False Scribe: [nervous] No.
Zik: [dismissively] Then you have wasted both our time.
Narrator: Zeek stepped aside.
Narrator: The scribe hurried past him, then stopped at the end of the alley.
False Scribe: [calmly] Zee-keer.
Narrator: Zeek looked over his shoulder. The scribe's face had changed. The nervousness was gone. For just a second he looked older.
False Scribe: [ominous] Some doors are sealed because what is behind them is dangerous.
Narrator: Zeek waited.
Narrator: The scribe's eyes dropped to the mark on Zeek's palm.
False Scribe: [ominous] Some are sealed because the thing behind them is still waiting.
Narrator: Then he walked away.
Narrator: Zeek remained in the alley.
Narrator: The words should have sounded like a threat. Instead they sounded familiar. He hated that.
Narrator: When Zeek returned to his mother's house to gather the last of his gear, he found the second wrong thing.
Narrator: The door was locked.
Narrator: The crack in the frame was exactly where he had left it. Nothing looked disturbed.
Narrator: That was the problem.
Narrator: Zeek stood in the hallway for nearly a minute without entering.
Narrator: His father had taught him to notice things that were about to fail. He had also taught him that a room could tell you when someone had been inside it. A moved object. A displaced grain of dust. A door closed with the wrong amount of force.
Narrator: Zeek pushed the door open.
Narrator: The room was dark.
Narrator: His mother was asleep.
Narrator: The small clay tablet he used as a weight for papers was on the table. It had been beside the window.
Narrator: His spare knife was beneath his pallet. It had been on the shelf.
Narrator: His father's measuring cord still hung beside the doorway.
Narrator: BAYL-ID-din had always coiled it clockwise.
Narrator: Someone had hung it back the other way.
Narrator: Nobody else would have noticed.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek did.
Narrator: And the loose brick behind which he kept emergency coin was slightly askew.
Narrator: Zeek closed the door behind him.
Narrator: He did not wake his mother.
Narrator: He crossed the room and crouched beside the wall. The brick had been replaced carefully. Too carefully. He pulled it free.
Narrator: The emergency coins were still there. He counted them anyway.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: One. Two. Three.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: All present.
Narrator: He put them back and examined the brick. A small smear of pale dust marked one edge. Someone had removed it. Someone had put it back. Someone had been careful enough to leave the money untouched.
Narrator: Zeek sat on the edge of his pallet for a long moment.
Narrator: His mother breathed softly in her sleep. The kiln beneath the house gave a low, constant rumble. The room was warm. Familiar. Safe.
Narrator: The word was ridiculous.
Narrator: Zeek looked at the journal in his hands.
Narrator: The mark on his palm throbbed in time with his heartbeat. He had lied to the Collector. But the Collector had already known he was lying. That was the part Zeek couldn't fit into the arithmetic.
Narrator: A lie was a transaction. You gave someone a falsehood and received something in return: information, time, distance, safety.
Narrator: But the Collector had not been negotiating with him. He had been waiting.
Narrator: Zeek opened the journal. The pages were blank. He turned to the map.
Narrator: The vertical section was still there. The rivers. The impossible structures. The spiral descending through the layers of the world.
Narrator: At the bottom of the map, beneath the words I WAS WRONG ABOUT THE DEPTH, there was now a second line.
Narrator: Zeek stared at it.
Narrator: He had not seen it before.
Narrator: The handwriting was his father's.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] I WAS WRONG ABOUT WHO WAS LOOKING.
Narrator: He read it twice.
Narrator: Then a third time.
Narrator: Not because he doubted the words.
Narrator: Because he was trying to remember whether they had existed a heartbeat earlier.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: They hadn't.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek's breath stopped.
Narrator: He looked toward the sleeping pallet. His mother had not moved.
Narrator: The house was silent except for the kiln. He looked back at the journal.
Narrator: The ink beneath the sentence was still wet.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek touched it.
Narrator: The mark in his palm burned. He pulled his hand away.
Narrator: Zeek closed the journal.
Narrator: He closed the journal carefully.
Narrator: The leather settled beneath his hand.
Narrator: His mother still slept.
Narrator: The kiln rumbled beneath the floor exactly as it had every night since he was a child.
Narrator: Nothing inside the room had changed.
Narrator: Everything outside it had.
Narrator: Zeek slung the satchel over his shoulder.
Narrator: At the doorway he paused.
Narrator: He almost woke her.
Narrator: Instead he repaired the cracked frame with two careful pushes of his thumb until the wood sat flush again.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Habit.
Narrator: Then he stepped into the alley.
Narrator: The city breathed around him.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Torchlight.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Voices.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Wheels.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Dogs.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Somewhere, someone laughed.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Halfway to the street the journal moved.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Once.
Narrator: Not the shifting of paper.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: A knock.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek stopped.
Narrator: The mark beneath the linen pulsed once.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence.
Narrator: Then, from somewhere deep beneath OO-rook, something answered.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not loudly.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not with sound.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: With recognition.
Narrator: Zeek stood motionless in the alley.
Narrator: He looked toward the dark western quarter where the others would be waiting before dawn.
Narrator: Then back toward his mother's house.
Narrator: The journal became still.
Narrator: It did not feel asleep.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: It felt as though it had received an answer.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 19 — THE WRONG DEPTH

Narrator: CHAPTER 19
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Wrong Depth
[pause 2s]
Narrator: By the time Zeek returned to the inn, the rain had begun.
Narrator: It tapped against the reed canopy stretched above the courtyard and ran in silver threads from its sagging edges. The basin beside the well had already overflowed, sending a narrow stream across the paving stones. Two carters sat beneath the driest corner, moving painted knucklebones through a game neither appeared to be winning.
Narrator: NEEN-soon had covered the table with notes.
Narrator: Copies of Ahm-MAH-roo's vanished entry lay beside her sketch of the impossible map. She had weighted each corner with a cup, a lamp, or one of BAH-lahg's knives. En-KHEH-doo sat sideways on a stool, watching the courtyard entrance. BAH-lahg stood beside the wall with his arms crossed.
Narrator: All three looked at Zeek when he entered.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's gaze dropped to the satchel pressed against his ribs.
Balag: [gruffly] You took your time.
Zik: [flatly] I was followed.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo straightened.
Enhedu: [sharply] By whom?
Zik: [flatly] A temple scribe.
Enhedu: [dryly] That is disappointing. I had hoped for someone competent.
Zik: [quietly] He carried a message from the Collector.
Narrator: NEEN-soon set down her stylus.
Ninsun: [curious] What message?
Zik: [flatly] He wants the journal.
Narrator: The rain struck harder against the canopy.
Narrator: BAH-lahg glanced toward the satchel.
Balag: [alarmed] He knows about it?
Zik: [grimly] He knows enough to offer ten times what he paid earlier.
Ninsun: [intently] For the journal specifically?
Narrator: NEEN-soon asked.
Zik: [flatly] Yes.
Ninsun: [knowingly] And you refused.
Narrator: Zeek pulled out a stool.
Zik: [dryly] I was overcome by professional pride.
Ninsun: [dryly] You own none.
Zik: [dryly] I borrow it for negotiations.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo leaned forward, forearms on her knees.
Enhedu: [curious] What else did the scribe say?
Zik: [quietly] That we shouldn't return to the site.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's expression remained flat.
Balag: [dryly] For once, the Collector and I agree.
Zik: [uneasy] He also said the mark announces itself to people who have seen it before.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's eyes dropped immediately to Zeek's hand.
Narrator: The discoloration beneath his palm had faded until it resembled the shadow of an old scar. Zeek could still feel it pulsing. The sensation had not stopped since the answer beneath the city.
Ninsun: [intently] He recognized it?
Narrator: she asked.
Zik: [flatly] The scribe did not say.
Ninsun: [intently] But the Collector has seen it.
Zik: [dryly] That was the implication.
Narrator: NEEN-soon reached across the table.
Narrator: Zeek pulled his hand away before she touched him.

CHAPTER 20 — SURVEYOR OF THE LOWER THRESHOLDS

Narrator: CHAPTER 20
[pause 1s]
Narrator: Surveyor of the Lower Thresholds
[pause 2s]
The Collector: [softly] Zeek.
Narrator: The Collector's voice came through the shutter.
Narrator: Zeek kept his hand pressed against the journal.
Narrator: The leather beneath his palm was motionless, but the mark in his skin tightened as though someone had drawn a cord through it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg shifted beside the wall. His axe came free without scraping the plaster.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo raised one finger, warning him to wait.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stood beside the table with BAYL-ID-din's warning folded against her chest. In the darkness, the pale clay strip in her other hand looked like a shard of bone.
Narrator: Zeek moved toward the shutter.
Zik: [quietly] [dryly] You could have used the door.
The Collector: [smoothly] I did,
Narrator: the Collector said.
The Collector: [smoothly] It remains barred.
Zik: [quietly] That was intentional.
The Collector: [amused] So I assumed.
Narrator: His voice sounded close enough for his breath to touch the wood, yet Zeek had heard no approach after the second footstep. No robe brushing the wall. No sandal shifting in the wet grit below the window.
Narrator: Zeek stopped an arm's length from the shutter.
Zik: [quietly] [tense] How did you find us?
The Collector: [smoothly] I have known where this room was since you rented it.
Zik: [quietly] [sharply] That wasn't what I asked.
The Collector: [smoothly] No. It was the answer available to you.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo glanced at Zeek and shaped a silent word with her lips.
Narrator: Alone?
Narrator: Zeek shook his head once. He did not know.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The Collector continued.
The Collector: [softly] You found the seventh drawer.
Narrator: Not a question.
Narrator: Zeek looked toward NEEN-soon.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Her expression sharpened.
Zik: [quietly] MAR-doo told you?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
The Collector: [dryly] MAR-doo tells me nothing. It is one of his better qualities.
Zik: [quietly] [accusing] You have people in the archive.
The Collector: [smoothly] I have people everywhere men mistake employment for loyalty.
Narrator: BAH-lahg took one silent step toward the shutter.
Narrator: Zeek raised a hand.
Zik: [quietly] [intense] What was inside when my father opened it?

Narrator: The title entered the room and seemed to take up space.

Narrator: Zeek said.
The Collector: [smoothly] Records disagree.

The Collector: [smoothly] No appointment was required.

The Collector: [smoothly] Yes.

Narrator: The words reached BAH-lahg before they reached anyone else.

Narrator: Zeek said.
The Collector: [smoothly] I know.

Narrator: BAH-lahg crossed half the room before Zeek caught his forearm.

CHAPTER 21 — THE GATE OF LIES

Narrator: CHAPTER 21
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Gate of Lies
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They started walking.
Narrator: For the first hour, OO-rook remained behind them as a black interruption in the western sky.
Narrator: The city wall followed at their backs, visible whenever the land flattened and disappearing whenever the old irrigation ridges rose between them and the torches. From this distance, the watch fires looked pinned to the darkness. They did not seem to move, though Zeek knew guards were pacing above the bricks, looking outward for enemies and inward for citizens who had discovered unofficial uses for drainage channels.
Narrator: The ground beyond the wall was not yet desert. Abandoned fields lay beneath the darkness, divided by dry canals and low embankments. Thorn scrub crowded the old channels. Rainwater gathered in the deeper ruts, turning dust into mud that clung to their sandals.
Narrator: BAH-lahg walked behind Zeek, breathing heavily beneath his pack.
Balag: [breathless] [grumbling] How far?
Zik: [dryly] You asked that already.
Balag: [grumbling] I disliked the answer.
Zik: [flatly] Another half league.
Balag: [accusing] You said that half a league ago.
Zik: [dryly] No. I said we would not stop until sunrise.
Balag: [grumbling] That was worse.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stepped over a collapsed section of canal wall and nearly lost her footing. BAH-lahg caught the back of her satchel before she went down.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: She twisted around.
Balag: [sharply] Careful.
Ninsun: [primly] I was.
Balag: [gruffly] You almost fell into a ditch.
Ninsun: [primly] I identified the ditch before entering it.
Balag: [dryly] With your foot.
Ninsun: [primly] An archaeologist uses the instruments available.
Narrator: BAH-lahg released the satchel.
Balag: [gruffly] Next time, use your eyes.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo moved ahead of them, barely visible except when she crossed a strip of open ground. She had removed the pale wrapping from her hair and replaced it with dark cloth. Mud had dulled the lower half of her robes. One hand remained close to her knife.
Narrator: She stopped at the top of an embankment and raised two fingers.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The others halted.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek listened.
Narrator: The night carried small sounds farther than daylight did: water dripping from reeds, insects clicking beneath the thorn, BAH-lahg's breathing, NEEN-soon trying not to breathe loudly enough for BAH-lahg to comment on it.
Narrator: Beyond those sounds came the soft creak of leather.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Then a snort.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's hand moved toward his axe.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo crouched, peered down the far side of the embankment, and looked back at Zeek.

Zik: [flatly] Two.
Enhedu: [accusing] You did not mention animals.
Zik: [dryly] You did not ask how I intended to cross the desert carrying four lamps, a hammer, two ropes, and NEEN-soon's private library.
Narrator: NEEN-soon adjusted the satchel against her chest.
Ninsun: [indignant] It is not a library.
Zik: [dryly] Libraries usually have fewer tablets.
Narrator: BAH-lahg climbed the embankment beside En-KHEH-doo.
Narrator: Below them stood the ruins of a small field shelter. Three mud-brick walls remained. The fourth had collapsed into the dry canal. A roof of bundled reeds sagged above one corner.
Narrator: Two donkeys waited beneath it.
Narrator: One was gray, narrow-faced, and watching the group with immediate suspicion. The other was brown and already attempting to chew through the rope that secured it to a post.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stared at them.
Balag: [incredulous] You left animals outside the city wall.
Zik: [evasive] I paid a farmer to leave them.
Balag: [skeptical] Which farmer?
Zik: [dryly] A reliable one.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked around the abandoned fields.
Enhedu: [suspicious] There is no farm.
Zik: [dryly] He is reliable because he understands distance.
Narrator: BAH-lahg descended the embankment.
Narrator: The gray donkey flattened its ears at him.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He stopped.
Balag: [grumbling] You selected this one?
Zik: [dryly] It selected itself.
Balag: [grumbling] It hates me.
Zik: [dryly] You have that effect on thoughtful animals.

Narrator: The donkey considered him, then allowed him to touch its muzzle.
Narrator: NEEN-soon climbed down after him.
Ninsun: [curious] When did you arrange this?
Zik: [flatly] Before we entered the archive.
Ninsun: [surprised] You expected to flee OO-rook?
Zik: [dryly] I expected OO-rook to become inconvenient.
Ninsun: [primly] That is not the same thing.
Zik: [dryly] It is in my work.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo entered the shelter and examined the shadows beneath the remaining roof. She checked behind the fallen bricks, beneath the reed matting, and inside a cracked storage jar.
Enhedu: [uneasy] No farmer.
Zik: [evasive] He was instructed not to wait.
Enhedu: [suspicious] No footprints either.
Zik: [evasive] The rain.
Enhedu: [uneasy] The ground beneath the roof is dry.

Narrator: No sandal prints led in.

Ninsun: [uneasy] Hours.

Narrator: BAH-lahg said.

Ninsun: [firmly] They always matter.

Narrator: Zeek said.

Zik: [dismissively] It moves whenever it dislikes the conversation.
Ninsun: [intense] It knocked after he called you BAYL-ID-din's successor.
Zik: [defensive] It also knocked inside a wall.
Ninsun: [firmly] That is not an argument against meaning.
Zik: [dryly] It is an argument against expecting clarity.
Narrator: NEEN-soon lowered her voice.
Ninsun: [whispers] What else did your father write?
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Narrator: The darkness beneath her eyes had deepened since the archive. She had copied the map until her fingers cramped, then packed the copies as carefully as if they were royal decrees. Mud streaked the hem of her robe. A loose strand of hair had stuck to her cheek.
Narrator: She had followed him through a wall, across the roofs, beneath the city, and into fields where animals appeared without footprints.
Narrator: She still expected honest documentation.
Narrator: Zeek almost admired the stubbornness.
Zik: [quietly] He wrote that he was wrong about the depth,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Ninsun: [quietly] We know that.
Zik: [quietly] And about who was looking.
Ninsun: [quietly] We know that too.
Zik: [flatly] That is everything.
Narrator: NEEN-soon watched him.
Narrator: The journal pressed once against Zeek's ribs.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Slow.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Deliberate.

Ninsun: [intently] What did it say after we left the city?
Zik: [evasive] Nothing.
Narrator: The journal pressed again.
Narrator: NEEN-soon folded her arms.
Narrator: BAH-lahg resumed pulling at a strap that required no adjustment.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo spoke without turning.

Zik: [reluctantly] It did not write.

Narrator: NEEN-soon asked.

Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.

Narrator: En-KHEH-doo left the shelter's edge and approached.

Narrator: BAH-lahg gave a quiet sound in his throat.
Balag: [gravely] My grandfather told stories about seven gates.
Ninsun: [dismissively] Most cultures have threshold myths.
Balag: [gravely] My grandfather called them warnings.
Ninsun: [primly] Most warnings become myths after enough people ignore them.
Narrator: Zeek secured the last waterskin.
Zik: [curious] What did each gate do?
Narrator: NEEN-soon ran her thumb above the copied signs without touching them.
Ninsun: [solemn] The traveler surrendered something at each threshold.
Zik: [curious] What sort of thing?
Ninsun: [solemn] At the first gate, a covering.
Enhedu: [curious] Clothing?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Ninsun: [solemn] Possibly. At the second, ornament. At the third, rank. The fourth passage is damaged.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked toward the drainage channel hidden beyond the fields.
Balag: [ominous] Four knocks.
Narrator: NEEN-soon nodded.
Ninsun: [intently] The surviving sign can mean name, declaration, or recognized identity.
Narrator: Zeek touched the pouch beneath his robe.
Narrator: The bronze seal lay cold against his chest.
Zik: [quietly] It tells the door what stands before it,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked at him.
Ninsun: [intently] The Collector's words.
Zik: [thoughtful] The seal has seven lines.
Ninsun: [quietly] And an empty center.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo held out her hand.
Enhedu: [curious] Show me.
Narrator: Zeek did not move.
Zik: [guarded] You examined it already.
Enhedu: [dryly] I examined it in a room where a man had climbed away with his hands pointing in the wrong direction. I was distracted.
Zik: [dryly] You seemed focused.
Enhedu: [dryly] I can be both.
Narrator: Zeek drew the pouch from beneath his robe and removed the bronze seal.
Narrator: The lines remained dark where they had changed beneath his marked palm. Mud had dried around the rim, but the grooves were clean.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon took it.
Narrator: She turned it toward the east, where the first thin suggestion of dawn had begun to separate the sky from the land.
Ninsun: [awed] Seven descending lines,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [quietly] Seven thresholds.
Narrator: BAH-lahg came closer.
Balag: [uneasy] And the empty center?
Ninsun: [quietly] The place beyond them.
Enhedu: [ominous] Or the thing they surround,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked at her.
Ninsun: [quietly] Yes.
Narrator: BAH-lahg rubbed his beard.
Balag: [gravely] My grandfather's version had no clothing.
Zik: [curious] What did it take?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
Balag: [gravely] First gate took a man's shadow. Second took the voice people knew him by. Third took the hand he trusted. After that, the story changed depending on how much beer my grandfather had.
Zik: [quietly] And the fourth?
Narrator: BAH-lahg's mouth tightened.
Balag: [gravely] His name.
Narrator: The gray donkey shifted beside him.
Narrator: Its hoof struck the ground four times.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Once.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Then twice.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Then once.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one spoke.
Narrator: BAH-lahg took hold of the animal's halter.
Balag: [grumbling] It has mud in its shoe.
Ninsun: [primly] Donkeys do not have shoes,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Balag: [grumbling] Then it has mud in whatever you call the part I need to blame.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked toward the fields again.
Enhedu: [curious] The story ends how?
Narrator: BAH-lahg stroked the animal's neck until it settled.
Balag: [gravely] The man reaches the seventh gate.
Enhedu: [quietly] And?
Balag: [gravely] He has nothing left that the gate can take.
Zik: [dryly] That sounds useful,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Balag: [gravely] No. It means the gate takes what is left.
Narrator: The eastern horizon grew paler.
Narrator: Shapes emerged from the darkness: dead canals, thorn clusters, the low swells of abandoned fields. Farther east, the cultivated ground thinned into scrub. Beyond that waited the open desert.
Narrator: NEEN-soon handed the seal back to Zeek.
Ninsun: [solemn] The lower archive contains other fragments. Some say clothing and jewelry. Some say memory, title, ancestry. One version says the final gate requires the living traveler to surrender the last thing that separates the living from the dead.
Narrator: BAH-lahg glanced at her.
Balag: [uneasy] Breath?
Ninsun: [quietly] The text does not say.
Enhedu: [curious] Soul?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Ninsun: [primly] That word is not present.
Enhedu: [dryly] Good. I was concerned it might become specific.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] The translations may describe ritual disarmament,
Narrator: NEEN-soon continued.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] A ruler entering sacred space is stripped of rank and visible authority. Each gate removes a social identity.
Zik: [dryly] Until the ruler is only a person,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Ninsun: [quietly] Possibly.
Zik: [dryly] And then the last gate kills him.
Ninsun: [quietly] Possibly.
Narrator: BAH-lahg gave Zeek a flat look.
Balag: [skeptical] You sound pleased.
Zik: [dryly] I am relieved the ancient priests may have been murdering kings instead of feeding doors.
Balag: [dryly] That is the lowest standard for relief I have heard.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo rested one hand on the hilt of her knife.
Enhedu: [ominous] What if it is not ritual?
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked toward the east.
Ninsun: [quietly] Then the stories are instructions.
Enhedu: [curious] For entering?
Narrator: Her gaze shifted to the seal in Zeek's hand.
Ninsun: [ominous] Or for preventing something from leaving.
Narrator: The wind moved across the fields.
Narrator: It came from the west, carrying the smell of wet brick, smoke, dung fires, and river mud.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: OO-rook.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek turned.
Narrator: The city had emerged behind them with the dawn.
Narrator: It rose in layers above the wall, rooftops pressed close together, smoke already unwinding from ovens and workshops. The temple platforms caught the first light. Their upper walls burned red while the lower districts remained blue with shadow.
Narrator: From here, no cracks showed.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: No false doors.
Narrator: No rain climbing walls.
Narrator: No narrow figure standing between torches.
Narrator: OO-rook looked ancient enough to have invented permanence and arrogant enough to tax anyone who copied it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon came to stand beside him.
Ninsun: [quietly] The texts call the place beyond the gates a city.
Balag: [awed] The First City,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Ninsun: [primly] Not necessarily.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Zik: [incredulous] You crossed a wall, a roof, a drainage passage, and three miles of mud to correct us now?
Ninsun: [primly] The term translated as city can also mean ordered enclosure, inhabited foundation, or place under rule.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo joined them.
Enhedu: [curious] Can it mean tomb?
Ninsun: [quietly] Sometimes.
Enhedu: [uneasy] Prison?
Narrator: NEEN-soon hesitated.
Ninsun: [hesitant] Yes.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked east.
Balag: [uneasy] And lid?
Narrator: Another pause.
Ninsun: [quietly] Yes.
Narrator: Zeek felt the bronze seal warming in his palm.
Narrator: The Collector's voice returned to him.
The Collector: [smoothly] The City is not a tomb.
Narrator: Zeek had dismissed the rest as a threat shaped to fit an old fear.
Narrator: But the old texts had been written before the Collector. Before BAYL-ID-din. Before KOO-rahsh raised the hammer over the black floor.
Narrator: NEEN-soon spoke more quietly.
Ninsun: [softly] The accounts do not describe people building the First City.
Zik: [quietly] What do they describe?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
Ninsun: [ominous] Closing it.
Narrator: The gray donkey snorted behind them.
Narrator: A bird called from the reeds near the dry canal.
Narrator: The ordinary sound made the silence afterward worse.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at the ruined shelter.
Balag: [skeptical] If this is a gate, where is the door?
Narrator: NEEN-soon considered the fields, the hidden drainage passage, and OO-rook beyond them.
Ninsun: [quietly] Perhaps behind us.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's gaze went to the wall.
Enhedu: [skeptical] We crawled through a drain.
Ninsun: [primly] Ritual accounts often preserve function rather than architecture.
Enhedu: [dryly] That sentence was designed to excuse anything.
Ninsun: [primly] It was designed to explain why old texts should not be read like building plans.
Narrator: Zeek opened his satchel.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg turned.
Balag: [sharply] What are you doing?
Zik: [dryly] Checking whether the journal has decided to become useful.
Balag: [grumbling] That thing has never been useful. It has been accurate. Those are not the same.
Narrator: Zeek drew it out.
Narrator: The cover was dry despite the mud and rain that had soaked the satchel. The scar along its surface caught the dawn like a thin line of oil.
Narrator: He untied the wrapping.
Narrator: NEEN-soon moved close enough to see.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo remained behind his shoulder.
Narrator: BAH-lahg muttered something that sounded like a ward and might have been an insult.
