Narrator: Zeek opened to the map.
Narrator: The page showed the descending route beneath the desert. Seven narrowing turns surrounded the dark center. The line from OO-rook still extended westward across the skin.
Narrator: For a moment, nothing changed.
Narrator: Then the ink nearest the city began to fade.
Narrator: NEEN-soon reached for the page.
Narrator: Zeek caught her wrist.
Zik: [sharply] Do not touch it.
Ninsun: [defensive] I know.
Zik: [dryly] You never behave as though you know.
Narrator: The drawn outline of OO-rook paled.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Walls disappeared first.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Then streets.
Narrator: Then the temple platforms.
Narrator: The last mark to vanish was the western gate, though they had not passed through it.
Narrator: In its place, a single vertical line darkened across the route.
Narrator: It ran from the top of the page to the bottom.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: A threshold.
Narrator: Beside it, letters appeared in BAYL-ID-din's hand.
Narrator: The strokes formed slowly, pressing upward through the skin as if written from the other side.
Narrator: NEEN-soon read them aloud.
Ninsun: [whispers] First gate.
Narrator: BAH-lahg came closer.
Balag: [tense] What did it take?
Narrator: Another word formed beneath the first.
Narrator: Zeek knew what it would say before the final mark appeared.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] PAID.
Narrator: The mark in his palm went cold.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stared at the word.
Ninsun: [fearful] Paid with what?
Narrator: The page remained still.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo checked her knives, then the dark cloth around her wrist, then the small pouch beneath her robe.
Narrator: BAH-lahg touched his throat.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked down at her tablets.
Narrator: Zeek searched himself for an absence.
Narrator: He remembered his mother sleeping in the repaired house. The archive. His father's face. KOO-rahsh raising the hammer. OO-rook behind them.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Nothing seemed missing.
Narrator: That did not reassure him.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked from one face to another.
Balag: [urgently] We count everything.
Enhedu: [confused] What?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Balag: [firmly] Names. Memories. Fingers. Whatever the stories say these gates take.
Enhedu: [dryly] You would notice a missing finger.
Balag: [firmly] I would prefer to establish that.
Narrator: NEEN-soon pressed two fingers beneath her jaw as though checking for a pulse.
Ninsun: [shaky] My name is NEEN-soon EH-tah-nah.
Balag: [firmly] BAH-lahg,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked at them.
Enhedu: [dismissively] This is foolish.
Balag: [firmly] Name,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Enhedu: [annoyed] You know it.
Balag: [insistent] Say it.
Narrator: She watched him for a moment.
Enhedu: [reluctantly] En-KHEH-doo.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at Zeek.
Narrator: Zeek closed the journal.
Zik: [firmly] Zee-keer AHSH-shoor.
Narrator: The journal knocked once between his hands.
Narrator: No answer came from beneath the ground.
Narrator: The sun broke over the eastern horizon.
Narrator: Light spread across the fields and reached OO-rook's upper walls. The city stood behind them in brick, smoke, and gold.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: It looked permanent.
Narrator: That was the first lie.
Narrator: Zeek wrapped the journal and returned it to the satchel.
Narrator: BAH-lahg finished loading the donkeys. NEEN-soon secured her tablets. En-KHEH-doo climbed onto the brown animal before it could object and settled the reins in one hand.
Narrator: Zeek mounted behind her pack frame. BAH-lahg took the gray donkey's lead rope and walked beside it, refusing to ride until he was satisfied the load had settled evenly.
Narrator: NEEN-soon climbed onto the remaining space behind the waterskins.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: They turned east.
Narrator: Behind them, OO-rook disappeared one wall at a time as the land rose between the city and the desert.
Narrator: Ahead, the old road ran through dead fields toward the dunes.
Narrator: The journal rested silently against Zeek's ribs.
Narrator: The first gate had taken its price.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: None of them knew what they had paid
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 22 — THE DESERT REMEMBERS

Narrator: CHAPTER 22
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Desert Remembers
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The first three days were almost ordinary.
Narrator: The desert remained what it had always been: heat that baked thought into dust, wind that found every seam in clothing, and long stretches of nothing that made a man question every choice that had brought him there. The dunes looked frozen in the act of becoming waves. Wind had carved ribs into the sand where forgotten roads once ran. Zeek had crossed enough of it to know that ordinary did not mean safe. It only meant nothing had decided to kill them yet.
Narrator: They rode in a loose line. BAH-lahg led the donkeys, muttering the old wards his grandfather had taught him whenever the wind shifted. En-KHEH-doo rode ahead, watching the horizon the way a thief watches a locked door. NEEN-soon kept to the middle, writing whenever they stopped. Zeek brought up the rear, the journal heavy against his ribs and the mark on his palm itching worse with every mile.
Narrator: On the second night, around a small fire, the friction that had been simmering since the gate finally surfaced.
Narrator: NEEN-soon spread three scraps of parchment across her knees.
Ninsun: [intense] We should be documenting everything. The map, the symbol, the exact location of the first chamber, the way the writing changes. This knowledge could alter how the entire region is understood.
Narrator: Zeek was checking a donkey's strap.
Zik: [dismissively] We document what sells. The rest is weight.
Ninsun: [indignant] That is an astonishingly small way to look at the world.
Zik: [flatly] It's a practical way.
Narrator: BAH-lahg poked the fire.
Balag: [gravely] Some things should stay buried. My grandfather said the deep desert doesn't forget. It just waits for someone stupid enough to remind it.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's voice came from the shadows.
Enhedu: [coldly] Then we remind it with a sharp knife and a heavier purse. Knowledge is a weapon. Stop treating it like a sacred relic.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at her.
Balag: [gruffly] You say that like you've ever learned anything you couldn't sell.
Enhedu: [defensive] I've learned plenty of things I couldn't sell.
Balag: [challenging] Name one.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo considered, then smiled without warmth.
Enhedu: [coldly] No.
Narrator: BAH-lahg gave a short nod, as if the answer confirmed something he had already decided.
Balag: [knowingly] Thought so.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The silence that followed was sharper than the argument.
Narrator: NEEN-soon shook her head.
Ninsun: [exasperated] You are all impossible.
Narrator: Zeek said nothing. The argument was older than any of them; it had simply found new mouths.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The third day brought the first wrong thing.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They found an old well just before dusk—an ancient ring of stone half-buried in sand, the remains of a road running past it toward nowhere. Footprints led to the edge and stopped. None led away.
Narrator: BAH-lahg circled it twice before approaching.
Balag: [uneasy] Someone's used it recently.
Narrator: While the others examined the tracks, he crouched at the rim, took a small carved bead from a pouch at his belt, and set it carefully on the stones. No one commented. Later, when NEEN-soon asked why, he only said,
Balag: [quietly] [solemn] Payment.
Narrator: When she asked to whom, he answered,
Balag: [quietly] Exactly.
Narrator: Zeek looked down the shaft. The darkness was deeper than the angle of the sun allowed. He lowered the bucket. The rope paid out far longer than the depth should have permitted. When the bucket finally struck water the sound that came back was wrong—hollow, delayed, almost like an answer.
Narrator: They drank. The water was warm, yet it tasted of iron and cold stone, as though it had never seen sunlight.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: That night the donkeys refused to graze. Both animals stood rigid, ears pointed toward the same empty stretch of dark. En-KHEH-doo was the first to speak.
Enhedu: [whispers] [uneasy] Did anyone hear that?
Narrator: No one had. The donkeys had.
Narrator: A thin line appeared in the sand several paces beyond the firelight, moving slowly toward the camp as if something beneath the surface were dragging a finger across the desert. It stopped. The mark on Zeek's palm went cold. Then the line vanished.
Narrator: BAH-lahg got the animals under control.
Balag: [firmly] [uneasy] We leave at first light.
Narrator: No one disagreed.
Narrator: Zeek sat apart with the journal. The page he had left blank was no longer blank. A single line had appeared in a hand that was not his father's and not quite his own:
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: [slowly] NOT YET.
Narrator: He closed the book before anything else could form.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Somewhere beyond the firelight the desert shifted, patient and vast, as if it had only just remembered their names.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 23 — THE VOICE IN THE SAND

Narrator: CHAPTER 23
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Voice in the Sand
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The fourth dawn rose the color of old copper.
Narrator: Zeek had not slept. The mark on his palm had throbbed through the night in time with his heartbeat, steady enough that the edges of the world felt thinner than they should. He kept the hand wrapped and told the others it was rope burn. They pretended to believe him.
Narrator: The donkeys had changed. By morning both animals refused the western slope of the dune where they had camped. They pulled against the ropes until the leather creaked, ears pinned flat. BAH-lahg crouched beside the nearer one and ran a hand down its neck. The animal trembled under his palm.
Balag: [quietly] [uneasy] They know,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo snorted as she tightened the straps on her pack.
Enhedu: [snorts] They know they're donkeys. That's already more than most men.
Narrator: BAH-lahg ignored her. He had spent enough years in the desert to trust what animals noticed first.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They broke camp without discussion and moved east. The donkeys settled once the western slope was behind them, though BAH-lahg kept glancing back. The desert stretched in every direction, a field of gold and red broken only by stone outcroppings and the skeletal remains of old caravan routes. The farther they traveled from OO-rook, the less the world seemed to belong to people.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: By midmorning the heat pressed down through their clothing and wrapped itself around the backs of their necks. Zeek walked for part of the day to spare the animals. NEEN-soon rode in silence, filling the margins of her parchment with notes. She had stopped trying to hide what she was writing. The spiral-drop mark appeared again and again, copied from memory in different forms—some precise, some distorted, one containing a second shape buried inside the first.
Zik: [curious] What are you looking for?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
Ninsun: [quietly] A pattern.
Zik: [curious] Find one?
Ninsun: [quietly] Several.
Zik: [dryly] That's usually when things become expensive.
Narrator: She looked up.
Ninsun: [pointedly] You say that as if everything can be reduced to a transaction.
Zik: [flatly] Most things can.
Ninsun: [challenging] Death?
Narrator: Zeek kept walking.
Zik: [flatly] Especially death.
Narrator: They stopped shortly after midday beneath the thin shade of a half-buried rock spine. The stone gave barely enough cover for the four of them, but the sun had grown fierce enough that even a narrow strip of shadow felt like a gift.
Narrator: Zeek drank from his waterskin and immediately spat the water into the sand.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked over.
Enhedu: [concerned] Still bad?
Zik: [disgusted] Still tastes like it never saw the sun.
Narrator: NEEN-soon took a careful sip from her own skin and stared at it.
Ninsun: [uneasy] Metallic. But not only iron. Something else is in it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's expression darkened.
Balag: [gravely] Old blood.
Narrator: No one asked how he knew.
Narrator: Zeek watched the water darken the sand and vanish almost at once, as if the ground had drawn it down. For a moment the grains seemed to shift against one another. He blinked. The sand was still.
Zik: [uneasy] How much water do we have left?
Narrator: BAH-lahg answered without checking.
Balag: [grimly] Enough if we reach the next well on schedule.
Zik: [quietly] And if we don't?
Narrator: BAH-lahg's silence was answer enough.
Narrator: The first sound came a few minutes later.
Narrator: At first Zeek thought it was wind. The noise was so low he felt it more than heard it—a vibration passing through the stone beneath his hand. The dune beyond the rock spine shivered. Sand slipped down its slope in thin streams. Every grain around their boots began to tremble. Tiny ripples crossed the surface of every waterskin. The fire flattened, every flame leaning toward the earth as though something below were drawing breath.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg stopped moving.
Enhedu: [whispers] [alert] What?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Narrator: He raised one hand.
Narrator: The vibration continued, rising slowly, gathering beneath the silence of the desert. A low hum emerged, then another tone beneath it, then another. The sound seemed to come from everywhere at once.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon stood.
Ninsun: [fearful] What is that?
Narrator: The hum deepened.
Narrator: The donkeys began to scream.
Narrator: They reared against their ropes, eyes rolling white. BAH-lahg lunged for the nearest bridle. The second animal kicked wildly and nearly caught him in the chest.
Balag: [shouts] Hold them!
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's knife was in her hand before she seemed to realize she had drawn it. Zeek stood motionless.
Narrator: The sound was no longer a hum. It was voices.
Narrator: Thousands of them spoke over one another in a language that scraped against the inside of his skull. Some sounded close enough to touch. Others seemed to come from impossibly far away, buried beneath miles of stone and sand.
Narrator: NEEN-soon pressed both hands over her ears.
Ninsun: [trembling] It isn't coming from outside.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her. Her face had gone pale.
Ninsun: [whispers] [fearful] It's inside,
Narrator: she whispered.
Narrator: The mark beneath the linen burned. The voices separated for one brief, terrible instant. Fragments reached him: his name, his father's name, the word lid, the word hunger. Then another word rose above the others—not spoken, but written across the inside of his mind.
The City: [whispers] Zee-keer.
Narrator: The sound stopped.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The silence that followed pressed harder than the noise had.
Narrator: The donkeys continued trembling. BAH-lahg stood between them and the open desert, one hand on a bridle, the other on the haft of his axe. En-KHEH-doo kept her knife raised. NEEN-soon slowly lowered her hands.
Balag: [shaken] That wasn't wind,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Zik: [quietly] No,
Narrator: Zeek answered. His own voice sounded strange to him.
Narrator: NEEN-soon turned toward him.
Ninsun: [urgently] What did you hear?
Narrator: Zeek looked at the desert. The dunes had not changed. The sky was clear. Nothing moved except the wind beginning to lift dust from the crest of the nearest ridge.
Zik: [quietly] An invitation,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: They made camp early that evening. No one argued. The sun descended behind the dunes, turning the sky the color of tarnished metal. They built a small fire and kept the animals close. No one talked much.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Later, after the others had gone quiet, Zeek sat apart with the journal open across his knees. The stars above the desert were impossibly clear. He turned the pages.
Narrator: A new entry had appeared on a page that had been blank the night before. The handwriting was his father's.
Bel-iddin: [gravely] You brought them with you.
Narrator: Zeek stared at the sentence.
Narrator: Below it, another line slowly darkened.
Bel-iddin: [softly] [ominous] That was kind.
Narrator: The mark beneath the linen began to pulse. Zeek looked toward the others—BAH-lahg asleep beside the donkeys, one hand still near his axe; En-KHEH-doo with her knife beneath her arm; NEEN-soon curled under a blanket, notes scattered beside her.
Narrator: The journal continued writing.
Bel-iddin: [sorrowful] You should not have brought them.
Narrator: Zeek's mouth went dry. He pressed his marked palm against the page. The journal shivered beneath his hand—not violently, not like an object being shaken, but like something alive responding to touch.
Narrator: He pulled his hand away.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: For a long moment he considered waking the others. Then the desert spoke.
Narrator: The words came in his father's voice, but without his father's rhythm. Every pause was wrong. Every breath arrived half a heartbeat too late, as though something had reconstructed the sound from memory rather than ever having spoken it.
Bel-iddin: [eerie] Everything has a price.
Narrator: Zeek froze.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He turned.
Narrator: There was nothing behind him but darkness and sand.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The fire crackled.
Narrator: The journal remained open on his knees.
Narrator: One word had replaced everything else.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] Yes.
Narrator: A final line appeared beneath it.
Bel-iddin: [ominous] And you already paid.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 24 — THE FIRST DOOR

Narrator: CHAPTER 24
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The First Door
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They reached the ridge at noon on the fifth day, and the land itself was lying.
Narrator: Zeek knew the shape from his father's map—the broken line of stone that should have marked the old outpost, the shallow depression where the outer wall had collapsed, the narrow ridge that should have risen behind it like the spine of a buried animal. None of that was there.
Narrator: Instead they found a low mound of newer masonry half-swallowed by sand, sitting at a slight, deliberate angle to the ridge. The stones did not match. The foundations did not match. Even the way the structure had settled into the earth seemed wrong, as if the entire outpost had been built according to one set of rules and then forced to conceal another.
Narrator: NEEN-soon noticed the discrepancy first. She dropped to her knees beside a collapsed wall and ran her fingers along the joint where newer brick met older foundation. Sand had collected in the seam, but not enough to hide the difference.
Ninsun: [intently] This wall is lying,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: Zeek crouched beside her.
Zik: [curious] Explain.
Ninsun: [intently] It's supposed to be plumb. Straight. The foundation beneath it is aligned with the ridge, but the upper structure isn't. The entire settlement was built at an angle.
Zik: [curious] Why?
Narrator: She looked at the wall.
Ninsun: [ominous] To cover what was already here.
Narrator: BAH-lahg shifted his axe from one shoulder to the other. He had been watching the surrounding desert instead of the ruin.
Balag: [uneasy] We've seen enough.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo smiled the way she did when she found the weak seam in a strongbox.
Enhedu: [excited] We've seen nothing yet.
Narrator: They worked for hours. The heat made the air shimmer above the stones. They uncovered broken pottery, ordinary foundations, the remains of a storage room, and the scattered bones of mules that had died centuries earlier. NEEN-soon catalogued everything she could identify. En-KHEH-doo searched the collapsed rooms for anything worth carrying. BAH-lahg did most of the heavy work and complained that everyone else had been born with useless hands.
Narrator: The ordinary things were almost comforting. Zeek found himself wanting the ruin to remain exactly that, and the realization frightened him more than the strange parts.
Narrator: Every few minutes his eyes returned to the angle of the walls. His father had taught him to read construction the way other men read written language. A building told you what its builders believed about the world—what they feared, what they valued, and what they expected to happen after they were gone. This place had been built by men who expected it to remain buried.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: At sunset En-KHEH-doo's shovel struck stone with a different sound.
Narrator: Zeek heard it immediately.
Zik: [alert] Again.
Narrator: She struck a second time. The hollow note came back. BAH-lahg stopped digging. NEEN-soon moved closer, her face tightening as she examined the exposed stone.
Ninsun: [intently] This isn't part of the foundation.
Zik: [quietly] No,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: They cleared the area carefully. The staircase began beneath the main chamber of the outpost and descended at an angle that made no architectural sense. The steps ignored the alignment of every wall above them. The upper settlement faced one direction; the staircase descended toward another.
Narrator: NEEN-soon traced the difference with a shaking finger.
Ninsun: [realizing] The upper settlement was built after this staircase was sealed. They knew what was down here.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked toward the surface.
Balag: [firmly] Then we go back up.
Ninsun: [intense] They built the whole place to hide the entrance,
Narrator: NEEN-soon continued, as if she hadn't heard him.
Ninsun: [intense] Not around it. Over it.
Narrator: Zeek stared down into the darkness. The words from the journal returned to him, altered by everything they had learned since: the First City had never been a place of the living. It had been built to hold something down.
Narrator: They lit fresh torches and began the descent.
Narrator: The stairs ended after only twenty feet at a blank wall. There was no door, no inscription, no visible mechanism—only a seamless surface made from a material none of them recognized. It was dull and almost oily, and it did not reflect the torchlight. The flames seemed to lose their strength when held near it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon scraped away centuries of dust with her knife. Beneath it lay the spiral-drop symbol, incomplete. A section had been deliberately left out, as if whoever carved it had stopped before finishing the design.
Narrator: Zeek's left palm began to warm. He stepped closer.
Balag: [warning] Zeek,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Narrator: Three measured knocks sounded from the other side of the wall.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Everyone froze.
Narrator: The sound was not loud, which made it worse. It had been deliberate, controlled, almost polite. Three more knocks followed, patient and precise.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's voice came out low and final.
Balag: [firmly] We're leaving.
Narrator: Zeek kept his eyes on the wall.
Zik: [quietly] We just got here.
Balag: [firmly] That is exactly the problem.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo lowered her knife.
Enhedu: [whispers] [fearful] Did that come from behind the wall?
Narrator: NEEN-soon stared at the symbol.
Ninsun: [whispers] Yes.
Enhedu: [whispers] [uneasy] Then why does it sound like it's standing on the other side?
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one answered.
Narrator: The journal shifted inside Zeek's satchel. He removed it slowly and opened the cover. A new line had appeared across the page. The ink was still settling into the fibers.
Narrator: The first door was built by men.
Narrator: The second was not.
Narrator: NEEN-soon read the words over his shoulder.
Ninsun: [whispers] What does that mean?
Narrator: Zeek looked at the wall.
Zik: [quietly] [determined] I think we're about to find out.
Narrator: His father had reached this place. Whatever it cost, Zeek needed to know how far he had gone.
Narrator: He pressed his marked palm against the stone.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The world unfolded.
Narrator: For a moment he was no longer standing in the stairwell. He saw the ground as it had been centuries earlier—open and raw beneath a sky that looked exactly like the one above them now. The older city lay beneath the earth like a secret pressed between pages. Above it, thousands of men worked beneath the eyes of priests and kings. They were not building a new city. They were burying the old one.
Narrator: Massive stones were dragged across the ground by teams of exhausted workers. Ropes groaned. Men shouted. Overseers struck anyone who stopped moving. There was no celebration, no ceremony, no dedication—only the silence men used when they were afraid of being heard.
Narrator: One worker paused and looked down into the darkness of the unfinished structure. Then he dropped his tools and began screaming. The others turned. Several men dragged him away while he kicked and clawed at the ground. He screamed until the sound became raw and animal. A priest struck him across the mouth. The worker kept screaming.
Narrator: His father stood in the same place. He was older than Zeek remembered—hair streaked with gray, face thinner, clothing torn and stained with dust. He looked like a man who had been walking for years without ever reaching the place he intended to go. Then he looked straight toward Zeek across the impossible distance. His expression was not fear. It was sorrow.
Bel-iddin: [sorrowful] You shouldn't be able to see this,
Narrator: he said.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The vision ended.
Narrator: Zeek woke on the ground with BAH-lahg's heavy hands pinning his shoulders.
Balag: [shouts] [fearful] Zeek!
Narrator: NEEN-soon was shouting his name. En-KHEH-doo stood over him with one hand on the journal and the other on her knife, apparently unsure which represented the greater threat.
Narrator: Zeek tried to sit up. BAH-lahg held him down.
Balag: [firmly] Stay there.
Zik: [weakly] I'm fine.
Balag: [shaken] You were standing there, then you weren't.
Zik: [dryly] [weakly] That happens.
Balag: [firmly] Not to people who are fine.
Narrator: Zeek shoved BAH-lahg's hands away and sat up slowly. Sand clung to his sweat-soaked clothing. His head felt as if someone had driven a wedge through the center of it. The mark on his palm was colder than the desert night.
Narrator: NEEN-soon crouched beside him.
Ninsun: [urgently] What did you see?
Narrator: Zeek looked at the wall. He could remember that he and his father had once explored a ruin together. He could remember that the day had mattered. He could remember his father's voice explaining the mortar. But the ruin itself was gone—the color of the stones, the shape of the entrance, the exact sound of his father's voice. There was a clean hole where the memory should have been.
Narrator: NEEN-soon watched his face.
Ninsun: [quietly] [fearful] What did it take?
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Zik: [confused] What?
Ninsun: [quietly] You were remembering. Then you weren't.
Narrator: He said nothing.
Narrator: The wall in front of them had gone silent. But the knocking had moved. Three came, then three more, no longer from the wall. They were coming from somewhere behind the group, deeper in the earth, and they seemed to be getting closer.
Narrator: BAH-lahg slowly turned toward the stairs.
Balag: [fearful] That's not possible.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked at him.
Enhedu: [dryly] You should stop saying that.
Narrator: Another knock sounded—this one from beneath their feet. The stone trembled.
Narrator: Zeek stood and looked at the misaligned stones above them, the staircase that should not exist, and the wall built by frightened men who had mistaken burial for safety. They were not standing above the First City. They were standing above the first door. And the City was deeper.
Narrator: He opened his father's map. The lines on the page had changed. The staircase was no longer drawn as a straight descent. It continued downward past the point where the map had previously ended, past the foundations and the buried settlement. A new line had appeared beneath the old notation.
Bel-iddin: [solemn] I WAS WRONG ABOUT THE DEPTH.
Narrator: Zeek stared at it.
Narrator: Then another line formed beneath it.
Bel-iddin: [ominous] I WAS WRONG ABOUT WHAT WAS BENEATH US.
Narrator: The knocking stopped.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: For several seconds there was only the sound of their breathing. Then a single knock came from directly beneath the floor.
Narrator: Zeek folded the map.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: The map had not been wrong about the depth. It had been wrong about where the city began.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 25 — THE WAY BACK

Narrator: CHAPTER 25
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Way Back
[pause 2s]
Narrator: Zeek woke before dawn to silence.
Narrator: Real silence—the kind the desert had not offered since they found the staircase. No wind moved across the dunes. No night birds called. Even the donkeys stood still, ears down, as if holding their breath with him.
Narrator: He lay beneath his blanket and listened for the knocking. Nothing came.
Narrator: Slowly he reached for the journal. The cover was colder than the desert night had any right to leave it. He opened it anyway. No new lines. The pages sat exactly as he had left them. The mark on his palm had gone cold too. For the first time since the tomb it looked like nothing more than an old scar catching the wrong light.
Narrator: For one treacherous second he let himself believe it was over.
Narrator: Then he checked his father's compass. The needle no longer pointed into the earth. It pointed back toward OO-rook—steady, almost eager.
Narrator: He did not trust it. He got up anyway.
Narrator: He woke BAH-lahg first. The big man cracked one eye open.
Balag: [groggy] What?
Zik: [quietly] We're leaving.
Narrator: BAH-lahg did not argue. He looked toward the staircase, said nothing, and started rolling his blanket. That silence frightened Zeek more than any argument would have.
Narrator: NEEN-soon agreed before he finished the sentence. She had slept badly; the hollows under her eyes showed it. En-KHEH-doo said nothing at all. Her silence had teeth.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They broke camp in the dark and put their backs to the ridge.
Narrator: For the first few hours the desert played along. The sky went from black to bruised purple to the flat white of morning. Heat crawled up out of the sand in waves you could watch. Their shadows stretched long and blue, then shrank beneath them as the sun climbed. Zeek navigated by the old caravan markers and his father's map, the same way he always had.
Narrator: The first marker was exactly where it should have been.
Narrator: The second was not.
Narrator: He stopped beside a half-buried stone pillar, worn smooth by centuries of wind, and traced the carved symbol with two fingers. Old work. Older than the outpost. But the direction it pointed was plain enough to read without translation.
Narrator: NEEN-soon came up beside him.
Ninsun: [curious] What is it?
Zik: [flatly] Route marker.
Ninsun: [curious] Does it point toward OO-rook?
Zik: [uneasy] It should.
Narrator: She studied the stone.
Ninsun: [skeptical] Should?
Narrator: Zeek looked at the horizon, then back at the marker. It pointed east. OO-rook was west. Had been west every day of his life.
Narrator: BAH-lahg came up behind them.
Balag: [uncertain] Maybe we read it wrong.
Narrator: Zeek did not answer.
Balag: [reassuring] It happens,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Zik: [flatly] Not to me.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo, several paces back, smiled.
Enhedu: [amused] There he is.
Narrator: They kept moving.
Narrator: The well should have been less than an hour ahead. Zeek remembered its position the way he remembered debts: the angle of the dunes, the low ridge of black stone to the south, the broken track leading into the basin. He had marked it twice.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They reached the place the map, the sun, and four separate memories agreed it should be.
Narrator: There was nothing there.
Narrator: Flat sand stretched unbroken to the horizon. No depression. No shadow of what had once been a well. The location itself had simply ceased to exist.
Narrator: BAH-lahg turned in a slow circle.
Balag: [uneasy] We're off course.
Ninsun: [firmly] We're not.
Narrator: NEEN-soon had already taken sightings off the sun and the ridgeline.
Ninsun: [uneasy] The desert has stopped agreeing with itself.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They found the well again when the sun stood directly overhead, throwing no shadows at all. It sat several miles off their line of travel, in a place none of them remembered crossing. The stones around its mouth were wet.
Narrator: Zeek stopped at the edge of the basin. Water sat inside, dark and glass-still. He could smell it from ten feet off—iron, old blood, and a taste underneath both that he did not have a word for and did not want one.
Narrator: BAH-lahg took a step toward it.
Zik: [sharply] Don't,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Balag: [defensive] Wasn't going to drink.
Zik: [quietly] Good.
Narrator: Carved fresh into the stone rim, dust still pale and scattered around the cuts, was the spiral-drop symbol. NEEN-soon crouched and held her fingers a hair above the grooves without touching them.
Ninsun: [uneasy] This was made recently. Hours.
Narrator: Nobody had carved it. They had been together every hour since leaving the ridge.
Narrator: Zeek looked down into the black water. Far below the surface—not a reflection, not a trick of light—a shape moved, and it was not still.
Narrator: He stepped back from the edge.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: That was when the retreat stopped being a plan.
Narrator: BAH-lahg wanted the staircase. NEEN-soon agreed with him at once.
Ninsun: [urgently] It's the only fixed point we have left. Everything else keeps changing under us.
Zik: [quietly] The staircase changed too,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Zik: [grimly] It just didn't disappear.
Balag: [firmly] It's more than we've got anywhere else out here.
Narrator: BAH-lahg nodded.
Balag: [firmly] We go back.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo did not move. She stood apart, staring at the empty shimmer of the horizon.
Enhedu: [intense] You keep calling it a prison because your father did,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Zik: [sharply] What?
Enhedu: [intense] Your father. The journal. The warnings. Every time this place scares you, you decide that means it's a trap.
Zik: [flatly] Most things trying to kill us are traps.
Enhedu: [intense] Most things trying to kill us are honest enough to just attack.
Narrator: NEEN-soon frowned.
Ninsun: [skeptical] That makes no sense.
Enhedu: [intense] It makes perfect sense.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo turned fully toward Zeek. Her eyes had gone bright—not with fear, but with something closer to hunger.
Enhedu: [excited] You keep assuming we need to escape from the First City. What if it isn't?
Zik: [skeptical] What do you think it is, then?
Narrator: She smiled the way a woman smiles when she has already picked her side.
Enhedu: [hungrily] A kingdom.
Narrator: BAH-lahg made a low sound in his throat. NEEN-soon stared at her.
Ninsun: [accusing] You want to go back,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Enhedu: [intense] I want to know what's down there.
Ninsun: [horrified] You want to walk into the place that erased an entire city from history.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo shrugged.
Enhedu: [casually] You say that like it wasn't impressive.
Narrator: Zeek looked between them. BAH-lahg wanted the staircase because walls still made sense to him. NEEN-soon wanted it because she needed one true thing while the world kept lying. En-KHEH-doo wanted it because she had decided the First City was the biggest score of her life—bigger than money, bigger than anything she had ever put a price on.
Narrator: Zeek wanted it because his father had been there.
Narrator: None of them said their real reason out loud. They turned back anyway.
Narrator: The desert did not make the return easy.
Narrator: They tried to pick up the caravan route that should have carried them to the ridge. Instead they found a road. The wind stripped enough dust away to show paving stones the size of coffins, fitted with a precision that made the outpost ruins look like a child's work.
Narrator: NEEN-soon dropped to her knees at one of the joints.
Ninsun: [awed] This is older than the known dynasties.
Balag: [curious] How old?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Ninsun: [quietly] I don't know.
Narrator: The answer seemed to cost her more than any number could have.
Narrator: The road ran the wrong direction—away from OO-rook, away from the ridge, toward nothing they recognized. Beside it, pressed into the sand, were footprints. Bare feet. One set. Fresh.
Narrator: The prints started beside the buried road and led toward the ridge. Zeek followed them until they stopped. No second set. No drag marks. No struggle. Only a strip of faded cloth half-buried where the last footprint ended.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek stopped breathing.
Narrator: He knew it before he touched it. The weave. The dye gone the color of old rust. The ghost of smoke and myrrh still clinging to the fibers after twelve years of desert wind that should have scoured it clean.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: His father's cloak.
Narrator: He picked it up. For one long moment the desert dropped away and he was eleven years old again, huddled beside a dying fire while his father draped this same cloth over his shoulders.
Bel-iddin: [warmly] [wistful] Desert remembers the sun. Doesn't mean it owes you warmth after dark.
Narrator: NEEN-soon watched him.
Ninsun: [gently] Is it his?
Narrator: He nodded.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked toward the ridge.
Balag: [quietly] Then he was here.
Zik: [quietly] [bitterly] It wanted me to think he was.
Enhedu: [flatly] Does it matter which?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Enhedu: [coldly] If your father left it, he was here. If something else placed it, then what's out there knows him well enough to make you believe it. Either way, this place stopped just waiting for us a while ago.
Narrator: The wind moved. The footprints vanished between one blink and the next.
Narrator: The cloth was no longer in Zeek's hand.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Nobody spoke. Then BAH-lahg said,
Balag: [firmly] We're going back,
Narrator: and this time no one argued.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: It took until nightfall to reach the ridge. The outpost was still there. For a moment Zeek let himself believe that was good news.
Narrator: Then he saw the staircase.
Narrator: It had been twenty feet deep that morning. Now the steps dropped away into a darkness that swallowed torchlight before it traveled more than an arm's length—a darkness with weight to it, like the absence of light had learned to push back.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stood at the edge.
Ninsun: [fearful] That wasn't there.
Balag: [softly] Neither was the rest of this,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said quietly.
Narrator: The wall at the bottom was simply gone. Only the black mouth of the stairwell remained, breathing cold up into the night air.
Narrator: Zeek pulled out the compass. The needle pointed straight down. Then, slow as a held breath, it turned and settled on the darkness of the stairwell itself.
Narrator: The journal opened on its own. Lines formed, wet ink crawling across dry paper.
Narrator: You tried to leave.
Narrator: The mark on his palm woke.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: That was wise.
Narrator: NEEN-soon read over his shoulder.
Ninsun: [quietly] That's not comforting.
Zik: [quietly] No.
Narrator: One final line.
Narrator: You should have gone farther.
Narrator: Zeek turned to look back the way they had come.
Narrator: Nothing. Flat sand stretched unbroken in every direction. No footprints. No buried road. No proof any of them had ever stood anywhere but here.
Narrator: BAH-lahg turned a slow circle.
Balag: [fearful] Then how did we get back?
Narrator: Zeek looked down into the stairwell's black throat.
Zik: [quietly] I don't think we did.
Narrator: From somewhere far below came three measured knocks.
Narrator: Three more answered from the open desert behind them.
Narrator: Zeek looked down into the stairwell. Then he turned and looked behind them, at the empty dark where the desert simply stopped being anything at all.
Narrator: There was nothing there.
Narrator: The journal turned a page on its own. A final line bled into the paper, letter by letter.
Narrator: [slowly] YOU ARE ALREADY INSIDE.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: The knocking began again—from everywhere at once.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 26 — IMPOSSIBLE LINES

Narrator: CHAPTER 26
[pause 1s]
Narrator: Impossible Lines
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They descended through the stairwell's impossible dark and stepped into the First City.
Narrator: Or what remained of it.
Narrator: The scale hit first, and it hit wrong. Streets wider than any avenue in OO-rook opened ahead of them, yet the buildings pressed too close, leaning out over the thoroughfares like men bent forward to eavesdrop. Towers rose at angles that should have brought them down centuries ago, upper stories jutting out in layers that made no architectural sense. Bridges crossed themselves in midair—vanishing into one structure and emerging from another several stories higher, as if the city had been built by someone who had only ever heard geometry described secondhand.
Narrator: Every surface carried the same dull, oily sheen as the wall they had pressed against above. Torchlight sank into the stone rather than reflecting from it. The air tasted of dust and something colder, like the inside of a sealed jar that had never been opened.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stopped three paces past the threshold.
Balag: [awed] This isn't a ruin,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon was already turning in a slow circle, eyes moving from street to arch to impossible bridge.
Ninsun: [intently] No. Ruins collapse according to rules. This is still following rules. Just not ours.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said nothing. She was staring upward at a colonnade that continued past the point where any supporting wall should have ended. Her expression was not fear. It was calculation.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek looked back.
Narrator: The stairwell they had descended was gone. In its place stood a solid wall of the same dark stone, seamless, as if the passage had never existed. The only evidence they had come from above was the cold still clinging to their clothes and the faint vibration of the mark on his palm.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: They were inside.
Narrator: No one stated the obvious.
Narrator: They moved carefully along the nearest street. The paving stones were large and fitted with the same impossible precision they had seen on the buried road above. No weeds grew between them. No dust had collected in the joints. The city looked abandoned and yet maintained, as though something still walked these streets after the living had left.
Narrator: Doorways stood too narrow for the buildings they belonged to. Along one wall a row of shallow niches faced downward instead of outward, each the size of a household altar. Names had been scratched out of the lintels above several entrances, the cuts deep and deliberate, as if the act of erasure had mattered more than the names themselves.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stopped at the first intersection and crouched beside a low wall. Carved into the stone at regular intervals were the same incomplete spiral-drop symbols they had seen on the door above. Seven of them. Each slightly different. Each facing a different direction.
Ninsun: [softly] The gates,
Narrator: she said quietly.
Ninsun: [softly] Not metaphors. Actual thresholds. The tablets weren't describing a journey through the underworld. They were describing a map.
Narrator: Zeek studied the nearest carving. The spiral was unfinished in the same place as the one above. A deliberate omission.
Zik: [quietly] What does incomplete mean?
Narrator: he asked.
Narrator: NEEN-soon shook her head.
Ninsun: [intently] The texts disagree. Some say each gate took clothing, then rank, then name. Others mention memory. One damaged tablet claims the last gate took whatever remained of the traveler that still made them recognizable as themselves. The incomplete mark could mean the threshold is still closed. Or that it is waiting for the missing piece. I can't tell which version is older.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's hand rested on the haft of his axe.
Balag: [firmly] Then we don't give it anything.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo laughed once, low.
Enhedu: [laughs] You think it asks politely?
Narrator: They continued.
Narrator: The deeper they walked, the more the geometry refused to settle. A street that appeared straight when viewed from one end curved sharply when they reached its middle. A courtyard they passed on their left reappeared on their right ten minutes later, identical down to a cracked basin in its center. Zeek tried marking their path with small stones. When he looked back, the stones were gone.
Narrator: Or they had never been placed.
Narrator: NEEN-soon kept a running record on a scrap of parchment, noting every turn, every symbol, every impossible junction. After the third time the same courtyard returned, she stopped writing.
Ninsun: [frustrated] The map is changing as we move,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [frustrated] Or we are changing relative to it. I can't tell which.
Narrator: Zeek opened the journal.
Narrator: The pages that had once shown the cross-section of the city beneath OO-rook were different now. The upper layers—the desert, the outpost, the first staircase—had faded almost to invisibility. In their place the ink had thickened into a dense network of streets, chambers, and descending lines that continued far past the edge of the paper. At the center of the densest tangle the spiral-drop symbol appeared again, complete this time, and larger than any they had seen.
Narrator: Beneath it, in his father's hand:
Bel-iddin: THE SECOND DOOR IS NOT A DOOR.
Narrator: Zeek closed the book before anyone else could read it. The choice surprised him even as he made it. The knowledge felt private, like a debt that belonged only to him.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They found the first living thing an hour later.
Narrator: Or what had once been living.
Narrator: A figure stood in the center of a wide plaza, facing a blank wall rather than the open space. Its feet were fused into the paving stones as if the city had grown around them. The body itself seemed assembled from several architectural surfaces—part column, part lintel, part threshold—rather than carved as a single statue. It wore the remains of a linen robe so old the fabric had fused with the dust that covered it. Around its neck hung a single bead of black glass, identical to the one BAH-lahg had left at the well.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon exhaled slowly.
Ninsun: [whispers] [awed] A gatekeeper.
Enhedu: [whispers] Is it dead?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo asked.
Ninsun: [whispers] I don't think dead is the right word.
Narrator: Zeek stepped closer.
Narrator: The figure's head turned while the rest of its body remained locked in the stone. The motion was exact, mechanical, as if the joint had been waiting centuries for the correct pressure. Empty sockets regarded him. The mouth opened.
Narrator: No sound came out.
Narrator: Instead the mark on Zeek's palm flared hot, and a single word formed behind his eyes—not spoken, not written, simply present.
The City: [whispers] Payment.
Narrator: He stepped back.
Narrator: The figure remained motionless, head still turned toward him, waiting.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's voice was very quiet.
Balag: [whispers] [fearful] What does it want?
Narrator: NEEN-soon answered without looking away from the stone face.
Ninsun: [whispers] [ominous] The same thing the tablets said every threshold wants. Something that cannot be bought back.
Narrator: Zeek looked down at his marked hand, then at the incomplete spirals carved into the walls around the plaza, then at the journal pressed against his ribs.
Narrator: The city had let them in.
Narrator: Now it was waiting to see what they would leave behind.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They left the plaza without touching the gatekeeper again.
Narrator: The figure remained exactly as they had found it—head turned, empty sockets fixed on the space Zeek had occupied, black bead resting against stone that had once been a neck. It did not follow. It did not need to. The word Payment still sat behind Zeek's eyes like a coin that refused to be spent.
Narrator: BAH-lahg took the lead, axe ready, moving as if the streets themselves might decide to close. En-KHEH-doo walked slightly apart, eyes cataloguing every doorway, every erased name, every place a valuable object might once have been. NEEN-soon kept close to Zeek, parchment and stylus ready, though she had not written anything since the courtyard repeated itself for the third time.
Narrator: The street they chose ran straight for nearly a hundred paces before it simply ended.
Narrator: Not in a wall. Not in a collapsed building. The paving continued for three more stones and then became blank stone floor, seamless and unmarked, as if the street had never been intended to go farther. On either side the buildings leaned inward until their upper stories nearly touched, forming a tunnel of shadow that smelled of old water and something metallic.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek stopped.
Zik: [uneasy] This wasn't here a moment ago.
Narrator: NEEN-soon checked her notes, then the walls, then the incomplete spiral carved at what should have been the next intersection.
Ninsun: [intently] It was. Or a different version of it was. The symbol has changed orientation.
Narrator: BAH-lahg tested the blank floor with the butt of his axe. The stone gave back a dull, solid sound.
Balag: [grimly] Dead end.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo was already examining the nearest doorway. The lintel above it had once carried a name. Only three characters remained, the rest gouged out so deeply that the stone itself looked wounded.
Enhedu: [quietly] Someone wanted these people forgotten,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [quietly] Or the people wanted to be forgotten,
Narrator: NEEN-soon answered.
Ninsun: [intently] In some of the older tablets, voluntary erasure is listed as a form of payment. A way to pass a threshold without losing something more essential. One damaged account claims that once a name was taken, no one who had known the person could speak it again. The word simply would not form.
Narrator: Zeek looked at the incomplete spiral on the wall. The missing section felt deliberate, like a blank space left for a signature.
Narrator: He opened the journal.
Narrator: The map had shifted again. The dense network of streets now showed a single clear path leading away from the plaza they had just left. The path ended at a mark that was not a spiral. It was a simple vertical line descending into a small open circle.
Narrator: Beneath it, new writing had appeared in a hand that was neither his father's nor his own.
Narrator: [slowly] THE STREET IS A THRESHOLD.
Narrator: [slowly] CROSSING IT REQUIRES A NAME.
Narrator: Zeek closed the book. This time he did not try to hide the act. The others had seen him look.
Ninsun: [urgently] What does it say?
Narrator: NEEN-soon asked.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He told them.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg's expression hardened.
Balag: [tense] We're not giving it names.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo tilted her head.
Enhedu: [ominous] What if it already has them?
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one answered.
Narrator: They stood at the edge of the blank floor for a long time. The air in the unfinished tunnel felt colder than the rest of the City. Torchlight sank into the stone and did not return. Somewhere far behind them, back toward the plaza, a single measured knock sounded—soft, almost polite.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Then another.
Narrator: Zeek stepped onto the first of the final three paving stones.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Nothing happened.
Narrator: He stepped onto the second.
Narrator: The mark on his palm warmed.
Narrator: He stepped onto the third.
Narrator: The blank floor ahead of him changed.
Narrator: It did not open. It did not crack. It simply acquired depth, the way a reflection acquires depth when the light is right. The stone remained stone, yet somehow continued downward into a darkness that had not been there a breath earlier. The incomplete spiral glowed faintly on the vertical face of the drop.
Narrator: A name rose in his mind unbidden—his own full name, the one his father had used only when frightened or trying to make certain he was listening.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zee-keer AHSH-shoor.
Narrator: The City was waiting for him to offer it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's hand closed hard on his shoulder and pulled him backward. En-KHEH-doo made a sharp sound of protest, half-frustrated, half-curious, as if she had wanted to see the threshold complete itself.
Narrator: Zeek closed his mouth and let himself be dragged onto solid ground.
Narrator: The depth vanished. The floor became blank stone again. The mark on his palm cooled.
Narrator: Behind them the knocking continued, steady now, as if counting the heartbeats until someone decided what they were willing to leave behind.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice was barely above a whisper.
Ninsun: [whispers] It almost took it.
Zik: [quietly] [shaken] Almost,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: He looked at the erased names on the lintels, at the gatekeeper still waiting in the plaza they could no longer see, at the three ordinary paving stones that had nearly cost him the only name his father still used when he was afraid.
Narrator: The City had shown them the shape of the next payment.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: It had not yet collected.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 27 — THE MAN WHO REFUSED

Narrator: CHAPTER 27
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Man Who Refused
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They did not return to the plaza.
Narrator: The knocking faded after a time, as if the City had noted their refusal and simply transferred the account to a later column. The three paving stones looked ordinary again. Only the erased names on the lintels remained as proof that someone had once answered when asked.
Narrator: BAH-lahg kept his hand on Zeek's shoulder until they were well clear of the unfinished street.
Balag: [firmly] Next time you test a threshold,
Narrator: he said,
Balag: [firmly] you say so first.
Narrator: Zeek nodded. He still tasted the shape of his own full name at the back of his throat.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo glanced back once at the blank floor.
Enhedu: [uneasy] We still don't know what happens if someone actually pays.
Ninsun: [quietly] We know enough,
Narrator: NEEN-soon answered. She was writing again, stylus moving in short, precise strokes.
Ninsun: [intently] That distinction survives in every damaged tablet that still mentions the gates.
Narrator: They chose a narrower street that at least possessed two walls and a visible far end. The geometry continued to refuse ordinary rules. A covered walkway they passed beneath reappeared ahead of them a short while later, identical down to one cracked tile. Zeek stopped leaving trail markers. There was no longer any point.
Narrator: The deeper they went, the more traces of ordinary life surfaced—and the more wrong those traces felt. A market stall carved directly into a building's side still held the faint outlines of jars that no longer existed. A child's handprint was pressed into a wall at waist height, the fingers too small, the impression too deep. Doorways showed thresholds worn smooth by generations of feet, yet their upper lintels remained sharp, as if the people who lived here had eventually stopped looking up.
Narrator: NEEN-soon slowed beside a low wall covered in faint wedge-shaped marks. She brushed dust away with careful fingers.
Ninsun: [quietly] Inventory,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [quietly] Grain. Oil. Rope. The same lists every household kept.
Narrator: Near the bottom three lines had been crossed out so thoroughly the surface beneath was scraped raw. Beside the erasure someone had later cut a single incomplete spiral.
Ninsun: [softly] They inventoried people,
Narrator: she said quietly.
Narrator: No one asked her to continue.
Narrator: The street narrowed further. Torchlight sank into the oily stone and did not return. Somewhere ahead, water dripped with the slow regularity of a clock that no longer measured hours anyone recognized.
Narrator: Then they heard the voice.
Narrator: It was thin, human, and exhausted, and it came from a side passage so narrow they would have walked past it without noticing.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] Don't give it your name.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's axe was up before the last word finished. En-KHEH-doo had already angled herself for a fight. NEEN-soon froze with the stylus still pressed to parchment.
Narrator: Zeek raised the torch.
Narrator: The passage ended after only a few paces in a shallow alcove. A man sat against the back wall, knees drawn up, wrists resting loosely on them. His clothes were the same dust-fused linen they had seen on the gatekeeper, but his skin was still skin—gray, stretched thin, cracked at the corners of the mouth. His eyes tracked the torchlight with the slow recognition of someone who had not expected living faces again.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [disbelieving] You're real,
Narrator: the man said. His voice sounded unused.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [dryly] I thought the City had finally learned how to make better copies.
Narrator: Zeek crouched just out of reach.
Zik: [cautiously] Who are you?
Narrator: The man gave a one-sided smile.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [dryly] That depends which name you're asking for. The one I arrived with, or the one it left me.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's stylus hovered.
Ninsun: [gently] How long have you been here?
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [weary] I stopped counting after the second winter that never ended.
Narrator: He looked past them toward the main street.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [weary] You're still carrying your names. That's rarer than it should be. Most who reach this depth have already paid at least once.
Narrator: BAH-lahg kept the axe ready.
Balag: [tense] Are there others?
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] There were.
Narrator: The man lifted one thin hand and pointed farther down the narrowing street.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [ominous] The City keeps what it collects. Sometimes it lets the emptier ones walk for a while. Sometimes it uses them as markers. Sometimes it just leaves them where they fall.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo studied him the way she studied anything that might still hold value.
Enhedu: [shrewdly] What did you pay?
Narrator: The man's gaze settled on BAH-lahg.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] The first threshold accepted your offering,
Narrator: he said. Then, to Zeek:
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [weary] First took a knife I inherited. Ordinary steel. Second took a scar I earned above. Third asked for the name of the woman who waited for me. She used to—
Narrator: He stopped. His mouth worked around a word that would not come.
The Man Who Refused: [sadly] I can still see her face,
Narrator: he said at last.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [sadly] I just can't remember what anyone ever called her.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence settled over the alcove.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon spoke carefully.
Ninsun: [carefully] And the fourth?
Narrator: The man looked at Zeek's wrapped hand, at the journal, at the mark that still pulsed faintly beneath the linen.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [weary] The fourth is why I'm still sitting here. I refused. The City does not force the later payments. It waits. And while it waits, the streets rearrange. The people you arrived with begin to forget why they came. Eventually you forget too—or you decide that whatever is left of you is still too expensive to keep.
Narrator: He leaned his head back against the stone.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [gravely] You're marked,
Narrator: he said to Zeek.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [ominous] That means an account is already open. Everything after this is collection.
Narrator: The journal pressed once against Zeek's ribs—deliberate, almost like confirmation.
Narrator: The man closed his eyes.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] Don't give it your name. And if you reach the place where the walls begin to breathe in time with your pulse, turn around. That threshold does not ask. It only records the answer you already gave by arriving.
Narrator: BAH-lahg lowered the axe and offered his hand.
Balag: [gently] Come with us.
Narrator: The man stared at the open hand for a long time.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [weary] I have followed strangers out of this alcove before,
Narrator: he said.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [resigned] They always bring me back.
Narrator: He did not take the hand.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They left him there.
Narrator: As they moved back into the main street, NEEN-soon spoke almost to herself.
Ninsun: [quietly] [shaken] The tablets never mentioned survivors.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Behind them, in the narrow alcove, the man began to hum—soft, tuneless, keeping time with a clock only he could still hear.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 28 — THE CITY BENEATH THE CITY

Narrator: CHAPTER 28
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The City Beneath the City
[pause 2s]
Narrator: They did not return to the plaza, and the City did not force them to.
Narrator: Instead it simply rearranged the streets until every corridor they chose led back to the same narrow alcove. Zeek noticed the first repetition when he saw the cracked tile under the covered walkway for the second time. BAH-lahg noticed it when the same dripping water resumed its patient count. NEEN-soon stopped writing after the third return and simply stared at the wall as though the stone itself had insulted her.
Narrator: The man who had refused still sat where they had left him, knees drawn up, humming under his breath. He did not look surprised to see them again.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [knowingly] You tried the eastern passage,
Narrator: he said. It was not a question.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's axe rested across his shoulders.
Balag: [frustrated] We are walking in circles.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] No,
Narrator: the man answered.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [dryly] You are being returned. Circles would be kinder.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's knife was already in her hand.
Enhedu: [tense] How many times does it do this?
Narrator: The man smiled with half his mouth.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [weary] Until you stop trying to leave the way you came, or until you decide the price of staying is lower than the price of another attempt. Most people choose the second option before the fifth return.
Narrator: Zeek looked down the narrowing street they had just walked. The geometry had already begun to shift again; the far end no longer matched the memory of the last attempt. The mark under the linen on his palm pulsed once, slow and deliberate, as if confirming a ledger entry.
Narrator: They tried a fourth time anyway.
Narrator: This passage led behind the low wall of inventories and down a slope that grew colder with every step. The air tasted of iron and old paper. For several minutes the City allowed them the illusion of progress. Then the floor leveled, the walls widened, and the same alcove waited at the end of the corridor. The man was still humming.
Narrator: BAH-lahg lowered the axe and sat on the paving stones without ceremony.
Balag: [grumbling] I don't like this place.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [dryly] That is sensible,
Narrator: the man said.
Balag: [gruffly] I wasn't asking.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [dryly] The living rarely do.
Narrator: NEEN-soon had already moved to the long panel of wedge marks that ran along the wall opposite the alcove. She brushed dust from the deepest lines with careful fingers. The torchlight sank into the oily stone and did not return.
Ninsun: [intently] These are older than the household inventories,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [intently] Different hand. Different purpose.
Narrator: Zeek stepped closer. The mark on his palm had begun to warm in a way that no longer felt like warning. It felt like recognition.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's stylus hovered above the first line.
Ninsun: [intently] The root forms are familiar. Settlement. Binding. Debt. But the combinations refuse ordinary translation. The first line could mean discovered. Or summoned. Those are not the same thing.
Zik: [quietly] No,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Narrator: She continued, voice growing quieter.
Ninsun: [quietly] The people who sealed the upper city did not build what lies beneath it. The earliest records claim the first settlers entered from above and found roads, wells, storage chambers already waiting. They believed the place had been abandoned.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked at the man in the alcove.
Balag: [uneasy] By whom?
Narrator: NEEN-soon's finger stopped on a character whose edges had eroded.
Ninsun: [uneasy] The word can mean inhabitants. It can also mean remains. Echoes. The dead.
Narrator: The man gave a dry, unused laugh.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [dryly] I preferred it when we were only lost.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's eyes stayed on him.
Enhedu: [suspicious] You know this language.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [gravely] I know what it cost.
Narrator: NEEN-soon forced herself farther along the wall. The next section was more formal, the wedges deeper and more carefully arranged. Phrases repeated with bureaucratic insistence: the agreement, the binding, the debt below.
Ninsun: [intently] Debt is not exact,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [quietly] Obligation. Price. Something owed to the foundation.
Narrator: Zeek studied the spiral-drop that appeared among the characters.
Zik: [quietly] You once told me it was a royal emblem.
Ninsun: [flatly] I was wrong.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice had gone flat.
Ninsun: [flatly] It is a claim of ownership. Not chosen. Assigned.
Narrator: The man in the alcove spoke before she could continue.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [gravely] I know what it means when the mark chooses someone.
Narrator: Zeek turned.
Zik: [sharply] Then say it.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [weary] You would not believe me.
Zik: [insistent] Try.
Narrator: The man's cloudy eyes held his.
The Man Who Refused: [hoarse] [sadly] You are already doing what your father did. He believed knowing the truth would make him free of it.
Narrator: The words landed with the weight of an unpaid account. Zeek said nothing. He had spent twelve years carrying the shape of that belief; hearing it spoken by a stranger did not make it lighter.
Narrator: NEEN-soon reached the final section. The characters here had been cut with force rather than patience. Several surrounding blocks were cracked. She read the first line once, then again, then stopped breathing.
Balag: [tense] What does it say?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Narrator: She looked at Zeek. Whatever he saw in her face drained the last of the anger from his expression.
Ninsun: [stunned] The First City was not the first city.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence settled over the alcove the way dust settles after a collapse.
Narrator: Then the stone moved.
Narrator: A thin groove appeared beneath the final line and carved itself into the black rock without tool or hand. Another line formed beside it. Then another. The City was writing—or correcting.
Narrator: NEEN-soon translated as the words appeared.
Ninsun: [slowly] The first city was built over the second.
Narrator: A new line.
Ninsun: [slowly] [fearful] The second was built over the third.
Narrator: Her breathing quickened.
Ninsun: [breathless] The third was built over the—
Narrator: She stopped.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek stepped closer.
Zik: [urgently] Over what?
Narrator: NEEN-soon stared at the final symbol.
Ninsun: [fearful] The word is present. But there is no place beneath it. No chamber. No city. No depth. Nothing the language is willing to name.
Narrator: The man in the alcove spoke softly.
The Man Who Refused: [fearful] Do not ask what lies beneath the last city.
Narrator: Zeek turned.
Zik: [sharply] Why?
Narrator: For the first time the man's composure cracked. His eyes shifted toward the darkness beyond the alcove, and something that resembled genuine fear moved across the wasted face.
The Man Who Refused: [whispers] Because it may hear you.
Narrator: The first knock sounded from somewhere beyond the chamber.
Narrator: Three measured strikes. Not loud. Not hurried. The sound traveled through the black stone without echoing, through the soles of their feet and the bones of their bodies.
Narrator: NEEN-soon turned slowly toward Zeek. He was staring at his left hand.
Narrator: The mark beneath the linen pulsed—not with his heartbeat. The spiral and the drop had darkened until they were almost black. Beneath the symbol the flesh of his palm began to rise. A thin line appeared under the skin, pushing upward from somewhere deeper than muscle or bone.
Narrator: Then the line moved.
Narrator: A single knock sounded against the inside of his palm.
Narrator: The man closed his eyes.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice dropped to a whisper.
Ninsun: [whispers] The knocks are not coming from the walls anymore.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Zik: [whispers] Then where?
Narrator: She pointed at his hand.
Narrator: The mark knocked again.
Narrator: This time the answer came from far below the City—faint at first, easily mistaken for settling stone or the ordinary shifting of ancient foundations. Then it came again. Three measured strikes.
Narrator: From somewhere beneath them.
Narrator: Zeek's face had gone completely still.
Narrator: The man opened his eyes.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: And somewhere far below the First City, something knocked back.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 29 — WHAT SHE WANTED

Narrator: CHAPTER 29
[pause 1s]
Narrator: What She Wanted
[pause 2s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo moved while the others were still listening to the knock that had answered from beneath the City.
Narrator: Zeek was staring at the new ridge rising under the skin of his palm. NEEN-soon was still translating the last line that had carved itself into the wall. BAH-lahg stood between them and the man in the alcove with the patient stillness of someone who had already decided the ground was wrong. For a few breaths no one watched her.
Narrator: She had spent her whole life noticing the exact moment when attention slipped. She used it the way other people used doors.
Narrator: The passage past the alcove narrowed at once. Walls pressed close enough that her shoulders brushed both sides. The torchlight behind her thinned, then vanished. She kept walking until the voices were gone, swallowed by stone the way everything in this place eventually got swallowed, and only then let herself stop.
Narrator: She did not take the journal. She took the question that had been sitting behind her teeth since the gatekeeper first said the word Payment.
Narrator: What will I possess when I leave this place?
Narrator: She did not speak it aloud. She simply held the thought the way she had once held a lockpick—firm, quiet, without apology—and waited to see whether the City would answer a request that had never been asked of it in words.
Narrator: The air changed first.
Narrator: The stone against her back went warm, then soft, then absent. Blue sky opened overhead, so bright it hurt. She was walking through a gate of white stone veined with gold. Bronze doors stood open. Servants bowed. Above the entrance, cut deep enough to last a thousand years, her own name:
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: [slowly] ENHEDU.
Narrator: The air smelled of clean cistern water. Caravans rolled past loaded with lapis and ivory. Men who had once looked through her now waited in line, hats in hand. A ring slid onto her finger, cool at first and then warm. A house north of the river. Walls high enough to keep the starving world outside where it belonged.
Narrator: A woman stood in the doorway of that house. For one strange second En-KHEH-doo did not recognize her—older, softer, safe in a way that felt like a foreign language. Then she understood. It was her.
Narrator: Coin was the one thing in the world that never pretended to love you first. It had all led here.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo smiled.
Narrator: Then, somewhere behind her ribs, everything went quiet.
Narrator: The change arrived so fast she almost missed it under the noise of her own triumph. She was still standing in the palace. She could still see the gold, still price it out of habit—the ring worth a season's rent, the vase worth a man's life if sold to the right buyer. She knew the house was beautiful. She knew the people around her were powerful.
Narrator: She felt none of it.
Narrator: The fire that had carried her through every bad deal and every worse night—the thing that had kept her moving when moving was the only thing keeping her fed—had simply gone out, like someone had reached into her chest and pinched the wick.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The vision folded.
Narrator: She was back in the narrow dark of the passage, a small golden figurine solid and cold in her open hand. She knew exactly what it was worth. She knew exactly how many throats would be cut for it. She looked at it the way she would look at a chipped cup.
Narrator: Nothing there worth the looking.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: That was when Zeek found her. He appeared at the far end of the passage without sound, the way everything in this place had learned to move. His eyes went from the figurine to her face. Something in her expression told him more than either object did.
Zik: [concerned] En-KHEH-doo?
Narrator: She turned toward him. For the first time since he had known her, she looked lost, and she hated that he was the one who got to see it.
Enhedu: [flatly] [desperate] I know this matters,
Narrator: she said, and held out the figurine like proof.
Narrator: He took it, turned it once in the torchlight. Even here, in a place that swallowed light whole, the gold seemed to drink the dark rather than push it back.
Zik: [concerned] Where did you get this?
Enhedu: [flatly] I don't remember.
Zik: [concerned] You don't remember taking it?
Enhedu: [flatly] I remember the room.
Narrator: Her gaze slid past him into the dark.
Enhedu: [flatly] I remember wanting it.
Zik: [gently] Wanting what?
Enhedu: [flatly] Everything.
Narrator: She looked down at her own empty hands as if they belonged to someone else.
Enhedu: [flatly] I saw the life I wanted. Money. A house. People who couldn't decide I was worthless because they'd never been able to afford me in the first place.
Narrator: Her voice stayed level. Whatever was underneath it had gone completely flat.
Enhedu: [flatly] And then I had it.
Zik: [gently] And?
Enhedu: [flatly] I don't care.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The words sat between them, ugly and small.
Narrator: Zeek's eyes moved to the mark on his own palm, still faintly raised where the knock had pressed outward from inside the skin.
Zik: [quietly] What did you ask?
Enhedu: [flatly] What will I possess when I leave this place.
Narrator: He looked at her for a long moment.
Enhedu: [flatly] You waited for it to ask,
Narrator: she said.
Enhedu: [flatly] I asked first.
Narrator: BAH-lahg and NEEN-soon arrived a minute later, drawn by the sound of voices carrying down the passage. BAH-lahg stopped when he saw the figurine. NEEN-soon's face lit with instinctive scholarly hunger—then died the moment she saw En-KHEH-doo's expression underneath it.
Ninsun: [alarmed] What happened?
Narrator: NEEN-soon asked.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo passed her the figurine without a word.
Narrator: NEEN-soon turned it over, ran a thumb along the tooling.
Ninsun: [excited] This is extraordinarily valuable.
Enhedu: [flatly] Yes.
Ninsun: [puzzled] You don't seem pleased.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's mouth twitched, not quite a smile.
Enhedu: [flatly] I know.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked between the three of them, working through it the way he worked through everything—one slow, honest step at a time.
Balag: [confused] So you got what you wanted.
Enhedu: [flatly] Yes.
Balag: [confused] Then what's the problem? Sell it. Buy the house. Be done with it.
Ninsun: [sharply] BAH-lahg.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice had an edge.
Balag: [defensive] I'm asking a real question.
Narrator: He wasn't being cruel. That was the worst part—he genuinely didn't see it yet, and En-KHEH-doo found she couldn't even be angry at him for that, because an hour ago she wouldn't have seen it either.
Balag: [confused] You wanted things. Now you have one. Isn't that the whole point of wanting?
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked at the figurine in NEEN-soon's hands—gold enough to have bought her out of every bad year she had ever lived—and felt exactly nothing reach for it.
Enhedu: [flatly] It was,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned, sensing he had missed something without being able to name it, and let the silence do what his questions could not.
Narrator: Somewhere beyond the alcove the triple knock began again.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Once.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Twice.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Three times.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo turned toward the sound.
Narrator: She did not reach for her knife.
Narrator: She did not reach for the gold.
Narrator: She simply stood there while the City waited to see what else she no longer cared enough to protect.
Narrator: They moved in uneasy silence after En-KHEH-doo's change.
Narrator: Nobody suggested asking the City another question. Not even En-KHEH-doo, who carried the golden figurine wrapped in cloth the way a man carries a stone in his boot—aware of it with every step, unable to feel anything about it. She knew it was valuable. She remembered wanting it. That was all that remained of the wanting.
Narrator: The passage opened into a chamber with three doors, identical in size and nothing alike in what waited beyond them.
Narrator: The first opened onto a sunlit courtyard. Trees heavy with leaves. Water moving somewhere out of sight. Birds cut through the branches. Laughter drifted from beyond the doorway, easy and unhurried, the sound of people who had never once had to calculate the weight of anything. Beautiful. Completely impossible. They were too deep beneath the First City for sunlight to exist at all, let alone this much of it—warm and gold and entirely unbothered by the stone pressing down from above.
Narrator: The second door opened onto a sheer drop into blackness. No floor beyond the threshold. No far wall. No bottom that torchlight or imagination could find. The dark simply continued, patient as everything else in this place.
Narrator: The third showed the corridor they were already standing in—empty. No Zeek. No BAH-lahg. No En-KHEH-doo. No NEEN-soon. The same stretch of stone reaching away into the distance, untouched and silent, as though the four of them had never set foot in it at all.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stared at the third door longest.
Balag: [uneasy] I don't like that one.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo glanced at him. Some of her old dryness surfaced, thinner than before.
Enhedu: [flatly] You don't like the one showing us exactly what we are.
Balag: [uneasy] I don't like the one showing us nothing.
Enhedu: [flatly] That's because you have simple tastes.
Narrator: His hand drifted toward his axe.
Ninsun: [sharply] Enough,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said. She stood between the three doorways, studying the carvings above each one—symbols she had already copied twice, comparing them against every fragment of the City's language she had catalogued. None of it matched. When she began to trace one familiar root with her stylus, the mark on the parchment went blank under the tip. She stared at the empty space for a long moment, then closed the scroll without comment.
Ninsun: [frustrated] The inscriptions aren't telling us which door to take.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Zik: [curious] Then what are they telling us?
Ninsun: [quietly] I don't know.
