Narrator: She lifted the compass instead. The needle swung from the courtyard to the black drop to the empty corridor and back again, circling once before settling—pointing straight down, through the floor, toward nothing any of them could name.
Narrator: The mark on Zeek's palm pulsed once, slow and deliberate.
Narrator: He stepped toward the third door.
Narrator: BAH-lahg caught his arm.
Balag: [urgently] That one shows us gone.
Zik: [quietly] I know.
Narrator: The words of the man who had refused returned without invitation: That threshold does not ask. It only records the answer you already gave by arriving.
Narrator: Zeek set his foot on the threshold of the empty corridor.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The floor held.
Narrator: He took another step.
Narrator: Behind him the others waited. En-KHEH-doo watched with the flat attention of someone who no longer calculated odds the way she once had. NEEN-soon's stylus hovered over parchment that had nowhere useful left to write. BAH-lahg's grip on the axe was white-knuckled and certain.
Narrator: Zeek looked back once.
Zik: [firmly] Stay behind me.
Narrator: He stepped fully through.
Narrator: The corridor beyond was the same corridor they had left. The same oily stone. The same slow drip of water that measured no hours anyone recognized. For three paces nothing changed.
Narrator: On the fourth the lesson was gone.
Narrator: Not the memory of his father.
Narrator: Not the sound of the old man's voice.
Narrator: Not the desert nights spent learning which stones would hold and which would open.
Narrator: Only the specific knowledge of how to test a floor—the exact sequence of weight and listening and the small adjustments of the ankle that had once been as automatic as breathing.
Narrator: He knew that something important had been taken. He could feel the absence the way he felt the mark under the linen. He knew it had once been the difference between walking and falling. The exact edges of the missing thing were already going soft, the way a word goes soft in the mouth right before it is lost entirely.
Narrator: His body still remembered.
Narrator: His hand still knew how to test the next stone. His feet still knew where not to fall. The lesson remained somewhere the City had not yet reached—buried in muscle and instinct too deep for the current extraction to dig out.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: For now.
Narrator: He stood in the corridor and waited for the others.
Narrator: BAH-lahg came first, then NEEN-soon, then En-KHEH-doo. None of them spoke. The three doors behind them folded shut without a sound, as though the chamber had never held more than one choice to begin with.
Narrator: From somewhere ahead, deeper than the corridor they now occupied, three measured knocks answered the mark.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek kept walking.
Narrator: Behind him En-KHEH-doo carried the gold she no longer wanted.
Narrator: Beside him NEEN-soon carried symbols that no longer yielded meaning.
Narrator: His body still knew which stones would hold.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Zeek no longer knew what it was listening for.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 30 — THE END OF CHOOSING

Narrator: CHAPTER 30
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The End of Choosing
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The City stopped pretending to be passive.
Narrator: It began with the water.
Narrator: They had grown used to the shallow black pools that appeared in chambers and along corridors—one more unpleasant feature of a place that seemed to hold a personal grudge against human comfort. Then the water started moving with intent.
Narrator: When BAH-lahg knelt to refill a waterskin, the surface rippled toward him before he touched it. Small, deliberate rings traveled uphill against the natural slope of the stone. He froze with the skin still in his hand.
Balag: [uneasy] Did you see that?
Narrator: NEEN-soon had already turned.
Ninsun: [quietly] Yes.
Balag: [fearful] It moved.
Ninsun: [dismissively] Water does that.
Balag: [fearful] Not toward a man before he touches it.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one answered.
Narrator: They pressed on, keeping clear of the black pools. A few minutes later BAH-lahg muttered under his breath about the City, the dead, and the questionable intelligence of anyone who walked willingly into a place that had been buried for thousands of years. His voice was low, barely more than breath.
Narrator: It carried strangely down the corridor.
Narrator: A moment later it came back.
Narrator: Not as an echo. The voice was stretched thin and wet, distorted in a way that made Zeek's skin crawl, but the words were unmistakably BAH-lahg's, and they came from somewhere ahead.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg stopped walking.
Narrator: A second repetition rose from behind. A third bled out of the darkness to their left.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stepped toward the nearest wall and held her palm just short of the oily stone.
Ninsun: [intently] It's recording us. Or learning how we sound when we're afraid.
Balag: [defensive] I wasn't afraid,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Narrator: The wall repeated him, word for word, in a voice that had learned his cadence better than he liked.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo laughed once, short and without humor.
Enhedu: [flatly] The City disagrees.
Narrator: Behind them the water rippled again—and this time it spoke in Zeek's voice.
Zik: [eerie] En-KHEH-doo.
Narrator: She spun. Nothing stood behind her. The black surface trembled once and went still. The mark under Zeek's wrappings gave a slow, answering pulse.
Narrator: He did not like that the City had borrowed his voice. He liked even less that it had known exactly whose name to say with it.
Narrator: They moved faster after that.
Narrator: The statues came next.
Narrator: The first stood at the mouth of a side passage they had not noticed before. It was taller than any of them, carved from the same dull, oily stone as the walls, its face blank except for the spiral-drop symbol cut deep into the brow. It did not move when they approached. It simply occupied the space with the absolute patience of something that had nowhere else to be.
Narrator: Zeek tested the floor in front of it out of habit. His body still knew the sequence even if the conscious lesson was gone. The stone held.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: They passed it.
Narrator: When Zeek looked back, the statue had turned its head to watch them leave.
Narrator: No one commented. Commenting would have required deciding what the movement meant.
Narrator: The second statue waited at a junction three turns later. The third appeared in a doorway that had been empty when they last checked. By the fourth they had stopped counting.
Narrator: The City was no longer content to wait behind thresholds. It was rearranging the board.
Narrator: They made camp in a chamber that offered two exits and no pools. BAH-lahg took first watch. NEEN-soon wrote until the stylus refused another line and then sat staring at the blank space as though the parchment itself had begun to withhold cooperation. En-KHEH-doo sat with her back to the wall, the figurine in her lap, looking at it the way a person studies an object whose purpose belongs to someone else.
Narrator: Zeek opened the journal.
Narrator: The map had shifted again. The upper layers—the desert, the outpost, the first staircase—had faded almost to invisibility. In their place the ink had thickened into a denser network of descending lines. At the center of the densest tangle the spiral-drop appeared complete, larger than any they had seen.
Narrator: Beneath it, in a hand that was neither his father's nor his own:
Narrator: [slowly] THE CITY IS TIRED OF WAITING.
Narrator: He closed the book before anyone else could see the words.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Sometime after the others had gone quiet he heard water moving in the dark. When he opened his eyes the statue was standing in the entrance, taller than the doorway, its head forced down beneath the stone arch to fit. The spiral-drop crown sat on its brow. Its carved face pointed straight at him.
Narrator: It did not attack.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: It waited.
Narrator: Zeek's hand went to his knife. The statue did not shift. He looked past it to the doorway, then back at the stone face, then down at the mark on his palm. The mark knocked once, soft and deliberate, against the inside of his skin.
Narrator: The statue's head turned—not toward Zeek, toward the corridor. Toward the way that led deeper.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon rose slowly.
Ninsun: [quietly] It wants us to follow.
Balag: [firmly] Then we go the other way,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said, already moving.
Narrator: They tried. The wall behind them opened onto a corridor that had not existed a breath earlier. It led—of course it led—straight back toward the statue.
Narrator: BAH-lahg swore under his breath.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo stood, picked up the figurine, studied it with the same blank expression she had worn since the vision, and tucked it into her pack.
Ninsun: [concerned] Why are you still taking that?
Narrator: NEEN-soon asked.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked down at it as though the answer might be written somewhere on the gold.
Enhedu: [flatly] I don't know.
Narrator: That silenced them faster than an argument would have.
Narrator: The statue stepped aside.
Narrator: Beyond it the corridor sloped downward into darkness that did not end in torchlight.
Narrator: The City had stopped offering choices.
Narrator: Now it was herding them.
Narrator: The statue did not move when BAH-lahg shoved it.
Narrator: It did not even shift. It felt less like rock and more like part of the foundation itself—an immovable molar set in the jaw of the City. BAH-lahg braced both hands against the cold carved stone and leaned until the muscle stood out across his shoulders and his boots skidded on the grit underfoot.
Balag: [hissing] Move,
Narrator: he hissed.
Narrator: The statue's face remained a mask of placid indifference. It was not attacking. It was not threatening. It stood with the heavy, unthinking purpose of a shepherd's dog blocking a gap in a fence.
Enhedu: [flatly] It won't budge,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said. She leaned against the damp wall of the corridor, hollowed out by exhaustion now that the last of the adrenaline had burned off and left only the bill behind. She was still clutching the figurine, her thumb tracing the same groove across its surface over and over without seeming to know she was doing it.
Balag: [growls] We have to go through,
Narrator: BAH-lahg growled, and backed up to try again.
Ninsun: [firmly] We aren't going through,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said. Her voice was too steady. She was staring at the ceiling.
Narrator: Zeek followed her gaze. The joints between the stones were moving—not much, not enough to notice unless you were already looking for it—slowly, perceptibly tightening, the gaps narrowing like the ceiling itself was drawing breath.
Ninsun: [quietly] We're being steered,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [quietly] The City doesn't want us in the main colonnade anymore. It's closing the loop.
Narrator: BAH-lahg turned from the statue.
Balag: [tense] What does that mean?
Ninsun: [ominous] It means the path behind us is being removed.
Balag: [defiantly] Then we make our own path.
Ninsun: [skeptical] Through that?
Narrator: NEEN-soon nodded at the statue.
Narrator: BAH-lahg glanced back at it, his expression suggesting he was reconsidering several of his life's choices at once, starting with the one that had brought him to this profession.
Narrator: Zeek did not answer. He was looking at the floor instead. The dust here was different—finer, disturbed in long overlapping trails, as though thousands of feet had walked this exact path in a time that was not theirs. The compass in his pocket had stopped merely spinning; it was vibrating now, a frantic hum climbing up through his thigh and into his ribs.
Balag: [urgently] Zeek?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Balag: [urgently] Orders?
Narrator: Zeek looked at the statue's blank face and understood, all at once, that the City had stopped testing them. If they forced the issue now they would spend the last of their strength against something that had never needed to fight them in the first place.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He stepped aside.
Narrator: The statue waited another moment, as if confirming the decision, then turned and began to walk.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: They followed.
Narrator: The corridor it led them down was narrower than the ones they had left, the ceiling lower, the air colder. The walls here carried no incomplete spirals. They carried names.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Thousands of them.
Narrator: The white limestone was covered in shallow, precise cuts—row after row of personal names, some still legible, many crossed out with a single deliberate stroke. Others had been erased so thoroughly that only a pale scar remained in the stone. NEEN-soon slowed, stylus forgotten, eyes moving from line to line with the stricken attention of someone who had spent her life reconstructing lost records and was now watching the process run in reverse.
Ninsun: [softly] These are accounts,
Narrator: she said quietly.
Ninsun: [softly] Not a memorial. A ledger.
Narrator: BAH-lahg kept his axe ready.
Balag: [tense] Of what?
Ninsun: [quietly] People who paid.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's thumb stopped moving on the figurine. She looked at the wall the way she had once looked at locked doors—calculating value even when the desire for value was gone.
Narrator: Zeek walked until he found the section that made his chest tighten.
Narrator: He recognized one of the names. The characters were older than the script NEEN-soon had taught him, but his father had drawn them often enough in the journal that Zeek knew their shape before he understood why.
Narrator: His heart gave a slow, heavy roll. He reached out before he had decided to.
Narrator: The instant his fingers touched the stone the air in the chamber sharpened. A high, thin vibration filled the room—so faint at first he thought it was coming from inside his own skull—and then the sound arrived in earnest.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Scrape.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Pause.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Scrape.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Pause.
Narrator: It came from everywhere at once. The wall was not simply a record. It was active, and it had been waiting for a hand to touch it the way a trap waits for a foot.
Narrator: The name beneath his fingers began to soften at the edges, the limestone rippling faintly as if it had decided, just for him, to remember what it felt like to be liquid. A fresh line cut across it—deep, precise, deliberate. Zeek pulled his hand back, but the scraping did not stop. The line kept growing.
Narrator: He knew that name. He had known it his whole life.
Narrator: The City was not carving anything new.
Narrator: It was erasing the old.
Narrator: He leaned closer. The name was not his. Not yet.
Narrator: It was his father's.
Narrator: The invisible chisel kept working. A second line crossed the first. A third crossed both. The letters disappeared beneath the cuts one stroke at a time. NEEN-soon's voice reached him from somewhere very far away.
Ninsun: [distant] Zeek?
Narrator: He did not answer. He could hear the scraping inside his own chest now, keeping time with something that was not quite his heartbeat. The wall was erasing his father's name, methodically, patiently, the way a debt gets settled by someone who has never once in their life been in a hurry to collect.
Narrator: As the last letters vanished, ink rose in their place.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not another name.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: A symbol.
Narrator: The spiral, and the drop beneath it.
Narrator: Zeek's palm caught fire under the wrapped linen.
Narrator: The statue turned toward him. This movement was different: not directional, not procedural, but attentive. It stepped between Zeek and the wall, unhurried, deliberate, and then it knelt, lowering its massive carved head until the stone face hung level with his own. Its mouth opened.
Narrator: Inside was only darkness.
Narrator: Out of that darkness came a voice that belonged to neither the City nor his father—a voice Zeek had never heard in his life, dry and unimpressed, like a debt collector checking a name off a very long list.
The Statue: [flatly] You are late.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: The mark on his palm knocked once, hard, in answer.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 31 — THE NAME THAT WAS CROSSED OUT

Narrator: CHAPTER 31
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Name That Was Crossed Out
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The wall behind the kneeling statue began to open without a sound.
Narrator: White limestone parted along a seam that had not existed a breath earlier. Cold air moved out of the darkness beyond, carrying the same faint scent of ozone and old copper that clung to the ledger chamber. The statue rose and stepped aside with the same unthinking precision it had shown since the corridor first closed behind them.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: They entered.
Narrator: The chamber beyond was larger than the name corridor and colder. The walls here were still white limestone, still covered in rows of personal names, but the cuts were deeper and the crossed-out lines more frequent. Condensation wept down the stone in thin sheets that smelled of a forge just before the metal comes out of the fire, though there was no heat—only cold that behaved as if it remembered being hot.
Narrator: The grain beneath the moisture slid like sand in an hourglass. Looking away and looking back had become its own kind of danger.
Narrator: Zeek found himself staring at a jagged, freshly etched stroke that looked like a scar cut into the stone.
Zik: [quietly] [distant] BAH-lahg,
Narrator: he said. His voice sounded strangely distant even to himself.
Zik: [quietly] [shaken] It's your name. It's crossed out.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stepped forward. His face had gone pale. He was not a man who scared easily—Zeek had seen him stand his ground against things with teeth and things with blades and things that were neither—but there was a particular kind of fear that came from being told your own name was written somewhere you could not see it, and that fear had him now.
Balag: [shaken] You're losing your mind, Zeek,
Narrator: he said, though there was no real conviction behind it.
Balag: [uncertain] There's nothing there but a blur.
Narrator: He leaned closer anyway.
Narrator: The stone under Zeek's gaze continued to rearrange itself. The crossed-out name that had looked like BAH-lahg's a moment earlier was already softening into something else. A different set of characters rose, then blurred, then settled into a shape NEEN-soon recognized before anyone else did.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: She went still.
Ninsun: [fearful] That one is mine.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked from one of them to the other, then at the wall, calculating even without the desire that had once made calculation feel like survival.
Enhedu: [flatly] Both of you can't be right.
Ninsun: [thin] We can if the ledger is rewriting itself as we watch,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said. Her voice had gone thin.
Ninsun: [thin] Or if it is showing each of us the account it intends to close next.
Narrator: The scraping resumed.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Scrape.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Pause.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Scrape.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Pause.
Narrator: It came from everywhere at once—sometimes from the wall ahead, sometimes from beneath the floor, once, impossibly, from somewhere directly behind Zeek's eyes, as though the sound had found a way to skip the ears and carve straight into the meat of his skull. He had stopped trying to locate it. Guessing implied the room followed rules the room had already refused.
Narrator: BAH-lahg backed away from the wall.
Balag: [fearful] I don't like this place.
Enhedu: [flatly] You said that about the last one,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo answered, but the dryness in her voice was thinner than before.
Narrator: Zeek kept watching the stone. A new name rose under the condensation, held long enough for him to read it, then began to close over itself the way skin closes over a wound. He knew the name. A merchant who sold grain near the Street of Scribes. A man who had once shorted him three measures and laughed when Zeek noticed. An ordinary man under an ordinary sky who, at that very moment, was almost certainly still haggling over prices.
Narrator: It looked less like killing than removing the evidence that anyone had ever been known.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's stylus hovered over parchment that no longer accepted ink the way it once had.
Ninsun: [quietly] It's testing which threads it can pull without the fabric noticing.
Narrator: BAH-lahg shook his head hard, as if the motion itself might dislodge something that had begun to settle.
Balag: [confused] I don't know what you're talking about. Where is the rest of the crew?
Narrator: The question landed harder than any shout could have, because it was not rhetorical. He meant it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked toward the empty space beside them. Recognition broke across her face, incomplete and unstable.
Ninsun: [whispers] KOO-rahsh,
Narrator: she whispered.
Narrator: Zeek followed her gaze. He could almost retrieve the shape—someone younger, ambitious, always calculating his share before the work was finished. The memory arrived in fragments: a voice complaining about the stairs, an extra waterskin that no one had claimed since the ridge, five bedrolls marked in the sand when only four people seemed present. The details had been there the entire time. They had simply stopped belonging to anyone.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo frowned at the empty space.
Enhedu: [flatly] I remember a knife. Not the hand that carried it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stared at them as though they had begun speaking a language he had never learned.
Balag: [confused] Who?
Narrator: The City had not merely been changing the walls around them.
Narrator: It had been cutting pieces directly out of their heads.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not only names.
Narrator: The threads connecting those names to the living.
Narrator: One relationship at a time.
Narrator: Zeek stared at the place where the merchant's name had been. The stone had already smoothed over the letters. His breathing had gone shallow. He looked at NEEN-soon, who was watching his face with a terrible, focused intensity, as though trying to memorize it before it could be taken from her too.
Ninsun: [whispers] Zeek,
Narrator: she whispered.
Ninsun: [whispers] Tell me your name.
Narrator: His mouth opened.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Nothing came out.
Narrator: The answer was there—he knew, with a certainty that had nothing to do with actually possessing it, that it was there, somewhere behind a wall, behind a door, behind something that had already been carved away and smoothed over before he had even noticed the cutting. He looked down at his palm. The wedge-shaped lines beneath the skin crawled like insects disturbed from beneath a stone.
Narrator: For one terrible, suspended second he could not remember whether Zee-keer AHSH-shoor was his name at all, or simply a name he had been told to answer to.
Narrator: The wall behind him began to scrape again.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: This time the chisel was not working on someone else's name.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 32 — THE INVENTORY OF GHOSTS

Narrator: CHAPTER 32
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Inventory of Ghosts
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The scraping stopped before Zeek could turn.
Narrator: Whatever the wall had done to his name, it left no mark he could see. The limestone remained smooth where his own account should have been. Only the residual heat under the linen on his palm told him the chisel had finished its first pass.
Narrator: BAH-lahg was still staring at NEEN-soon.
Balag: [confused] Who?
Narrator: he asked again.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The silence that followed was not empty. It had weight, the particular density of a room holding its breath to see which of them would be the first to disturb it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's grip on his axe had gone loose. He regarded NEEN-soon with the polite, distant curiosity reserved for a stranger bumped into in a crowded market—a flicker of acknowledgment, nothing more, no history behind the eyes at all.
Zik: [quietly] She's a scholar,
Narrator: Zeek said. The words tasted like ash.
Zik: [quietly] NEEN-soon. She's been with us since the gate.
Narrator: He kept his voice level, the way he might speak to a spooked animal, though he was no longer certain whether the effort was for BAH-lahg's benefit or his own.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned. The information seemed to travel a great distance to reach him, losing pieces of itself along the way.
Balag: [skeptical] No,
Narrator: he said slowly.
Balag: [confused] We were three. You, me, and the knife-woman.
Narrator: He gestured vaguely toward En-KHEH-doo, who watched without reacting, the figurine turning slowly in her hands.
Balag: [coldly] I don't know who told you to follow us, but you're a liability.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stepped forward and reached for his arm. He flinched away as though her hand carried contagion.
Balag: [sharply] Don't.
Narrator: Zeek's mind moved through the cold arithmetic of the situation the way he had once turned over the mechanics of a trap before deciding where to place his foot. If the City had edited BAH-lahg's memory, what exactly had it put in the empty space where NEEN-soon used to be? A simple blank? Or an alternate history, complete and self-consistent, in which she had never existed and the three of them had always been three?
Narrator: The second possibility was worse.
Narrator: NEEN-soon turned to him.
Ninsun: [desperate] Tell him.
Zik: [quietly] I already did.
Ninsun: [desperate] Tell him again.
Narrator: Zeek opened his mouth. For a moment the words would not form. The shape of NEEN-soon's presence was still there—the scholar who had argued for knowledge over silver, who had copied the map until her fingers stained, who had watched his face as if trying to memorize it—but the specific reasons she belonged with them had begun to soften at the edges, the way a word softens in the mouth right before it is lost.
Narrator: He stared at her face and felt the absence of a reason even while knowing, with a certainty that had nothing to do with evidence, that there had once been one. The way a man missing a tooth can feel the exact size and shape of the gap without being able to describe the tooth itself.
Ninsun: [whispers] Zeek,
Narrator: NEEN-soon whispered.
Ninsun: [whispers] Don't let it count me.
Narrator: The scratching stopped.
Narrator: Every sound in the chamber vanished with it, all at once, the way a held breath finally lets go.
Narrator: BAH-lahg turned slowly. En-KHEH-doo looked up from the figurine. Zeek felt something move inside BAH-lahg's pack—not shift, not settle, but move, deliberately, the canvas bulging once before subsiding again into stillness.
Narrator: He reached for the journal.
Narrator: The words on the page began to change before his eyes, wet ink spreading and reforming with the slow, deliberate patience of a hand that had all the time in the world:
Narrator: [slowly] THREE WERE SENT INTO THE CITY.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: [slowly] THREE REMAIN.
Narrator: He looked at the three people standing around him—BAH-lahg, En-KHEH-doo, NEEN-soon—and then, finally, at himself.
Narrator: Which of us is not one of the three?
Narrator: Something knocked from inside BAH-lahg's pack.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Once.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Twice.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Three times.
Narrator: Each strike as precise and unhurried as the last.
Narrator: BAH-lahg reached slowly for the straps, his face gone slack with a curiosity that had no fear in it yet. Zeek grabbed his wrist before he could open it.
Zik: [urgently] Don't.
Balag: [confused] Why?
Narrator: Zeek looked down at the journal as the final line finished writing itself, the ink drawing out each letter with excruciating deliberation:
Narrator: [slowly] ONE OF YOU WAS NEVER SENT.
Narrator: The chamber seemed to contract around them. The walls leaned inward as if to hear better. NEEN-soon made a small, strangled sound. En-KHEH-doo's earlier detachment cracked into something closer to alarm. BAH-lahg's face had gone the color of old ash.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: From inside the pack came a fourth knock.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 33 — THE PRICE OF RETENTION

Narrator: CHAPTER 33
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Price of Retention
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The scratching did not return.
Narrator: Something else answered in its place.
Narrator: From inside BAH-lahg's pack the fourth knock still hung in the air, unanswered. No one moved to open the straps. BAH-lahg had gone still in the particular way he did when the ground itself had become untrustworthy. En-KHEH-doo watched the canvas without reaching for it, the figurine turning once in her fingers and then forgotten. NEEN-soon stood two feet from them, hands clenched white at her sides, and neither of the others looked at her.
Narrator: They were not looking through her.
Narrator: They were looking past her entirely, the way a person's eyes slide off a shadow that has not quite decided whether it wants to be noticed.
Balag: [gruffly] We need to move,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said. He stood and slung the pack over one shoulder. The leather straps groaned under the weight of whatever still moved inside it.
Balag: [impatient] Zeek? You coming, or are you going to keep staring at your book?
Narrator: Zeek looked from BAH-lahg to NEEN-soon.
Narrator: She was visibly terrified and just as visibly refusing to stop existing because the world around her had decided she was an inconvenience to be filed away.
Ninsun: [whispers] Zeek,
Narrator: she whispered. Her voice hitched on his name.
Ninsun: [whispers] They don't hear me. It's like I'm not even making sound.
Narrator: He took a slow breath. His hand drifted to the journal, then past it. He had told himself he would not bargain with the City again. He knew exactly what it cost—the holes it punched cleanly through history, the strange smooth empty spaces that remained afterward like scars with nothing underneath them. The worst part had never been the clean forgetting. It was knowing that something important had been taken and having no shape left behind to mourn.
Narrator: Looking at NEEN-soon changed the arithmetic.
Narrator: The shadows in the room seemed to curl toward her. The stone behind her had begun to soften at the edges, the way limestone softened when the ledger was preparing to close an account.
Narrator: He spoke without raising his voice.
Zik: [firmly] Her name is NEEN-soon. She is with us. She stays.
Narrator: For a moment nothing answered.
Narrator: Then BAH-lahg blinked, once, hard, as if something behind his eyes had been forcibly rethreaded. He looked at NEEN-soon with the startled recognition of a man who has just remembered a debt he did not know he owed.
Balag: [confused] NEEN-soon,
Narrator: he said. The name arrived incomplete, as though retrieved from a damaged tablet.
Balag: [confused] Right. The scholar.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's gaze slid toward her and held. Not with warmth. With the remote attention she now gave anything that had been declared relevant by someone else.
Narrator: NEEN-soon exhaled a sound that was almost a sob and almost nothing.
Narrator: Zeek felt the cost arrive a heartbeat later.
Narrator: It was not dramatic. A single clean absence opened somewhere behind his ribs—the specific reason he had decided she was worth the price. He still knew her name. He still knew she belonged with them. He still knew he had paid something irreversible to keep her present in the others' minds.
Narrator: He simply could no longer remember what had made her worth that price in the first place.
Narrator: BAH-lahg shifted the pack on his shoulder. Whatever was inside it had gone quiet.
Balag: [confused] What did you just do, Zeek? Why is she still here?
Narrator: Zeek looked at her, then at the wall of names behind them.
Zik: [quietly] Because I decided she was.
Narrator: He was not entirely sure, even as he said it, whether he believed his own answer.
Narrator: They began walking. NEEN-soon stayed close enough behind him that he could hear the rhythm of her breathing. He knew her name. He knew she belonged with them.
Narrator: The rest was gone.
Narrator: Behind them the chisel began again, unhurried and precise.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: The City kept its records.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 34 — THE WOMAN HE CHOSE

Narrator: CHAPTER 34
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Woman He Chose
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The corridor was a lung.
Narrator: It contracted and expanded around them with a slow, rhythmic wheeze of stone. Dust drifted from the ceiling in thin gray threads, caught and released with each breath the passage took. The oily stone sweated moisture that smelled of copper and wet earth, and beneath that something colder—the smell of anything in this place that had decided to remember them.
Narrator: Zeek walked with one hand resting on his satchel.
Narrator: The journal no longer felt like a book. It felt like a hook set somewhere behind his ribs, its weight tugging with every step. Every step reminded him of the price he had paid to keep NEEN-soon real. Every step also reminded him that the reason for that price was gone.
Narrator: He had always believed that understanding a price preserved some ownership over the transaction. He had traded sleep for profit, honesty for the Collector's coin, comfort for survival. The City had shown him the flaw in that belief. It took the receipt along with the goods.
Narrator: Behind him, NEEN-soon kept pace.
Narrator: Her footsteps were light against the stone. For the first hour after the corridor swallowed the previous chamber he had kept expecting them to disappear—for the rhythm behind him simply to stop, the way a heartbeat stops, without announcement. He kept expecting her shape to blur, her name to slip through his fingers the way water slips through a cracked jar.
Narrator: She was still there.
Narrator: He knew her name–NEEN-soon. Nothing followed the name.
Narrator: He could remember the payment.
Narrator: He no longer remembered why.
Narrator: BAH-lahg walked ahead, axe resting across his shoulders, the pack heavy on his back. Whatever had knocked from inside it had remained quiet since the fourth strike. He had not opened the straps. Curiosity, they had learned, was often a form of consent.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo kept to the side, the golden figurine still in her possession. She simply carried it, as though carrying it had become part of a route whose purpose belonged to someone else.
Narrator: The corridor narrowed without warning.
Narrator: The walls pressed close enough that Zeek's shoulders brushed both sides. Looking back had begun to feel like asking a question the City might answer with a cost. He did not look back.
Narrator: After a time the walls eased. The corridor opened into a wider section scored with shallow parallel grooves, as though something large had been dragged through it more than once. The air grew colder. The copper smell thickened.
Narrator: NEEN-soon spoke for the first time since they had left the ledger chamber.
Ninsun: [quietly] You don't remember why.
Narrator: It was not a question.
Zik: [quietly] No.
Ninsun: [quietly] But you remember that you chose.
Zik: [quietly] Yes.
Narrator: A longer silence. Then:
Ninsun: [vulnerable] Does that still count?
Narrator: He did not answer immediately. Some questions were themselves transactions.
Zik: [quietly] [uncertain] It counts to me,
Narrator: he said at last.
Narrator: He was not sure whether that was true.
Narrator: NEEN-soon did not press. She walked a few paces farther and then said, almost to herself,
Ninsun: [sadly] The tablets never mentioned this part. The part where you keep the person and lose the reason.
Narrator: BAH-lahg glanced back once.
Balag: [impatient] You two finished? The floor ahead looks wrong.
Narrator: Zeek tested it out of habit. His body still knew the sequence even if the conscious lesson was gone. The stone held. They continued.
Narrator: The corridor began to slope more steeply. At one point the torch caught a pale scar in the limestone—the ghost of a name crossed out so thoroughly only the shape of the erasure remained. He did not stop to read it.
Narrator: Behind him NEEN-soon's breathing stayed steady. He counted it without meaning to: the quiet intake, the quieter release. As long as he could hear it, the payment was still holding.
Narrator: He did not know how long payments held in this place.
Narrator: He did not know whether the City considered the transaction closed or merely deferred.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The corridor turned.
Narrator: Ahead the stone opened into a chamber whose far wall was lost in shadow. The air moved differently here—not the breathing of the passage, but a slow circulation that seemed to come from below the floor itself. Four shallow depressions marked the threshold, each the size of a kneeling figure. A fifth depression sat slightly apart, empty and darker than the rest.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg stopped.
Balag: [uneasy] This is new.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo tilted her head, studying the darkness.
Enhedu: [flatly] Or very old.
Narrator: NEEN-soon came up beside Zeek. She did not touch him. She simply stood close enough that he could feel the fact of her presence without needing the memory of its value.
Ninsun: [softly] You chose me,
Narrator: she said quietly. Not accusing. Not grateful. Stating the remaining fact.
Zik: [quietly] I did.
Ninsun: [quietly] Even without the reason.
Zik: [quietly] Even without the reason.
Narrator: She was silent a moment. Then:
Ninsun: [gravely] The City will not stop at one payment. You know that.
Zik: [quietly] I know.
Ninsun: [quietly] When it asks again, what will you give it?
Narrator: Zeek looked into the dark beyond the chamber's edge. The mark under the linen on his palm was warm, patient, waiting.
Zik: [quietly] [uncertain] I don't know.
Narrator: They stood in silence while the City breathed around them.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Then Zeek stepped into the dark, listening for NEEN-soon's next breath.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 35 — THE LIE HE TOLD

Narrator: CHAPTER 35
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Lie He Told
[pause 2s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon caught up within a dozen paces.
Narrator: She did not run and she did not shout. She simply walked alongside him, her presence a weight he had not asked for and could not put down.
Narrator: The corridor stretched ahead, narrow and oppressive. Torchlight died against the darkness before it could reach the walls.
Narrator: Zeek kept his eyes fixed forward.
Ninsun: [flatly] You lied,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Narrator: Her voice contained no anger. That was worse. It was flat and clinical, the voice of someone examining a wound and deciding, with professional detachment, how deep it went and whether it would scar.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek kept walking.
Zik: [defensive] I haven't said anything.
Ninsun: [carefully] You read the journal. You closed it. You decided what the rest of us were allowed to know.
Narrator: She placed each word carefully.
Ninsun: [carefully] That is a lie of a different kind. You have never once refused to show me the words before.
Narrator: He stopped.
Narrator: The journal was warm against his fingers when he pulled it free. The mark under the linen on his palm pulsed once, slow and deliberate, as if the account were taking notice of the intention itself.
Narrator: He intended to open it. He intended to end the accusation with evidence.
Narrator: He did not open it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon watched the decision form on his face.
Ninsun: [quietly] You're still deciding,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [quietly] Even now.
Narrator: BAH-lahg's voice carried from ahead.
Balag: [impatient] If the two of you are going to stand there arguing about paper, the rest of us are moving.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo did not look back. She simply continued walking, the figurine turning once in her hand and then forgotten again.
Narrator: Zeek returned the journal to the satchel. The weight of the decision settled differently this time—not the clean absence of a payment already made, but the heavier, unfinished pressure of a payment deferred.
Narrator: They walked in silence until the corridor forked.
Narrator: Three identical passages opened ahead, each sloping downward at the same angle, each breathing with the same slow rhythm. The torchlight revealed nothing that distinguished one from the others.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stepped forward and studied the stone above each opening. When she began to copy one of the incomplete symbols, the mark on her parchment went blank under the stylus.
Ninsun: [frustrated] They're the same,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [uneasy] Or the City wants us to believe they're the same.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo looked at the three openings with the remote attention she now gave anything that could not be priced.
Enhedu: [flatly] Then we pick one and stop standing here.
Narrator: BAH-lahg shook his head.
Balag: [uneasy] Last time we picked, the walls rearranged themselves behind us. I'd rather know which one is the trap.
Ninsun: [grimly] They're all traps,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Ninsun: [grimly] The only question is which trap the City prefers us to choose.
Narrator: Zeek opened the journal.
Narrator: The page that had been blank a moment earlier now held a single line in the hand that was neither his father's nor his own:
Narrator: [slowly] THE LEFT PATH BELONGS TO THE ONE WHO WAS NEVER SENT.
Narrator: He read it twice. The sentence did not say the path was safe. It did not say the path was dangerous. It only assigned ownership.
Narrator: He closed the book before anyone else could see it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon was watching his face.
Ninsun: [suspicious] What does it say?
Narrator: He could have told her. He could have shown her the page. He could have treated the information as shared rather than private.
Narrator: He looked at the three openings, at BAH-lahg waiting for direction, at En-KHEH-doo already half-turned toward the left-hand passage because someone else would eventually declare one of them relevant.
Narrator: He converted ownership into safety because safety was the word that would make the others follow.
Zik: [evenly] It says the left path is safe.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stared at him.
Narrator: For a moment he thought she might argue, might reach for the journal again, might demand he open it and prove the accusation one way or the other.
Narrator: Then she simply looked away, and something in her posture folded inward, the way paper folds when it has finally given up resisting the crease.
Narrator: Zeek turned toward the left-hand passage.
Narrator: He did not look back.
Narrator: Behind him NEEN-soon remained standing for a long moment before her footsteps finally started again—quieter now, more careful, as though she had learned something about the ground beneath her that she had not known before.
Narrator: Zeek reached the mouth of the left-hand passage and stopped because some remaining part of him expected NEEN-soon beside him.
Narrator: A heartbeat later he could not remember why he had stopped.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He looked back.
Narrator: NEEN-soon was still there, a dozen paces behind him, her arms wrapped around herself against a cold that had nothing to do with temperature.
Narrator: He knew her name.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Then he walked on.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 36 — THE FOURTH MARK

Narrator: CHAPTER 36
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Fourth Mark
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The left-hand passage opened into a chamber that held a silence so absolute it felt like a physical object—something with weight and edges that had settled into the room ahead of them and simply refused to leave.
Narrator: Zeek raised the torch.
Narrator: Two marks already occupied the far wall.
Narrator: The lower one was nearly erased by centuries until it barely resembled a loop at all—more suggestion than symbol, the way a word worn smooth on a gravestone becomes more shape than language. The middle one was clearer, filled with slime and age, its angles deliberate, as though whoever carved it had learned something from a mistake they could no longer remember making.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stepped closer, fingers hovering above the stone without touching it.
Ninsun: [intently] These are not the incomplete spirals,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [quietly] These are attempts.
Narrator: The journal opened in Zeek's hands before he reached for it.
Narrator: [slowly] THIS IS THE THIRD TIME YOU HAVE READ THIS.
Narrator: He stared at the line. Then at the two marks. Then at the blank space above them.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stood near the entrance, axe ready.
Balag: [urgently] Whatever you're reading into those scratches, make it quick. The passage we came through is starting to close.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo studied the wall with the remote attention she now gave anything that could not be priced.
Enhedu: [flatly] Two versions of the same decision. Or two people making the same decision and calling it different names.
Narrator: Zeek understood what the journal required of him the way he had once understood the price of a trap before placing his foot.
Narrator: He took out his knife.
Narrator: The third mark was cut with a kind of desperate precision he recognized in his own aching fingers even as he made it. The lines were cleaner than the oldest attempt, less confident than the middle one. When he stepped back, the three carvings stood in a vertical line: crude, deliberate, desperate.
Zik: [hollow] The oldest is the first attempt,
Narrator: he said. His voice sounded hollow against the stone.
Zik: [hollow] The middle one is the second. And the one I just carved—that was supposed to be the third.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's torchlight moved over the stone in slow arcs.
Ninsun: [uneasy] But the journal said this is the third time you have read this. Not the fourth. The third.
Narrator: The logic assembled itself with a speed that felt almost cruel.
Narrator: If the journal claimed this was the third reading, and the wall already held three marks—two older than his own—then either the journal was counting something different from the carvings, or the carvings recorded attempts the journal refused to acknowledge.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Or both.
Narrator: As they watched, the newest mark began to darken. The pale edges took on the same aged sheen as the middle carving. The stone was absorbing the cut, folding the fresh wound into its history as if the act of carving had never been new.
Narrator: NEEN-soon tried to copy the oldest mark. The ink refused to hold. The line appeared, then faded, then appeared again in a slightly different shape.
Ninsun: [frustrated] It's rewriting itself while I watch.
Narrator: Above Zeek's own mark a fourth position began to form—not a carving yet, only a faint discoloration in the stone, the ghost of a place where something had once been, or would be.
Zik: [quietly] There's a fourth,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon followed his gaze.
Ninsun: [uneasy] I don't see it.
Zik: [ominous] Not yet.
Narrator: The discoloration deepened. A thin line appeared, then another. The shape assembling itself was not a knot. It was something else entirely: a vertical line descending into a small open circle.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Zeek's palm burned.
Narrator: The journal opened again.
Narrator: [slowly] THE FOURTH PLACE IS YOURS.
Narrator: Zeek's blood went cold.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked at him, her voice barely above a whisper.
Ninsun: [whispers] Then whose is the mark already forming?
Narrator: He had no answer that was not another transaction.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The chamber went completely dark.
Narrator: Not gradually. Not as though the torches had failed. Darkness arrived all at once, as though something had reached into the room and closed a hand around every flame.
Narrator: Zeek heard NEEN-soon inhale sharply beside him. The sound seemed unnaturally loud in the sudden absence of light.
Narrator: Then, somewhere beyond the wall, something knocked.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Three measured blows.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: A pause.
[pause 0.6s]
Narrator: Then a fourth.
Narrator: Zeek tightened his grip on the journal. The leather felt warm beneath his fingers.
Zik: [urgently] We need to go.
Balag: [tense] Where?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Narrator: Zeek couldn't see the wall anymore, but he could still feel it, the way a man knows where a doorway stands in a familiar room even with his eyes closed.
Zik: [quietly] The knocking.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one argued.
Narrator: They followed the sound because it was the only direction the darkness had left them.
Narrator: Zeek walked first, one hand sliding along damp stone, the journal pressed beneath his arm. Behind him he heard NEEN-soon's footsteps falter only once.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not from fear.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: From hesitation.
Narrator: As though she had already begun wondering whether they had left something unfinished.
Balag: [dryly] I'd like it recorded,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Balag: [dryly] That I objected to following the invisible thing that knocks.
Enhedu: [flatly] You didn't object,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Enhedu: [flatly] You raised the torch and went first.
Balag: [dryly] I objected internally.
Enhedu: [flatly] That seems convenient.
Balag: [dryly] It saves time. Imagine how slow we'd move if I explained every damn sensible thought I had.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo's mouth shifted at one corner.
Enhedu: [flatly] I've never had to imagine that.
Narrator: Zeek's thumb crossed the scar in his palm. The pressure behind his eyes had diminished after they left the chamber, though it had not vanished. It waited behind his thoughts like the last note of a struck bell, too low to hear but still present in the bone.
Narrator: Behind them came the soft mineral sigh of stone settling into stillness.
Narrator: Zeek resisted the urge to look back.
Narrator: Somehow he knew the fourth mark remained where they had left it.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Not completed.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Waiting.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 37 — THE CHOICE ALREADY MADE

Narrator: CHAPTER 37
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Choice Already Made
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The knocking came again from somewhere ahead of them, three measured blows traveling through the dark with enough force to tremble beneath Zeek's boots. It did not sound nearer than before. The passage had changed direction beneath his hand, bending away from the chamber where the fourth place waited, yet the sound continued to arrive from directly in front of them, as though the City had moved its source to remain useful. BAH-lahg lifted the torch and peered into the narrow turn.
Narrator: Its light showed damp stone, a low ceiling, and nothing that might possess knuckles. Behind them, the journal remained open to the same line.
Narrator: [slowly] THE FOURTH PLACE IS YOURS.
Narrator: The page beneath it was blank.
Narrator: Zeek closed the cover before it could add anything else. The leather resisted for an instant, not enough to stop him but enough to make the action feel noticed. He tucked the journal beneath his arm and followed BAH-lahg into the new passage, leaving NEEN-soon and En-KHEH-doo to fall in behind them. No one suggested another direction. The darkness that had swallowed the chamber had withdrawn only from this route, and even BAH-lahg's suspicion had limits when the alternative was walking into a wall they could no longer see.
Narrator: The corridor descended at a shallow angle. Moisture shone in the seams of the stone, gathering into threads that slipped downward beside them and vanished beneath fitted floor slabs. The walls breathed more faintly here, their movement difficult to distinguish from the tremor left by the knocking. Zeek kept his left hand against the rock as they walked. Each expansion pressed cool stone into his palm; each contraction left a thin space between skin and wall. The rhythm was irregular, unlike the long, deep respirations they had felt elsewhere in the City.
Narrator: It reminded him of someone attempting to remain quiet while in pain.
Narrator: They had gone no more than thirty paces when NEEN-soon stopped.
Narrator: Her boots made no sound beyond the scrape of grit under one heel, yet the interruption passed through the group at once. En-KHEH-doo nearly walked into her. BAH-lahg took two more steps before noticing the torchlight no longer followed, then turned with the expression of a man prepared to dislike whatever came next.
Ninsun: [firmly] We need to return to the wall,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stared at her for several seconds.
Balag: [firmly] No.
Ninsun: [coolly] You can remain here.
Balag: [gruffly] That wasn't permission for the rest of you.
Narrator: She shifted the strap of her satchel higher on her shoulder, still studying the darkness behind them.
Ninsun: [intently] We accepted the inscription too quickly.
Balag: [exasperated] We did not accept it. We left it. That is the opposite of accepting.
Ninsun: [intently] The City gave us a statement. We argued about its meaning, became frightened by its timing, and moved in the direction it provided. That is acceptance in every way that matters.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo leaned one shoulder against the wall, careful to move with its shallow rise and fall.
Enhedu: [flatly] What did we miss?
Ninsun: [quietly] I don't know yet.
Narrator: BAH-lahg let out a quiet breath through his nose.
Balag: [dryly] Those words have become more dangerous than the knocking.
Narrator: NEEN-soon finally faced them. Dust had settled in the folds of her clothes and along the loose strands of hair near her temple. Fatigue showed in the stiffness of her shoulders, but her attention had sharpened since leaving the chamber. Zeek recognized the change. It was the same look she wore when a familiar translation produced an impossible date or when two fragments that should have belonged together refused to meet along the break. Uncertainty did not weaken her. It offended her into precision.
Ninsun: [intently] The first three marks were already there,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [intently] The fourth appeared after Zeek approached the wall. We assumed that made it the next mark in the sequence.
Balag: [defensive] It was above the others,
Narrator: BAH-lahg replied.
Balag: [defensive] It looked unfinished. The journal called it his place. There wasn't much assumption required.
Ninsun: [firmly] There was enough.
Narrator: NEEN-soon took the torch from him before he could object and held it low, illuminating the floor between them.
Ninsun: [intently] Did any of you hear stone being cut?
Narrator: The corridor quieted around the question. Water moved somewhere inside the wall with a thin, dragging sound. Zeek searched his memory again, trying to separate what he had witnessed from what the completed shape had taught him to imagine. He remembered NEEN-soon reading the older carvings, the pressure building behind his eyes, the pale line extending downward. What he could not remember was the sound that should have accompanied it.
Enhedu: [flatly] No,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Enhedu: [flatly] But we've seen the City do louder things without noise.
Ninsun: [sharply] That is not an answer.
Enhedu: [flatly] It's the only one I have.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked at Zeek.
Ninsun: [intently] You were closest.
Zik: [uncertain] I saw the mark appear.
Ninsun: [intently] How?
Narrator: The word caught him.
Zik: [uncertain] It formed.
Ninsun: [pressing] That describes the result.
Narrator: He tried again.
Zik: [uncertain] The stone changed color first. Then there was a line.
Ninsun: [intently] Did the surface split? Did anything fall from it? Did the line sink into the wall, or did the wall withdraw around it?
Narrator: BAH-lahg rubbed a hand over his face.
Balag: [protective] You are asking him to remember the depth of a groove while a dead city assigned him a place among three unknown people.
Ninsun: [firmly] I'm asking because the City relies on us not asking.
Narrator: That silenced him, though not happily.
Narrator: NEEN-soon did not repeat the question. She waited, allowing Zeek to reconstruct the moment without offering words he might borrow. He closed his eyes and saw the chamber in fragments: torchlight unsteady across the carvings, the journal open in his hands, En-KHEH-doo near the threshold, BAH-lahg cursing under his breath. The fourth mark had not entered the wall. That was what troubled him now. He remembered no incision, no shadow forming along an edge.
Narrator: The pale shape had emerged as though it had always existed beneath a thin surface and the stone had merely become unable to conceal it.
Zik: [quietly] I don't think it was carved,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: When he opened his eyes, NEEN-soon was already turning back.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stepped into her path.
Balag: [skeptical] Suppose you're right. What changes?
Ninsun: [firmly] The question.
Balag: [gruffly] That is not worth returning for.
Ninsun: [intense] It may be the only thing worth returning for.
Narrator: She handed the torch back to him, then pointed toward the journal beneath Zeek's arm.
Ninsun: [intense] We keep treating every inscription as language because it wants to be read. We ask what it means, who wrote it, and whether it is telling the truth. We never ask what it is made from, which cuts came first, what tools were used, whether the surface matches the surrounding stone. We have allowed the City to decide the terms of every examination.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo watched her carefully.
Enhedu: [flatly] And now?
Ninsun: [firmly] Now we examine the mark before we obey it any further.
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked down the corridor. The passage remained open, its slight descent disappearing beyond the torchlight. The knocking did not repeat. Its silence made the route ahead feel less like an opportunity than a door being held open by someone growing impatient.
Balag: [skeptical] You believe it will still be there?
Narrator: he asked.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon's expression tightened.
Ninsun: [determined] I believe we should find out.
Narrator: They retraced their steps.
Narrator: The return felt longer than their departure. No new turn had appeared, yet the walls seemed to resist recognition. A crack Zeek remembered passing on his right was now on the left. Moisture ran against the slope in several places, climbing the stone before slipping into the ceiling. BAH-lahg marked each junction with charcoal, though the black strokes faded almost as soon as the torch moved away. He stopped commenting after the second mark disappeared.
Narrator: When they reached the chamber, the darkness withdrew from its entrance in a slow vertical line, revealing the wall as though a curtain had been drawn aside. The three old carvings remained where they had been. Above them, the unfinished fourth mark waited in pale stone, unchanged in shape and position.
Narrator: The journal warmed beneath Zeek's arm.
Narrator: He did not open it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon entered first, lowered her satchel to the floor, and began unfastening its buckles.
Ninsun: [firmly] No one touches the wall yet.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stepped inside behind her.
Balag: [dryly] You say that as though the wall is the one at risk.
Ninsun: [quietly] It may be.
Narrator: She unfolded a square of cloth across the floor and arranged her instruments upon it: measuring rods, waxed cord, charcoal, a small bronze pick, two tablets, and a brush worn almost to the binding. The familiar tools looked absurdly ordinary beneath the City's living walls. That ordinariness steadied Zeek more than the torch. NEEN-soon was not preparing a ritual. She was setting up an excavation.
Narrator: Before reaching for the marks, she knelt beside the base of the wall and brushed dust from the seam where stone met floor. She spread what she gathered across the cloth, separated the pale grains from the dark with the point of the pick, then held one fragment beneath the fourth mark.
Enhedu: [flatly] The colors don't match,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said from the entrance.
Ninsun: [intently] Not yet.
Narrator: NEEN-soon moved to the three completed carvings and brushed beneath them. Small white flakes appeared almost immediately, some no larger than salt, others retaining one sharp surface where a tool had broken them free. She placed those beside the first sample and looked at BAH-lahg.
Ninsun: [satisfied] Now they do.
Narrator: He handed the torch to En-KHEH-doo and crouched opposite her. For all his complaints, his expression changed the moment the stone became a practical question. He picked up one of the fragments, turned it between thick fingers, and pressed it against the lowest carving. The color matched closely enough that it seemed to disappear where he held it.
Balag: [intently] The old cuts left waste,
Narrator: he said.
Balag: [approvingly] Not much. Whoever made them knew the work.
Ninsun: [intently] And the fourth?
Narrator: He examined the dust beneath the pale groove again.
Balag: [intently] Nothing that belongs to it.
Narrator: NEEN-soon used the measuring rod to test the width and depth of the three lower marks. Their differences were slight but consistent, each cut narrowing as it descended, shaped by repeated blows from a small chisel held at an angle. BAH-lahg pointed out where the tool had slipped and been corrected. NEEN-soon found a section where the mason had deepened one side to compensate for a flaw in the stone. The marks had seemed crude when read as symbols. Examined as work, they became evidence of a careful hand.
Narrator: The fourth refused every comparison.
Narrator: Its width did not change. Its depth remained identical from the upper line to the open circle below, though there were no fractures along its edges and no roughness at the bottom. The paleness was not merely a fresh surface exposed by cutting. When NEEN-soon scraped gently inside the groove, the powder beneath it remained white. A finger's width away, the same motion produced dust the color of wet ash.
Narrator: BAH-lahg studied the two smears on the cloth.
Balag: [uneasy] It wasn't carved.
Ninsun: [quietly] No,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Ninsun: [quietly] [ominous] It was revealed.
Narrator: Zeek moved nearer despite the pressure gathering behind his eyes.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon noticed.
Ninsun: [intently] What did you feel?
Zik: [evasive] Nothing.
Ninsun: [pointedly] That wasn't nothing.
Zik: [uneasy] The pressure is returning. It eased in the corridor. It grew stronger when we came back.
Ninsun: [intently] Does the mark feel familiar?
Narrator: He knew the symbol. He had seen it scratched beneath the compass lid in the tomb, half-hidden under the dent along the rim. He had not understood it then. He still did not understand it now. He only recognized it the way a man recognizes a voice from behind a closed door, before he has any chance to place it consciously.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo shifted the torch, bringing the fourth mark into steadier light.
Enhedu: [flatly] Ask him whether it feels unfamiliar.
Narrator: Zeek looked at her.
Enhedu: [flatly] There is a difference,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: The pressure sharpened. Familiar was not the right word. Familiar things carried memories. The mark carried expectation. It resembled the place on a page where a word had not yet been written but where the sentence already required one.
Zik: [uneasy] It feels prepared,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: BAH-lahg gave a quiet, humorless laugh.
Balag: [laughs] That is worse.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stretched the waxed cord between the centers of the lower carvings and extended the line upward. The fourth mark sat slightly outside the sequence. Not misplaced. Adjusted.
Enhedu: [flatly] It was made for him,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Narrator: NEEN-soon wound the cord around her fingers.
Ninsun: [quietly] It was made around him.
Narrator: Zeek disliked the distinction immediately.
Zik: [uneasy] What does that mean?

Narrator: BAH-lahg rose and paced once across the chamber, constrained by the small space and his own unwillingness to approach the wall again.
Balag: [firmly] Then we leave it alone.
Ninsun: [firmly] That is what it expects.

Narrator: He turned on her.
Balag: [tense] You use that word whenever you want to make danger sound respectable.

Narrator: BAH-lahg gestured toward the wall.

Narrator: NEEN-soon folded the cloth over the dust samples and packed away the measuring tools one at a time.
Ninsun: [quietly] The part where it still needs us to recognize the mark.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo came to stand beside her.
Enhedu: [flatly] You think recognition completes it.

Narrator: BAH-lahg crossed his arms.
Balag: [sarcastic] And your proposed scholarly response?
Narrator: NEEN-soon drew her knife.
Narrator: The blade had dulled since the desert. Its edge bore chips from locks, stone seams, and more than one use its maker had not intended. She tested its point against her thumb, then approached the wall. Zeek moved between her and the mark before he had consciously decided to do so.
Zik: [alarmed] What are you going to carve?

Zik: [worried] You don't know what changing it will do.
Ninsun: [firmly] That is why it is a test.
Zik: [uneasy] To whom?
Narrator: Her gaze remained on the pale groove beyond his shoulder.
Ninsun: [quietly] To the record.
Narrator: The journal warmed against his ribs. Pages shifted inside it with a faint, dry murmur.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo heard them.
Enhedu: [flatly] The book objects.
Ninsun: [firmly] The book comments,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Ninsun: [firmly] We are the ones who keep mistaking commentary for law.
Narrator: Zeek did not move.

Narrator: There was no comfort in the answer, but there was sense. NEEN-soon had not promised safety. None of them trusted promises anymore.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He stepped aside.
Narrator: She placed the point of the knife against the upper half of the unfinished symbol and drew the blade downward across it. The scrape seemed louder than the knocking had been. The wall resisted her hand in ordinary ways.
Narrator: She made a second stroke in the opposite direction, crossing the first.
Narrator: The uneven X obscured the pale mark. For the first time, the wall carried a human correction.
Narrator: The City did nothing.
Narrator: The walls continued their weak, irregular breathing. No tremor moved through the floor. The journal remained closed, and the pressure behind Zeek's eyes held steady rather than increasing.
Narrator: BAH-lahg waited with his arms crossed. When nothing followed, he looked toward the ceiling as though expecting the City to require encouragement.
Balag: [dryly] Is there a customary period before an archaeological experiment explodes?
Narrator: NEEN-soon wiped the knife on the cloth.
Ninsun: [dryly] Not usually.
Balag: [dryly] Good. I would hate to discover we had been doing them improperly.
Narrator: They remained long enough for NEEN-soon to measure the fresh cuts and brush the chips into a separate fold of cloth. [pause 1.5s]
Narrator: At last NEEN-soon packed her tools.
Balag: [incredulous] That is all?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Ninsun: [primly] That is all we can observe here.

Ninsun: [firmly] My conclusion is that the alteration remained.
Balag: [uneasy] For how long?
Narrator: NEEN-soon tightened the satchel strap.
Ninsun: [firmly] We will find out.
Narrator: The words carried something close to relief. Zeek felt it too, though he did not trust it enough to name. The pressure behind his eyes had begun to recede. Narrator: BAH-lahg retrieved the torch from En-KHEH-doo.

Enhedu: [flatly] To what?

Enhedu: [flatly] You no longer hate them?

[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They left the chamber together. This time the darkness did not close immediately behind them. The opening remained visible through the first turn, and torchlight continued to catch the pale wall whenever Zeek looked back. The X held its shape.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: By the second turn, BAH-lahg had begun talking about food. It was not a serious plan. None of them knew how long their remaining supplies would last, and the City's changing passages made distance meaningless. Enhedu: [flatly] You cannot name roasted lamb in a place that smells like wet graves,
Narrator: she said.
Balag: [cheerfully] I can name it anywhere.
Enhedu: [flatly] You included onions.
Balag: [cheerfully] They were essential.
Enhedu: [flatly] You've made the air worse.

Narrator: NEEN-soon walked ahead of Zeek, one hand trailing near the wall without touching it. The set of her shoulders had loosened. She listened without joining them, though when BAH-lahg claimed no respectable kitchen used fewer than three kinds of pepper, Zeek saw her smile.
Narrator: The sight disturbed him at first because he had forgotten how ordinary it was.
Narrator: Their relief did not arrive as celebration. Zeek allowed himself to breathe without matching the walls. He shifted the journal beneath his arm and found it cool again.
Narrator: Perhaps refusal could be recorded without being defeated. Perhaps a mark could be damaged and remain damaged. Perhaps the City, for all its reach, was not without limits.
Narrator: They had gone far enough that the chamber should have disappeared beyond several turns when NEEN-soon stopped for the second time.
Narrator: This halt was different. She did not announce a decision or study the corridor ahead. She turned slowly toward the passage behind them, her attention fixed on the stone at shoulder height.
Narrator: BAH-lahg lowered the torch.
Balag: [alert] What is it?
Ninsun: [uneasy] My charcoal.
Narrator: A black line crossed the wall where BAH-lahg had marked their route. Zeek remembered watching it fade before they reached the chamber. Now it was visible again, though no longer black. The stroke had sunk into the stone and dulled to the same gray-brown as an old mineral stain.
Narrator: BAH-lahg touched it. His thumb came away clean.
Balag: [uneasy] That was charcoal.
Ninsun: [quietly] It still is,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Balag: [uneasy] No.
Narrator: He rubbed harder. Nothing smeared.

[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: They found the second around the corner. Like the first, it no longer lay on the surface. The stone had absorbed its shape, preserving the gesture while removing the material that made it theirs. Fine dust had settled in the line, and a thread of mineral growth crossed one end.
Narrator: NEEN-soon hurried toward the chamber without speaking.
Narrator: The corridor lengthened beneath their feet. Turns appeared farther apart than Zeek remembered, and the ceiling sank low enough to force BAH-lahg to duck. The City's breathing had stopped entirely. Without it, the silence felt sealed.

Narrator: They reached the chamber together.
Narrator: The darkness had not returned. The wall stood exposed in the torchlight, the three old carvings arranged beneath the fourth. NEEN-soon's X remained across it, but the cuts were no longer fresh. Their edges had rounded. Gray dust filled the grooves. A hairline fracture ran through the lower stroke and vanished beneath a patch of pale mineral crust. The X looked older than NEEN-soon, older than any of them, as though it had waited on the wall for the same centuries as the three completed marks.
Narrator: She crossed the chamber and pressed her fingers into one of the lines. A thin shell of hardened dust broke beneath her nail. She lifted the fragment into the torchlight, turned it over, then tested another section of the groove with the bronze pick. More crust broke loose beneath it.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stood behind her, breathing heavily from the return.
Balag: [breathless] [shaken] That is not possible.
Enhedu: [flatly] The alteration remained,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Narrator: No one mistook the words for reassurance.

Ninsun: [intently] It didn't heal the wall.
Zik: [quietly] It aged it,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Ninsun: [quietly] No.
Narrator: She looked at the old X and then at the preserved charcoal marks behind them.
Ninsun: [awed] It accepted it.
Narrator: The journal opened under Zeek's arm.

Narrator: Ink rose through the paper, the letters surfacing from the fibers in the same manner as the fourth mark had surfaced from stone.
Narrator: [slowly] THE REFUSAL WAS RECORDED.
Narrator: NEEN-soon read the sentence once, then looked from the page to the ancient X.
Ninsun: [quietly] [fearful] It kept it.
Narrator: The torch went out.
Narrator: Darkness did not fall from above or advance from the corridor. Ninsun: [whispers] [urgently] Don't move,
Narrator: she said.
Ninsun: [whispers] [urgently] No one moves.
Narrator: Something knocked from the other side of the wall.
Narrator: Three measured blows sounded directly behind Zeek's head.
Narrator: He turned despite her warning. His hand found only empty air where the carved wall should have been. The floor remained beneath his feet, but the chamber's boundaries had vanished. Narrator: A voice spoke close to his left ear.
The Remainder: [whispers] [exhausted] Do you remember the key?
Narrator: Zeek froze. The voice was not BAH-lahg's, NEEN-soon's, or En-KHEH-doo's. It sounded worn rather than old, each word roughened by disuse. Beneath that wear lay something he almost recognized, a cadence that made the scar in his palm tighten.

Narrator: Zeek's fingers closed around the object on the floor. It was long and narrow, with a toothed end and a circular bow crusted in something that felt like dried salt.

Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
