Narrator: Zeek inserted the key.
Narrator: For a moment it would not turn. The crust around its teeth scraped inside the mechanism, shedding brittle flakes across his fingers. Then something deeper in the door shifted, and the key moved by itself beneath his hand.
Narrator: The lock opened with a small, ordinary click.
Narrator: Air rushed through the seams, damp and warm, carrying the mineral smell of deep stone. The door swung inward without being touched. Beyond it, a narrow corridor descended between walls that expanded and contracted in slow, deliberate breaths.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Behind them, the journal's pages began turning in the dark.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 38 — THE RULE THAT NEVER CHANGES

Narrator: CHAPTER 38
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Rule That Never Changes
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The corridors breathed faster now.
Narrator: Earlier, the City's slow, deliberate rhythm had reminded Zeek of an animal asleep beneath the earth, something so vast that each breath took half a minute to complete. That illusion was gone. The inhalations came unevenly now, broken by long pauses and shallow gasps that echoed through the stone. The silence between them had become worse than the sound itself. Every pause carried the expectation that the next breath might never come.
Narrator: NEEN-soon walked ahead without looking back.
Narrator: She had stopped trying to hide her exhaustion hours ago. Dust clung to the hem of her robe, and dried blood darkened one sleeve where sharp stone had opened the skin. Yet there was nothing unsteady about her pace. She studied the floor as she moved, holding her torch low enough that its light skimmed across the polished stone instead of disappearing into the darkness overhead.
Narrator: Zeek followed a few paces behind with the journal tucked beneath one arm. It had remained unnaturally quiet since the chamber of names, and somehow that unsettled him more than its interruptions ever had. The silence felt deliberate, as though the book were waiting for something it already knew would happen.
Narrator: BAH-lahg broke the silence first.
Balag: [tired] [grumbling] I've had enough of corridors.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one answered.
Balag: [grumbling] I'm serious. If I ever get out of this place, I'm building a house with one room. No passages. No bloody hallways. You open the door, there's the room. That's civilized.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo glanced sideways at him.
Enhedu: [flatly] And where would you sleep?
Balag: [dryly] In the room.
Enhedu: [flatly] And where would you cook?
Balag: [dryly] The room.
Narrator: She shook her head.
Enhedu: [flatly] You'd burn your own house down.
Balag: [casually] Maybe.
Narrator: He shrugged.
Balag: [casually] Still sounds better than this.
Narrator: His voice faded as the passage narrowed.
Narrator: The walls had changed. Earlier they had been cut with the straight, deliberate lines of worked masonry, but here the stone curved inward in slow, uneven folds. Moisture gathered in shallow depressions and reflected the torchlight in dull patches that looked almost metallic. Every few steps Zeek caught himself studying those reflections, convinced for an instant that something had shifted just beyond the reach of the light.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Nothing ever had.
Narrator: The pressure behind his eyes returned without warning.
Narrator: It wasn't pain—not yet. It was the same tightening he had learned to recognize since entering the City, a sensation that began somewhere behind thought itself. Every question he allowed himself to form seemed to brush against it.
Narrator: What is the fourth place?
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: The pressure sharpened.
Narrator: What happens when—
Narrator: He stopped the thought before it reached its end.
Narrator: Immediately the sensation eased.
Narrator: NEEN-soon spoke without turning.
Ninsun: [quietly] You felt it.
Zik: [quietly] Yes.
Ninsun: [quietly] It isn't reading your mind.
Narrator: She let her fingertips trail lightly across the wall as she walked.
Ninsun: [intently] It doesn't care what you know. It cares about the part of you that's still trying to know more.
Narrator: BAH-lahg frowned.
Balag: [skeptical] That's a ridiculous sentence.
Ninsun: [dryly] I know.
Balag: [gruffly] Then explain it like I'm an idiot.
Ninsun: [patiently] You've never touched a hot cooking pot twice.
Balag: [defensive] I have.
Narrator: She looked over her shoulder.
Ninsun: [pointedly] On purpose?
Narrator: BAH-lahg hesitated.
Balag: [hesitant] …No.
Ninsun: [gently] You lost the part of yourself that believed it was worth trying again.
Narrator: He scratched at his beard.
Balag: [grudgingly] …That's annoyingly sensible.
Ninsun: [quietly] It isn't the question that's dangerous,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said.
Ninsun: [quietly] [ominous] It's whatever survives inside you after the answer.
Narrator: The corridor ended so abruptly that BAH-lahg nearly walked into her.
Narrator: All four of them stood before a blank wall of black stone.
Narrator: It was unmistakably the same material they had found beneath the desert months earlier: smooth, almost oily to the touch, without mortar joints or visible tool marks. There was no doorway, no crack, no inscription. Only a single symbol had been carved into its center—a vertical line descending into an open circle.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: For several moments no one spoke.
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo stepped forward first, lifting her torch until the flame almost touched the carving.
Enhedu: [flatly] The cuts are clean.
Narrator: NEEN-soon crouched without replying. She set her satchel aside and brushed a layer of dust away from the base of the wall with the edge of her sleeve.
Zik: [curious] What are you looking for?
Narrator: Zeek asked.
Ninsun: [intently] Anything.
Narrator: She leaned closer.
Ninsun: [intently] The City repeats itself. If this place mattered before we arrived, someone else came here.
Balag: [curious] And if they did?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Ninsun: [firmly] They left evidence.
Narrator: He snorted softly but crouched beside her anyway.
Narrator: The chamber fell quiet except for the distant breathing that rolled through the stone.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Then NEEN-soon stopped.
Ninsun: [excited] There.
Narrator: She wasn't looking at the symbol.
Narrator: She pointed at the floor.
Ninsun: [intently] There are scratches.
Narrator: Zeek knelt beside her. At first he saw nothing beyond dust and worn stone. Then the torch shifted, and faint parallel grooves appeared beneath the light.
Narrator: BAH-lahg ran a thumb across one of them.
Balag: [intently] Dragged.
Zik: [curious] What?
Balag: [grimly] Something heavy.
Narrator: He studied the grooves for another moment.
Balag: [knowingly] I've seen marks like these moving limestone blocks.
Narrator: NEEN-soon brushed away more dust.
Ninsun: [intently] The scratches stop.
Balag: [dismissively] They reached the wall,
Narrator: BAH-lahg said.
Ninsun: [firmly] No.
Narrator: She swept away another strip of dust.
Ninsun: [intently] They stop before they reach it.
Narrator: The grooves ended in a perfectly straight line a handspan short of the carved symbol. Beyond that point the stone remained untouched, as smooth as polished glass.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg leaned closer.
Balag: [curious] What difference does that make?
Narrator: NEEN-soon sat back slowly.
Ninsun: [intently] If they'd abandoned the work, the marks would wander. They'd hesitate. Correct themselves. Whoever made these stopped exactly where they intended.
Balag: [skeptical] They were interrupted.
Ninsun: [firmly] No.
Balag: [skeptical] You don't know that.
Ninsun: [firmly] I know stone.
Narrator: She looked up at him.
Ninsun: [quietly] [ominous] And stone remembers.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: No one answered.
Narrator: Zeek found himself staring at the narrow strip of untouched floor between the scratches and the wall. It could not have measured more than a handspan, yet it seemed impossibly wide. Whoever had dragged something here had reached the final distance…and never crossed it.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He wondered why.
Narrator: The pressure behind his eyes returned instantly.
Narrator: He drew a slow breath and forced the thought away.
Narrator: The sensation faded before it became pain.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon noticed.
Ninsun: [concerned] It happens sooner now.
Zik: [defensive] I wasn't asking anything.
Ninsun: [knowingly] You were about to.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence settled over the chamber again.
Narrator: The City's breathing seemed slower now, deeper somehow. Zeek couldn't tell whether the sound had actually changed or whether the stillness around them had simply made it easier to hear. The vibration beneath his boots came and went with each distant breath, subtle enough that he might have imagined it.
Narrator: Then something shifted beneath his arm.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He frowned.
Narrator: The journal had not slipped.
Narrator: It had become heavier.
Narrator: Almost reluctantly, he drew it into both hands.
Zik: [alarmed] I didn't—
Narrator: The leather cover eased open before he could finish speaking.
Narrator: No hidden mechanism revealed itself. No invisible force tore the pages apart. The journal simply opened to a blank sheet with the quiet certainty of a ledger being consulted by someone who already knew where the next entry belonged.
Narrator: The parchment remained empty for several heartbeats.
Narrator: Then dark ink began to rise through the fibers.
Narrator: None of them moved.
Narrator: The letters formed themselves with deliberate precision until the first line stood complete.
Narrator: [slowly] THE RULE DOES NOT CHANGE.
Narrator: Another line emerged beneath it.
Narrator: [slowly] YOU ALREADY KNOW WHAT HAPPENS IF YOU ASK.
Narrator: BAH-lahg took an involuntary step backward.
Balag: [fearful] Tell me the rest of you can see that.
Enhedu: [flatly] [whispers] I can,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo whispered.
Narrator: NEEN-soon never looked away from the page.
Ninsun: [intently] It isn't answering a question.
Balag: [uneasy] What is it answering?
Narrator: BAH-lahg asked.
Ninsun: [quietly] Our presence.
Narrator: She glanced toward the scratches.
Ninsun: [ominous] We came looking for an answer. That was enough.
Narrator: Zeek stared at the words until they blurred.
Narrator: They didn't feel like a threat.
Narrator: They felt like a record.
Zik: [whispers] I know,
Narrator: he whispered.
Narrator: The pressure behind his eyes vanished not because he had obeyed, but because something inside the City had acknowledged him.
Narrator: A low vibration moved through the floor before anyone could speak. It began beneath Zeek's boots and spread outward, passing through the chamber in widening pulses that set dust trembling along the base of the wall. The sound was too deep to hear clearly, but he felt it in his teeth and in the hollow behind his ribs. Above them, stone ground against stone with the slow, deliberate force of something testing its jaw.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg looked up.
Balag: [tense] That sounds bad.
Enhedu: [flatly] It is bad,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said.
Balag: [tense] [dryly] I was trying not to commit us to that conclusion.
Narrator: The ceiling shifted.
Narrator: Not far. No more than the width of a finger. Yet fragments of black grit fell across NEEN-soon's shoulders, and the torch flame narrowed until it became a blue thread. The carved symbol in the wall seemed to deepen as the light failed, its open circle turning into a dark mouth.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon rose quickly.
Ninsun: [urgently] Zeek, close the journal.
Narrator: He tried.
Narrator: The cover resisted him.
Narrator: It was not locked, and nothing visible held it open, but the leather had become rigid beneath his hands. The pages continued to darken around the message, thin veins of ink spreading outward from the words as though the writing had roots.
Zik: [strained] I can't.
Ninsun: [urgently] Then drop it.
Narrator: He loosened his grip. The journal remained against his palms.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: BAH-lahg stared.
Balag: [disturbed] That is deeply unpleasant.
Narrator: The vibration increased. Somewhere behind the blank wall, a mechanism began to move with the grinding weight of buried architecture. The sound did not come from one direction. It surrounded them, passing through floor, ceiling, and walls alike until the chamber seemed less like a room than the inside of a machine waking after centuries of neglect.
Narrator: NEEN-soon seized Zeek's wrist.
Ninsun: [urgently] What did you answer?
Zik: [strained] Nothing.
Ninsun: [pressing] You said you knew.
Zik: [quietly] I do.
Ninsun: [sharply] That is not nothing.
Narrator: The torch went out.
Narrator: Darkness filled the chamber so completely that Zeek lost all sense of distance. NEEN-soon's hand remained around his wrist for half a second longer, then slipped away—not pulled, not torn from him, simply absent. The grinding ceased at the same instant, leaving behind a silence so absolute that the sound of his own breathing seemed indecently loud.
Narrator: A latch lifted somewhere behind him.
Narrator: The click was small and ordinary. That made it worse.
Narrator: Zeek did not turn. He could feel the open space at his back where no open space had existed before, a change in the air no wider than a doorway. Cold moved across the nape of his neck. It carried the dry smell of old paper, stone dust, and a faint trace of sweat he recognized as his own.
The Remainder: [exhausted] Zeek,
Narrator: said a voice near his right shoulder.
Narrator: It sounded exactly like him.
Narrator: His hand found the knife at his belt, though some part of him already knew the blade would be useless. A knife belonged to a world in which flesh could be opened and blood could be spilled. Whatever stood behind him had arrived from somewhere those rules had already failed.
The Remainder: [exhausted] Do you remember the key?
Narrator: the voice asked.
Narrator: Zeek kept his eyes fixed on the darkness where NEEN-soon had been.
Zik: [quietly] [tense] No.
Narrator: The answer came too quickly. The thing behind him noticed.
The Remainder: [exhausted] You remember losing it.
Ninsun: [trembling] [shouts] Zeek!
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice broke across the chamber, distant and strained.
Ninsun: [trembling] Move away from the wall.
Zik: [confused] I'm not near it.
Ninsun: [urgently] You're touching it.
Narrator: He looked down, though there was nothing to see. His free hand hung at his side. The journal was still pressed against the other.
Zik: [confused] I'm not touching anything.
Narrator: A dull impact sounded somewhere to his left, followed by BAH-lahg swearing.
Enhedu: [flatly] Everyone stop moving,
Narrator: En-KHEH-doo said. Her voice came from much closer than NEEN-soon's, though Zeek could not tell whether she stood two steps away or was speaking directly into his ear.
Enhedu: [flatly] The floor is changing.
Balag: [sharply] The floor is not changing,
Narrator: BAH-lahg snapped.
Balag: [sharply] The wall is.
Enhedu: [flatly] What wall?
Balag: [strained] [angrily] The one trying to crush me, damn it.
Narrator: Their voices overlapped without occupying the same space. NEEN-soon shouted Zeek's name again, but the sound stretched strangely at the edges, as though the City were pulling her farther away while leaving her words behind.
Narrator: The voice behind him spoke more softly.
The Remainder: [exhausted] They cannot see what you see.
Zik: [tense] I can't see anything.
The Remainder: [exhausted] Neither could I, the first time.
Narrator: Zeek drew the knife.
Narrator: The sound of steel leaving leather seemed to travel far beyond the chamber. For a moment he heard it repeated in the distance: one blade drawn, then another, then another, each echo slightly later than the last.
Narrator: The voice gave a tired laugh. It was his laugh, stripped of amusement.
The Remainder: [exhausted] You still do that.
Zik: [whispers] [tense] Who are you?
The Remainder: [exhausted] I am the remainder.
Narrator: The words passed through him with the weight of recognition. He did not understand them, yet his body reacted before his mind did. His grip tightened around the knife. His scarred palm began to ache where it pressed against the journal.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon cried out.
Narrator: Zeek turned toward the sound despite every instinct warning him not to.
Narrator: A dim gray light had appeared within the chamber, though it came from no torch or opening. It revealed NEEN-soon several paces away with both hands braced against empty air. Blood ran across her fingers. She shoved with all her strength, teeth clenched, as if holding back a wall only she could feel.
Ninsun: [gasps] Help me,
Narrator: she gasped.
Ninsun: [gasps] It's closing.
Narrator: BAH-lahg stood beyond her, facing another direction entirely. He swung his hammer against the floor, but each blow stopped in midair with a heavy crack, as though striking stone invisible to everyone else. En-KHEH-doo was on her knees between them, one hand stretched out before her while the other covered her mouth. Her eyes followed something moving across the ceiling.
Narrator: They occupied the same chamber and no longer shared it.
Narrator: The remainder stepped into the gray light.
Narrator: Zeek saw the outline first: narrow shoulders beneath a torn coat, one arm held close to the body, the posture of a man who had learned to conserve strength because no one was coming to replace it. The face emerged last.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: His own face.
Narrator: Older, though not by years Zeek could count. The cheeks had hollowed. The lips were split. Dust had settled permanently into the lines around the eyes. What disturbed him most was not the resemblance, but the absence of surprise. The figure looked at him with the exhausted patience of someone watching an event recur exactly as expected.
Zik: [shaken] You are not me,
Narrator: Zeek said.
The Remainder: [exhausted] Not anymore.
Narrator: The remainder lifted one hand. A scar twisted across the wrist in a jagged knot, identical to the mark Zeek had carved into the stone—or remembered carving, though the memory had become uncertain enough that he no longer trusted its shape.
Zik: [urgently] I paid at the gate,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Zik: [desperate] I gave the City a memory. I gave it the reason I chose NEEN-soon. I gave it everything it asked for.
The Remainder: [exhausted] You paid with what you understood.
Zik: [desperate] What else is there?
The Remainder: [exhausted] What you left behind.
Narrator: The remainder opened its hand.
Narrator: A small object rested across the palm, shaped from the same calcified black stone as the City's oldest walls. It resembled the carved symbol: a narrow stem ending in an open circle, rough along one edge where it appeared to have been broken from something larger.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: A key.
Narrator: Zeek stared at it. The pressure behind his eyes returned, but it did not sharpen into pain. It held itself at the threshold, waiting.
The Remainder: [exhausted] The City does not forgive incomplete transactions,
Narrator: the remainder said.
The Remainder: [exhausted] You crossed the first gate without understanding the price. Every payment since has postponed collection. Nothing more.
Zik: [quietly] And you are what it collected?
The Remainder: [exhausted] I am what it could not use.
Narrator: Behind them, NEEN-soon screamed as her arms buckled. BAH-lahg shouted for En-KHEH-doo, who did not answer. The chamber shifted again—not physically, but in the arrangement of its possibilities. For one instant Zeek saw the wall split into three overlapping shapes: one whole, one collapsing, one already open. Then they merged.
Narrator: The remainder stepped closer and held out the key.
The Remainder: [exhausted] Take it.
Narrator: Zeek did not move.
Zik: [quietly] What happens to you?
The Remainder: [exhausted] You stop asking that question.
Zik: [sharply] That is not an answer.
The Remainder: [exhausted] It is the only one the City permits.
Narrator: The journal trembled in his hand. Fresh ink rose beneath the earlier message, but the letters formed and erased themselves too quickly to read. The pages seemed to be arguing with one another, revising the moment before it could settle into a single truth.
Narrator: The remainder's expression tightened. For the first time, impatience broke through the exhaustion.
The Remainder: [impatiently] [exhausted] Take the damned key, Zeek.
Narrator: He looked at the offered object, then at the matching scar on the man's wrist.
Zik: [quietly] How long have you been here?
Narrator: The remainder glanced toward the blank wall.
The Remainder: [exhausted] Long enough to stop measuring.
Zik: [quietly] That could mean days.
The Remainder: [exhausted] It could.
Zik: [quietly] Years?
The Remainder: [exhausted] It could.
Zik: [realizing] Or this has happened before.
Narrator: The remainder said nothing.
Narrator: That silence answered him.
Narrator: NEEN-soon's voice came again, weaker now.
Ninsun: [weakly] [desperate] Zeek, please.
Narrator: He reached out.
Narrator: The key was colder than stone should have been. The moment it touched his palm, the scar there burned, and the chamber lurched around him. For an instant he stood on both sides of the transaction: receiving the key and surrendering it, arriving and remaining, hearing his own voice ask a question he had already answered.
Narrator: The remainder closed Zeek's fingers around the object with surprising gentleness.
The Remainder: [pleading] [exhausted] Don't make me wait here again.
Narrator: A seam of pale light appeared in the wall behind him. It ran from floor to ceiling in a line no wider than a hair, then widened with a sigh of displaced air. The black stone folded inward without hinges, revealing a narrow passage beyond.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stumbled forward as the invisible weight released her. BAH-lahg's hammer struck the floor with enough force to jar it from his hands, while En-KHEH-doo collapsed onto one hip and stared at the ceiling as though expecting it to follow.
Narrator: None of them looked at the remainder.
Narrator: They could not see him.
Narrator: NEEN-soon saw the key instead.
Ninsun: [breathless] Where did you get that?
Narrator: Zeek glanced toward the figure.
Narrator: The remainder was already fading into the dark, not vanishing, but becoming less distinct as the new doorway brightened. His face remained visible longest.
The Remainder: [exhausted] Choose,
Narrator: he said.
Narrator: The pressure behind Zeek's eyes flared. The journal snapped shut against his palm, trapping the unfinished writing between its pages.
Narrator: NEEN-soon reached for him.
Ninsun: [desperate] Zeek.
Narrator: Beyond the doorway, pale light spilled across a descending stair. Behind him, the City resumed its ragged breathing, each inhale pulling darkness toward the open passage.
Narrator: The remainder stepped back into that darkness.
The Remainder: [exhausted] The City is a much worse artist than you are,
Narrator: he said.
The Remainder: [exhausted] It doesn't understand mercy. It only understands completion.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: Zeek closed his hand around the key.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 39 — THE LIBRARY OF DRAFTS

Narrator: CHAPTER 39
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Library of Drafts
[pause 2s]
Narrator: The library was not a place of learning. It was a graveyard of drafts.
Narrator: Zeek stumbled through the doorway as it sealed behind them, the calcified key fused to his left hand scraping against the obsidian floor. Gray necrosis had climbed past his elbow now, spreading in delicate branching lines beneath his skin. Every heartbeat forced something thick and cold through the veins that remained. It no longer felt like blood. The sensation had settled into something stranger, as though ink itself had replaced whatever the City had already taken.
Narrator: Ahead of him, NEEN-soon slowed just enough to study the room before continuing. She no longer glanced toward corners or searched for hidden exits. Somewhere during their descent she had abandoned the hope that every chamber concealed another way out. Now she looked only for patterns—for repetitions in the stonework, for symbols carved where they did not belong, for the quiet logic that held the City together beneath all its contradictions.
Narrator: The chamber stretched beyond the reach of the torchlight. Black shelves rose in impossible rows, climbing into darkness so complete that they appeared to dissolve before reaching a ceiling. Upon them rested thousands of glass jars, each identical in size despite containing scenes that could not possibly fit inside. Some glowed with a pale, rhythmic light while others remained dark, yet even those seemed to pulse faintly whenever the City's distant breathing rolled through the floor beneath their feet.
Narrator: There was no smell of parchment or old leather, none of the familiar scents a library should have carried. The air tasted instead of dry stone, cold glass, and something faintly metallic that lingered at the back of Zeek's tongue.
Narrator: It was only when he turned to speak—some half-formed instruction, the sort of thing he would once have tossed over his shoulder expecting BAH-lahg to answer before he had finished—that he realized how completely the silence had settled behind them.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: He stopped.
Zik: [weakly] [uneasy] BAH-lahg.
Narrator: NEEN-soon looked back at him, distracted.
Ninsun: [distracted] What?
Zik: [weakly] [uneasy] BAH-lahg.
Narrator: He frowned.
Zik: [weakly] [slowly] And En-KHEH-doo.
Narrator: She waited.
Zik: [weakly] [uneasy] When did we lose them?
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: The question hung between them longer than it should have. NEEN-soon opened her mouth to answer, hesitated, then looked back toward the doorway as though she expected the two of them to emerge from the darkness laughing at some joke neither of them had heard.
Ninsun: [confused] I don't…
Narrator: She stopped.
Ninsun: [uncertain] The corridor with the teeth. Before that, I think.
Zik: [weakly] [uneasy] You think?
Narrator: She pressed a hand against her temple.
Ninsun: [uncertain] I remember BAH-lahg complaining about the climb. I remember En-KHEH-doo arguing with him about something. Or maybe that was earlier.
Narrator: Her expression tightened.
Ninsun: [shaken] No. That's wrong.
Narrator: Zeek waited.
Ninsun: [quietly] I don't remember a moment they weren't with us,
Narrator: she said quietly.
Ninsun: [quietly] But I also can't remember a single moment they were.
Narrator: The words struck him harder than he expected.
Zik: [weakly] [shaken] That's impossible,
Narrator: he said.
Zik: [weakly] [insistent] BAH-lahg doesn't just stop talking. En-KHEH-doo doesn't go five minutes without pocketing something she insists has no value.
Ninsun: [quietly] I know.
Zik: [weakly] [quietly] No.
Narrator: He shook his head.
Zik: [weakly] [unconvinced] We would have noticed.
Narrator: Wouldn't we?
Narrator: He turned back toward the doorway. The polished floor behind them lay undisturbed except for two sets of footprints leading into the library. Two. Not four.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: His pulse quickened.
Narrator: He searched the room anyway, walking back to where the doorway had vanished, expecting to find a dropped pack, a broken weapon, some mark that proved memory had failed before reality had.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: There was nothing.
Narrator: Only as he turned away did the weight pulling at his shoulder begin to feel unfamiliar. He shrugged off his pack and opened it. Tucked carefully beneath his bedroll lay BAH-lahg's spare axe head, wrapped in the same strip of cloth the big man always kept oiled to keep rust away. Zeek stared at it, certain it had not been there before.
Zik: [weakly] [surprised] I've got something.
Narrator: NEEN-soon had opened her own satchel. She reached inside and withdrew a small copper figurine no taller than her thumb. Time had polished the little smiling face nearly smooth.
Zik: [weakly] [quietly] En-KHEH-doo's,
Narrator: Zeek said immediately.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon nodded.
Ninsun: [wistful] She always claimed it was worthless.
Zik: [weakly] [sadly] So nobody would think to steal it.
Narrator: Neither of them remembered packing either object.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: For a long moment NEEN-soon simply turned the figurine over in her hand, her thumb tracing the worn edges as though expecting the memory of its weight to return with enough patience.
Narrator: Finally she closed her fingers around it and slipped it back into her satchel.
Ninsun: [firmly] We didn't leave them.
Narrator: Zeek looked once more at the empty floor behind them.
Zik: [weakly] [quietly] No.
Narrator: Her voice dropped almost to a whisper.
Ninsun: [whispers] We just stopped being able to keep them.
Narrator: She did not sound convinced by the sentence. She sounded like she had found the only words that fit around something neither of them yet understood.
Narrator: They stood there, listening for the break in the silence, the sound of heavy footsteps or En-KHEH-doo's dry voice reminding them that they were fools.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Nothing came.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: At last NEEN-soon adjusted the strap of her satchel and started forward again.
Ninsun: [firmly] We keep moving,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: Stopping never once persuaded the City to explain.
Narrator: The shelves grew stranger the deeper they walked.
Narrator: At first Zeek tried to make sense of them, searching for some order in the endless rows of glass. The labels suggested one existed. Every jar bore the same careful hand, each line etched into the base with deliberate precision. Subject. Variant. Outcome. Occasionally a number. Occasionally a single word. Whatever system governed the collection had never been intended for visitors. It assumed the reader already understood the rules.
Narrator: The jars themselves refused explanation.
Narrator: One contained nothing but a chair beneath a narrow window. Dust drifted lazily through a shaft of afternoon light that never changed, though no breeze stirred it. Another held a child carefully stacking smooth black stones into a tower that collapsed each time the last piece was set in place. The child never showed frustration. He simply began again, every movement identical to the last. Farther along, a woman sat before a table covered in maps, crossing out one route after another until the parchment itself disappeared beneath the ink.
Narrator: None of the figures acknowledged the world beyond the glass.
Zik: [weakly] [uneasy] They aren't moving,
Narrator: Zeek said.
Ninsun: [quietly] They are.
Narrator: NEEN-soon had stopped before another shelf.
Ninsun: [awed] They're just… moving very slowly.
Narrator: He joined her. Inside the nearest jar a man stood in the middle of a desert, one hand raised to shield his eyes. Zeek watched him for several seconds before noticing that the figure's fingers had shifted almost imperceptibly. It was movement so gradual he might have mistaken it for imagination had he not been staring directly at it.
Zik: [weakly] [awed] They're alive?
Ninsun: [quietly] I don't know.
Narrator: She frowned.
Ninsun: [thoughtful] I don't even know if that's the right question.
Narrator: Her torchlight drifted across the labels until she found one that seemed to catch her attention. She leaned closer, brushing a thin film of gray dust from the glass with the back of her fingers.
Ninsun: [quietly] Here.
Narrator: Inside stood a miniature version of Zeek.
Narrator: He held a lantern before a doorway cut into black stone. The flame illuminated only his face. Everything beyond the threshold remained swallowed by darkness.
Narrator: NEEN-soon pointed toward the inscription.
Narrator: [slowly] SUBJECT 402
Narrator: [slowly] VARIANT: STOIC
Narrator: [slowly] OUTCOME: FAILED
Narrator: [slowly] ERASURE: IMMINENT
Narrator: Neither of them spoke immediately.
Narrator: The tiny figure remained frozen with the lantern held aloft, waiting for a door that never opened.
Ninsun: [quietly] We aren't the first,
Narrator: NEEN-soon said at last.
Narrator: Zeek kept staring at the miniature version of himself.
Zik: [weakly] [uneasy] What are we, then?
Narrator: She rested her hand lightly against the cool surface of the glass.
Ninsun: [gently] Not replacements.
Zik: [weakly] [uncertain] No?
Ninsun: [thoughtful] If we were, there would be no reason to keep the others.
Narrator: He looked along the endless shelves.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Thousands.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: Perhaps more.
Narrator: His torchlight reached only the nearest rows before disappearing into darkness crowded with more glass than any library could have held.
Ninsun: [softly] These aren't graves,
Narrator: NEEN-soon continued quietly.
Ninsun: [softly] A grave marks something that's finished.
Zik: [weakly] [quietly] Then what are they?
Ninsun: [hesitant] I think…
Narrator: She hesitated, an unusual uncertainty creeping into her voice.
Ninsun: [hesitant] I think they're corrections.
Narrator: She withdrew her hand.
Ninsun: [quietly] Or attempts at correction.
Zik: [weakly] [grimly] The City keeps trying until something works.
Ninsun: [quietly] Perhaps.
Narrator: She looked down another aisle where hundreds more jars disappeared into shadow.

Narrator: Zeek had always imagined failure as something clean. A wrong decision. A closed door. A body buried beneath enough years that the living eventually stopped looking over their shoulders.
Narrator: The City had found another use for it.
Narrator: It had archived failure.
Narrator: Every abandoned path remained here, catalogued with the same care another civilization might have reserved for kings or sacred texts.
Narrator: They continued through aisle after aisle, saying little. Some labels bore only a number. Others carried a single word beneath the outcome.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: [slowly] FAILED.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: [slowly] INCOMPLETE.

Narrator: [slowly] PENDING.

Narrator: Near the end of the next aisle something caught his eye.

Narrator: One jar sat slightly apart from the rest.
Narrator: The figure inside was not him. He recognized the man before he recognized his own certainty.

Narrator: His father.
Narrator: The tiny figure knelt in an endless stretch of sand, tapping the ground ahead with a walking stick before taking each careful step. Tap. Step. Tap. Step. Patient. Methodical. Exactly as he had taught Zeek when he was young enough to think the desert itself enjoyed swallowing careless men.
Narrator: Without realizing it, Zeek stepped closer.
Narrator: His breath fogged the glass.
Narrator: He read the label anyway.
Narrator: [slowly] SUBJECT 001

Ninsun: [gently] That could mean many things.

Narrator: He cut himself off before frustration found a target it didn't deserve.

Narrator: he said quietly.

Narrator: If Subject 001 still existed somewhere within the City, then perhaps nothing it collected was ever entirely gone.

Narrator: Zeek said.

Ninsun: [thoughtful] Everything we've seen so far has already happened. Someone preserved it. Recorded it. Perhaps we're reaching the place where those decisions are made.
Narrator: The last row ended without ceremony.
Narrator: Beyond it stretched an open chamber, almost austere after the impossible abundance of the library behind them. There were no shelves here, no jars, no endless catalog of forgotten lives. At its center stood a single desk raised upon a pedestal fashioned from pale bone fused into black stone. Whatever had once belonged to either material had long since disappeared. They had become something new, seamless enough that Zeek could no longer tell where one ended and the other began.
Narrator: Unlike the shelves behind them, the desk bore no dust.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: It looked used.
Narrator: Not recently. Not often. But regularly enough that the gray powder coating the rest of the library had never been allowed to settle there.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon slowed.
Ninsun: [quietly] This isn't a reading room.
Zik: [weakly] [quietly] No.
Zik: [weakly] [awed] It's where the shelves begin.
Narrator: Zeek wasn't sure why he believed that, only that the certainty arrived before the thought itself. The library behind them no longer felt like a collection.
Narrator: It felt like the result of whatever happened here.
Narrator: Resting upon the desk lay an enormous ledger opened to its middle pages. Beside it rested a single sharpened quill carved from the same calcified material as the key fused to Zeek's hand. They were not identical in shape, yet they belonged to one another as surely as a lock belonged to its key. The same gray veins ran through them. The same unnatural cold seemed to draw warmth from the air around them.
Narrator: Neither of them reached for either object.
Narrator: Instead Zeek stepped to the side of the desk and looked down at the open page.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: His stomach tightened.
Narrator: The handwriting was his.
Narrator: Not merely familiar, but unmistakable.
Narrator: The loops of the letters, the slight rightward slant, the habit of crossing a stroke twice whenever he wrote too quickly. He had spent enough nights copying inventories and caravan accounts to know his own hand better than his own face.
Narrator: He had never written any of it.
Narrator: The final entry described their arrival in the library. It described the empty shelves waiting beyond the jars. It described the disappearance of BAH-lahg and En-KHEH-doo, the discovery of Subject 001, and words that still echoed in the chamber around them.
Narrator: The ink remained wet.
Narrator: Zeek reached toward the page before thinking better of it.
Zik: [weakly] [quietly] It isn't prophecy.
Narrator: NEEN-soon didn't answer immediately. She was studying the edge of the writing, watching where the ink thinned into the parchment.
Ninsun: [quietly] No,
Narrator: she said at last.
Ninsun: [slowly] Prophecy is written before it happens.
Narrator: Her eyes lifted to his.
Ninsun: [quietly] This is happening.
Narrator: Neither of them spoke.
Narrator: The blank pages that followed seemed far more unsettling than the words already written. Narrator: Zeek looked at the quill.
Narrator: It was carved from the same calcified stone that had become part of his hand. The same gray veins ran through it. The same impossible cold seemed to gather around it.
Zik: [weakly] [uneasy] I don't think it wants one.
Narrator: NEEN-soon never took her eyes off the open page.
Ninsun: [quietly] I think it expects one.
Narrator: He lowered his gaze to the final line.
Narrator: Unlike the rest of the entry, it stood alone:
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: [slowly] THE AUTHOR MUST BE CONSUMED.
[pause 3s]


CHAPTER 40 — THE AUTHOR’S TOLL

Narrator: CHAPTER 40
[pause 1s]
Narrator: The Author's Toll
[pause 2s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon read the final line again because the words refused to become more sensible through repetition.
Narrator: [slowly] THE AUTHOR MUST BE CONSUMED.
Narrator: The ink remained wet beneath the library's colorless light. It had pooled slightly at the hooks of the letters, as if the Ledger had written in haste, though nothing else in the chamber suggested urgency. The jars stood motionless on their shelves. The tiny figures within them continued their unfinished gestures, repeating movements too small to make a sound. Even the walls had quieted. NEEN-soon could hear Zeek's breathing beside her and, beneath it, the faint settling of the copper figurine in her fist whenever her grip shifted.
Narrator: Zeek had not moved since the sentence appeared. His left hand remained fixed to the calcified key resting against the Ledger, the distinction between his skin and the pale mineral around it nearly gone. The grayness that had begun at his fingertips now crossed the back of his hand in branching lines, climbed his wrist, and vanished beneath his sleeve. More of it showed above his collar. It followed the veins of his throat like writing worked beneath the skin by someone who had no patience for spacing.
Narrator: NEEN-soon forced herself to look away from him and examine the desk.
Narrator: The Ledger occupied most of its surface. Blank pages waited beyond the command, their edges clean despite the dust coating everything around them. The quill lay to the right, set parallel to the book's spine with the care of an instrument prepared before a procedure. Its shaft had the same chalk-white texture as the key fused to Zeek's hand. No inkpot stood nearby. There were no stains on the desk, no discarded nibs, no scraped practice marks. Yet the final sentence shone as if written seconds ago.
Ninsun: [sharply] Don't touch it,
Narrator: she said.
Narrator: Zeek's eyes remained on the quill.
Zik: [weakly] [defensive] I haven't.
Ninsun: [sharply] You were about to.
Zik: [weakly] [flatly] I was thinking.
Ninsun: [dryly] With your hand halfway across the desk.
Narrator: He withdrew his right hand by the width of two fingers. Ninsun: [dryly] I think guessing is how people die in ruins.
Zik: [weakly] [dryly] Investigation hasn't done much better for us.
Ninsun: [dryly] No, but it has made our deaths more informed.
Narrator: The answer almost drew something from him. Not a smile, exactly, but the beginning of the expression he used when BAH-lahg said something offensive enough to deserve admiration. Ninsun: [firmly] Help me turn the page,
Narrator: she said.

Zik: [weakly] [dryly] With what control?

Narrator: NEEN-soon opened her mouth and found no useful answer.
Narrator: Neither of them remembered losing BAH-lahg or En-KHEH-doo. The library had not dragged them away. Ninsun: [tearfully] [laughs] She hated not being paid.
Zik: [weakly] [fondly] Then tell her I still owe her.
Ninsun: [tearfully] You can tell her yourself.
Narrator: The quill stopped.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: Silence gathered so completely that NEEN-soon heard the small metal knock of the figurine against the buckle inside her pocket.
Narrator: A final ruled line impressed itself into the page.
Narrator: Zeek's hand began to move again.
Narrator: The first letter had barely formed when he said,
Zik: [weakly] [urgently] Go.
Narrator: She held his gaze.
Narrator: His voice came more quietly.
Zik: [weakly] [quietly] You know what this place does when we hesitate.
Narrator: Behind him, the jar that had held Subject 001 stood dark upon its shelf. The dash on its label remained, but the name beside it had blurred beyond recognition. NEEN-soon looked from the jar to the Ledger and understood that the library was not waiting for her agreement. It had already begun removing the evidence that any other choice had existed.
Narrator: She pulled BAH-lahg's axe head from Zeek's pack and shoved it beneath her belt. The weight struck her hip.
Ninsun: [determined] I will come back,
Narrator: she said.
Zik: [weakly] [gently] Don't promise the City anything.
Ninsun: [tearfully] I wasn't speaking to the City.
Narrator: His right eye closed briefly. When it opened, more gray had entered it.
Zik: [weakly] [gently] Then don't look back.
Narrator: NEEN-soon stepped away from the desk.
Narrator: The distance of one pace felt like betrayal. The second felt worse. By the third, the passage had begun to contract behind her, shelves drawing inward without moving. She turned and ran before the opening could decide it had never existed.
Narrator: The corridor changed beneath each footfall. Numbers carved beside the shelves vanished as she passed. One arch narrowed until her shoulder struck stone that had not been there when she entered it. A row of jars appeared ahead, blocking the passage, and for a sickening moment she saw the father's label among them—SUBJECT 001, the dash still waiting—before every vessel darkened and the row became a wall.
Narrator: NEEN-soon swerved through an opening to her right.
Narrator: The air grew warmer.
Narrator: Behind her, the scratch of the quill continued. It followed through walls and turns, sometimes close enough to seem beside her ear, sometimes buried so deep beneath the stone that it might have been the City itself writing.
Narrator: She did not look back.
Narrator: A staircase rose beneath her without warning. She stumbled onto the first step, caught herself against the wall, and climbed as the lower steps flattened behind her. Halfway up, the staircase angled sideways. NEEN-soon braced both hands against the narrowing stone and forced herself through. BAH-lahg's axe head struck the wall with every movement. En-KHEH-doo's figurine cut into her palm inside her pocket.
Narrator: At the top, daylight filled a doorway no larger than a burial niche.
Narrator: She ran toward it.
Narrator: The opening began to close.
Narrator: NEEN-soon threw herself through as the stone narrowed against her shoulders. Heat struck her face. Sand scraped beneath her palms when she landed. She rolled, came to her knees, and dragged one boot free an instant before the threshold sealed behind it.
Narrator: Wind moved across the desert.
[pause 1.5s]
Narrator: For several breaths she could do nothing but kneel in the sand and pull air into lungs that had forgotten how open space felt. The sky stretched above her without pillars or ceiling. Sunlight hurt her eyes. Every exposed inch of skin seemed to tighten beneath the heat.
Narrator: Behind her, a narrow doorway remained cut into the rock.
Narrator: No darkness showed within it. Only a pale gray surface, deeper than shadow, shifting as though pages turned somewhere beyond sight.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: NEEN-soon rose.
Narrator: She took one step toward it.
Narrator: The scratch of the quill had stopped.
Narrator: Her body turned before she permitted it. She caught herself with one hand against the rock and stared at the sand near her feet.
Zik: [whispers] Don't look back.
Narrator: Zeek's last instruction had been neither noble nor ceremonial. He had spoken it with the same exhausted irritation he used when she interfered with a measurement or refused to admit a corridor was unstable. The familiarity of it made obedience harder.
Narrator: She waited for footsteps.
[pause 0.5s]
Narrator: None came.
Narrator: She waited for his voice, for a hand against the stone, for the blunt scrape of his boots crossing the threshold. The wind passed between the rocks and carried nothing with it.
Narrator: The doorway narrowed by the width of a finger.
Narrator: NEEN-soon pressed her forehead against the sun-warmed stone beside it.
Ninsun: [whispers] [sorrowful] Zeek.
Narrator: The gray surface inside the opening rippled once.
Narrator: Something struck the other side.
Narrator: Not a fist. Not a body. A single dull vibration traveled through the rock and into her hand, followed by the faint sound of a page being turned.
Narrator: Then the doorway folded inward.
Narrator: The edges met without grinding. The seam thinned, disappeared, and left an unbroken face of stone beneath her palm.
Narrator: NEEN-soon remained there until the rock grew too hot to touch. When she finally lowered her hand, En-KHEH-doo's copper figurine had marked her palm, and BAH-lahg's axe head pulled heavily at her belt. She did not search the stone for another entrance. She did not call Zeek's name again.
[pause 1.2s]
Narrator: She turned toward the desert with the City sealed behind her and began walking before it could decide to remember the door.
